## Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

* Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.
* Accredited Entities are required to submit a revised proposal with the details provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in the revised proposal.
* The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 50. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time.
* The recommended font is Arial, size 12.

## Upcoming Disclosure

Under the GCF Transparency Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website simultaneously with the submission to the Board, subject to the requirements of the GCF Transparency Policy. Please refer to the ISP. Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.4.

The infrastructure adaptation component (e.g., water supply systems) is considered as a mitigation measure because it will be implemented while the household adaptation intervention is mitigating half Guinea Bissau and all of Somalia funding needs – the other core indicator.

4. 5. The GCF contribution (i.e., GCF financial resources requested by the Accredited Entity) to implement the project or programme is calculated based on the total cost of the project or programme, excluding co-benefits when both adaptation and mitigation aspects are present in the given investment.

## A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

While the proportion of renewable energy (RE) generation is rising every year, it remains a small percentage of the total world-wide electricity production and far from the levels needed to meet the Paris Agreement targets. The IEA projects that RE will need to increase its share in global electricity generation from Variable Renewable Energy (VRE), biomass and waste-to-energy, geothermal, marine and small hydro rose 6% in 2010 to 13-29 percent in 2018. Large solar and wind energy deployment is needed to ensure that the world meets its climate change commitments. The IEA projects that RE will need to increase its share in global electricity generation from Variable Renewable Energy (VRE), biomass and waste-to-energy, geothermal, marine and small hydro rose 6% in 2010 to 13-29 percent in 2018. Large solar and wind energy deployment is needed to ensure that the world meets its climate change commitments.

The limited installed capacity of cost-competitive solar and wind in developing countries reveals important regulatory, structural, and technical barriers. The main constraints identified are (i) limited generation and transmission planning capacity; (ii) inadequate regulatory framework that constrains the

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE A

## Mobilization of sustainable private investments, limited procurement capacity to select the Independent Power Producers (IPPs), limited financial viability of tolls and VRE grid integration challenges due to weak grids especially in Sub-Saharan Africa.

### In addition, climate change will adversely impact populations, infrastructure and economic systems leading to increased adaptation needs. Adaptation solutions take many shapes and forms, depending on the unique context of a community, business organization, country or region. The scale and pace at which adaptation is needed are increasing, and the need for greater action and ambition will be needed to cost-effectively manage the risks. While many sectors of the economy are already adapting to climate change, there remains significant room for improvement in how climate change impacts are managed.

Wildlife in many countries (e.g., USA, Turkey, Greece) have caused large damages to the power infrastructure as well as cyclones (Mozambique, Caribbean Islands), droughts (Turkey and heat waves (USA, Europe). The World Bank has developed a framework for adaptation that can be applied depending on the context, among which: (i) access to reliable electricity to increase households' ability to access cooling systems in the event of extreme heat waves; (ii) increase resilience of the grid infrastructure to extreme weather events such as cyclones and droughts; (iii) increase energy security by diversifying wind speed, landslides and wildfire increase; and (iv) diversification of the energy mix to mitigate the impact of climate change in countries rich countries and of heat waves in countries with large shares of thermal plants with high cooling needs.

The World Bank through its Energy Sector Management Assistance Program (ESMAP) developed the Solar Risk Mitigation Initiative (SRMI) as a comprehensive framework for addressing the adaptation challenge and propose a comprehensive financial and technical support to countries. Launched in 2018 for the COP 24 under the leadership of the World Bank in partnership with the Agence Française de Développement (AFD), SRMI is a multi-donor initiative that includes the Global Solar Alliance (GSA) and SE4ALL, SRMI – previously known as the Solar Risk Mitigation Initiative – aims to support countries in developing a comprehensive framework for addressing solar energy risks which are conducive to private investments to reduce reliance on public sector financing while maximizing socio-economic benefits. The SRMI is currently being implemented by 10 countries (Botswana, Central African Republic (CAR), Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Mali, Kenya, Namibia and Uganda) with the support of 23 donors including the World Bank, AFD, GIZ, KfW, SIDA, USAID, IDA/IBRD financing and leveraging US$ 3.8 billion in private investments. SRMI focuses on Botswana, Central African Republic (CAR), Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Mali, Kenya, Namibia and Uganda.

Under the context of the COVID-19 crisis, SRMI's unique, integrated and replicable approach can be used to mobilize additional public investments in order to leverage private sector investments at scale and adapt their energy systems to climate change. SRMI supports countries develop evidence-based VRE targets, implement a sustainable renewable energy program, develop guides to adapt their energy mix and infrastructure to climate change, and maintain robust procurement processes for IPPs. The SRMI framework also includes the development of grid infrastructure resilient, finance solar/wind park infrastructure, and increase access to electricity to households and businesses in rural areas. SRMI also provides a risk mitigation framework that can cover residual risks perceived by private investors for renewable energy projects and mini-grids. This financial support for renewable energy development is needed more than ever in the current situation alongside government economic support measures (such as short-term job allowances, direct cash transfers


---
**Page 9**
---

To citizens and organized liquidity support to small and medium size enterprises). To assess the resilience to climate change and natural hazards of the projects that will be financed under the SRMI facility, the Resilience Scoring System methodology (RSS) will be followed. The RSS is in its pilot phase and aims to evaluate the vulnerability of infrastructure projects to climate change impacts. The final version of the RSS will be adopted in 2023/2024.

The SRMI Facility Phase (2), focused on resilience of Infrastructure (SRMI-Resilience) to be funded by the World Bank, provides technical and financial support to nine countries: namely Ethiopia, Guinea Bissau, Indonesia, Kyrgyzstan, Mongolia, Seychelles, Somalia, Tajikistan and Tunisia. SRMI-Resilience is expected to contribute to the shift to low-emission sustainable development pathways and increase resilience of vulnerable households and infrastructure as follows:

* **Directly support the development of an estimated 2.16 GW of RE (i.e., 1.76 GW of solar PV, (ii) 300 MW of wind power), and (iii) 150 MW of geothermal. This capacity is expected to be built during the lifetime of the projects.**
* **Directly reduce GHG emissions by an estimated 55.0 t in metrics tons of CO2 reflecting fossil fuel use and coal displacement by 1,560 MW of RE built under the projects over the 25 years of their operation.**
* **Unlock an estimated USD 1.6 billion of private investments for the 2.16 MW of RE built during the lifetime of the projects.**

* **Directly increase the climate change resilience of an estimated 3.2 million people through access to low-wave electricity, in heat wave and extreme temperature prone countries across to cooling infrastructure, water supply and sanitation, vulnerability adaptation capacities.**
* **Strengthen institutional and regulatory systems for energy sector development integrating climate risk. The use of climate data and robust methodologies to inform infrastructure investment decision making is critical to ensure that public infrastructure services will be made more resilient to climate change and be able to fully play their role. Institutions will be increasingly capable of attracting private sector, countries will develop a track record enabling them to enhance their credit rating (when available).**

1 World Bank Group, Feb. 2021, Resilience Rating System

a. **Ethiopia:** Ethiopia is one of the world’s most drought-prone countries. The country faces numerous development challenges that exacerbate its vulnerability to climate change, including high levels of food insecurity and ongoing conflicts over natural resources. Climate risks include increases in temperature (10 models), precipitation (9 models), and extreme events. Climate change will have key impacts on agriculture, livestock, ecosystem services, water availability, and energy systems. These changes will impact electricity generation through hydrological processes associated with hydropower, drought, and changes to water level, affecting the overall reliability of hydropower in the country. In the 2030 and 2040 decade, Ethiopia is expected to experience an increase in temperature (10 models) and precipitation (9 models) for most climate model uses. Nearly all models predict changes of 1–2 degrees for RCP8.5.

For RCP8.5, the 2030 decade overwhelmingly predicts changes of 1–2 degrees Celsius (17 of 21 models). The median change is +1 degree Celsius and the range is from −0.4 to +2.6 degrees Celsius over the decade. In Ethiopia, Cooling Degree Days (CDD) should increase by the 2030 decade between 10 percent (RCP8.5, median) and four times (RCP8.5, high-end), on average annually for most locations in Ethiopia.

The RCP8.5 scenario is characterized as a high-emission pathway with rapid increases of greenhouse gas concentrations leading to significant warming by the end of this century. It translates to an increased energy demand for cooling needs between 10 and 18 percent annually by the 2040 decade. Through SRMIL-Resilience, increased access to reliable power for cooling will be critical in order to meet these demands.

b. **Guinea Bissau:** In Guinea-Bissau, the impact of climate change is already being felt in the economy for some time now. The country has experienced a significant rise in temperature; in terms of water resources, this can be seen in aquifers that have decreasing quantities of water and are more easily flooded by salt water, ground waters increasingly deeper and dried up; lakes concerning the salinity and acidity of humophilous soils and the flooding of rice fields. Drought has also been prevalent in many regions of the country. Most of the country has seen 6–9 drought events in the past thirty years. The frequency of extreme weather events is increasing, with an increasing number of droughts over the past 90 years for which data is available. Wildfire risk is highest in the north and east of Guinea-Bissau. In terms of temperature, increases are expected to be at least 20 percent or higher. This is an issue for the grid that should be developed under the project (and in general) as transmission and distribution lines are particularly vulnerable to wildfires (and are in some cases already damaged). The median change in precipitation between the 2030 decade and temperature in the 2040 decade are approximately +1–2 degrees Celsius in most locations for both RCPs in both

### Indonesia: Climate change increasingly threatens Indonesia's coastal population and infrastructure, as well as the country's ecologically and economically important tropical forests and coastal ecosystems.

Indonesia is among the world’s most vulnerable countries to sea level rise. Indonesia is also very vulnerable to other weather-related disasters such as floods and drought that have caused widespread infrastructural damage in forest and coastal ecosystems, leading to loss of life, property, ecosystem services, and livelihoods. Climate change is expected to greatly impact the country's economy and infrastructure.

The present project is proposing to focus in Maluku and Nusa Tenggara regions, two areas that are highly vulnerable to climate change. It will directly impact the workforce whose income depends on natural resources such as timber, fish, and other forest products. The project will also indirectly affect roads, transmission lines, power plants and ports located in the coastal will also be impacted.

At the same time, climate change is expected to disrupt the operation of existing infrastructure in Indonesia: (i) heavy wind and rainfall; (ii) rising sea temperatures and heatwaves; and (iii) severe droughts and lightnings. Heavy wind and rainfall are by far the most significant threats to power generation capacity in Indonesia. In 2014, a tropical cyclone caused the loss of over 5% of percent of weather-related power outages during 2014-2015. Heavy rainfall also made coastal power plants and transmission substations more vulnerable to floods which sometimes lead to emergency power shutdowns. A major example is the 2013 flood that forced the gas-fired Muara Kating power plant to shut down for 12 days. At the time, more than half of Indonesia’s electricity supply was generated by coal-fired plants and was therefore cut off for safety reasons. The damages created by this flood amounted to more than USD 15 million.

Rising sea temperatures and heatwaves can also affect the operation of power plants. Higher seawater temperatures can cause corrosion in cooling systems, while heatwaves can damage the Paliton coal power plant in East Java to shut down for over 20 days. This caused an estimated loss of USD 2.17 million for PLN utility. Extreme dry seasons affect hydro power plant operations. Drought induced by climate change has already affected the operation of the Sidoarjo coal-fired power plant in East Java, in resulting in estimated financial losses of USD 5.15 million for PLN (the State Utility).

In Indonesia, SRII-Resilience's technical assistance will mostly support the development of grid upgrades and solutions to improve VRE integration as well as the reliability and the resilience of the grid. Grid investments will be designed considering the risks mentioned above, in particular the wind and flood hazards. In addition, droughts, polar vortices, heat waves, and extreme precipitation events while lines are often quite vulnerable to high wind speed. The potential increase in frequency and magnitude of these natural disasters will be considered in the design and the operations of the assets by development partners.

The southern part of the country sees the highest cyclone risk. Analysis of drought over the past thirty years shows an increasing occurrence when compared to the number of events in 1928-1958, although similar to the number of events shown in the previous thirty-year period of 1958-1988. Consideration should be given to the potential increase in frequency and magnitude of droughts as resilience considerations for infrastructure in high-risk regions. In the 2030 decade, RCP4.5 shows that most models predict a temperature increase less than one degree Celsius.

For the 2040 decade, RCP8.5 shows an increase from 1 to 2 degrees Celsius. The maps detail these changes that go through various parts of the country with higher changes seen in the southern part of the country and lower changes in the northern part of the country. These changes are more even, with the RCP5.0/2040 decade showing changes of 3 degrees Celsius or more in many locations.

In the 2040 decade, the increases range from 5 percent (RCP4.5, medium) to 10 percent (RCP8.5, high-end). The RCP4.5/2040 scenario shows an increase of up to 3 degrees Celsius in many locations and may see an average annual increase of 4 to 10 percent by the 2040 decade. Under the present proposal, the population to be connected to the grid is located in Maluku and Nusa Tenggara regions that are more vulnerable to these changes.

### d. Kyrgyzstan:
The Kyrgyz Republic is a landlocked, mountainous country in Central Asia susceptible to natural hazards such as earthquakes and landslides, as well as economic shocks resulting from volatile food prices and political instability. Impacts from natural hazards and economic shocks may be amplified by climate change impacts on the region's weather patterns, extreme weather events, and glacial melt. These impacts are likely to significantly affect key natural resources, economic activities, and infrastructure in Kyrgyzstan. The country is one of the most vulnerable countries with many areas having experienced 5 or more droughts in the past thirty years. The number of drought events has increased in the recent thirty years relative to earlier time periods. By extension, this will have a significant impact on the water resources and agricultural production in Kyrgyzstan. Flood risk is widespread throughout the country, threatening the grid infrastructure (and in particular poles and substations) that will be built under the project. Changes in extreme temperature events are expected to increase by 2 degrees Celsius or more for both RCP4.5 and RCP8.5 scenarios based on the latest climate models, most decades for both RCP4.5 and RCP8.5 scenarios show increases of more than 2 degrees Celsius. Increases in the Kyrgyz Republic range from 37 percent (RCP4.5, medium) to 50 percent (RCP8.5, high-end). In the 2040 decade, the increases range from 45 percent (RCP4.5, medium) to 60 percent (RCP8.5, high-end).

### e. Mongolia:
The impacts associated with climate-related disaster can be severe. Mongolia's geographic location, extreme weather and fragile ecosystems, coupled with prominent pastoral livestock and rain-fed agriculture sectors, make Mongolia's economy, livelihoods and traditional cultures highly vulnerable to climate change risks. Historical climate warming is believed to have increased the frequency of heat waves and droughts in recent decades.

take place on some of the fastest rates in the world in Mongolia and other shifts in climate dynamics are already strongly impacting on lives and livelihoods. Flooding is widespread throughout the country, concentrated along existing waterways and threatening the grid infrastructure (and in particular the power supply to Ulaanbaatar). The frequency and intensity of extreme heatwaves and high temperatures in Mongolia are predicted to be 1-3 degrees Celsius by most models for the 2030 and 2040 decades for both RCPs. For the 2040 decade, RCP8.5 shows the highest changes (exceeding 3 degrees Celsius). The increase is projected to be between 1.9-2.7 degrees Celsius in more than half of the country that exceeds 3 degrees Celsius for the 2030 and 2040 decades. For RCP 8.5, both the 2030 and 2040 decade show an increase from 6 to over 10 percent of the country exceeding 3 degrees Celsius. Increases in the 2030 decade in Mongolia range from 21 percent (RCP4.5, medium) to 34 percent (RCP8.5, high-end). In the 2040 decade, the increases range from 25 percent (RCP4.5, medium) to 46 percent (RCP8.5, high-end).

### Seychelles

Seychelles is highly dependent on tourism, and climate change poses long-term sustainability risks. Climate change adaptation, including through strengthened disaster preparedness and response systems, will be critical for the country’s development. The Seychelles has high risk for sea level rise and coastal flooding as well as cyclone events. The southern islands see wind speeds of Category 3 in the 0-200 annual probability event, indicating windspeeds between 165 km/h to 209 km/h. For the northern islands, the category is 4, with wind speeds at or above that, at system level; hence, redundancy will need to be created to ensure some service continuity during cyclones. Under SRI-MResilience, climate resilience of the infrastructure and services in Seychelles is expected to increase by 2035. The project’s ambition is to improve the system wide resilience and not just to ensure the resilience of the assets funded under the project. The more northern and eastern islands see low windspeeds, below 119 km per hour at system level; hence, redundancy will need to be created to ensure some service continuity during cyclones.

### Somalia

Somalia’s current climate is hot and dry, with uneven rainfall and regular droughts. The GDP contribution from the agricultural sector (34%) and agriculture represents 55% of the country's employment. Of this, 65% of the workforce (34% of households engaged in agriculture are engaged in rainfed farming, usually subsistence agriculture). Land degradation is a prominent environmental issue in the country, with over 70% of the land area affected by desertification and soil erosion. In recent years drought events lasting more than one year are seen in the Northeastern part of the country and near the borders with Ethiopia, Eritrea and Kenya. Flooding is concentrated in the southern parts of the country, especially around Mogadishu and in the regions between Kenya and the coast. Floods

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 13

## Tunisia is vulnerable to climate variability and change in numerous ways. Stressors such as rising temperatures and varied precipitation levels coupled with potential increased frequency of extreme events, such as floods and droughts, could threaten agriculture, economic development, and availability of water resources.

### TUNISIA: Vulnerability to Climate Change

Tunisia is vulnerable to climate variability and change in numerous ways. Stressors such as rising temperatures and varied precipitation levels coupled with potential increased frequency of extreme events, such as floods and droughts, could threaten agriculture, economic development, and availability of water resources.

### Sea-level Rise Threatens Water Resources

Sea-level rise threatens the water-scarce, low-lying islands off Tunisia's coast, where climate change will likely exacerbate existing water resource stress. The country is also vulnerable to increased frequency of extreme events such as floods and droughts due to rising temperatures. Water availability is critical for the power sector as most thermal generation plants are located in coastal areas. Climate change impacts on water resources need to be mitigated at the system level, by e.g., diversifying the power mix and moving towards generation sources resilient to these events. Many areas in the Central and Northern parts of the country are vulnerable to flooding due to increased precipitation intensity. The frequency of extreme rainfall events exceeding 0.5 meters or more. Wildfire risk is concentrated in the Northern parts of the country, although the annual area at risk is less than five percent in all locations. Droughts is widespread throughout the country, with nearly half of the country being vulnerable to drought.

### Energy Demand for Cooling

The energy demand for cooling will increase from 2030 onwards and will be an important source from historical data, with only 6 or fewer events occurring in most locations for the two previous decades. The majority of models predict increases in temperature of 2 to 3 degrees Celsius in 2030 for the country as a whole. Increases in the 2040s decade range from 1 percent (RCP4.5, median) to 8 percent (RCP6.0, high). For the 2040 decade, increases range from 10 percent (RCP4.5, median) to 30 percent (RCP6.0, high). This shows that an increase in energy demand for cooling needs may see an average annual increase of up to 8% by 2040 and 30% by 2060. The proposed project will enable the meeting cooling demand in a more sustainable way (i.e., reliable and affordable) and be designed to be resilient to climate induced natural disasters.

### SRM/Climate Action's Project Aim

SRM/Climate Action’s project aims to support countries in their energy transition and contribute to the overall achievement of the Paris Agreement. The project design will determine whether the Paris Agreement targets can be achieved. In that regard, macro-level economic stimulus packages need to be put in place to ensure that the necessary investments are made when the necessary incentives are given. If in 2017 those countries represented 5 percent of the total global GHG emissions, under a BAU scenario they would on average double their emissions between 2017 and 2030. With the proposed project, these countries could achieve an average reduction of 48 percent, 60 percent, 26 percent, 30 percent, 24 percent and 45 percent respectively. However, the priority of countries is to ensure that their energy transition is sustainable and resilient to climate change. Climate recovery is designed remains crucial in shaping the long-term pathways for emissions and determining whether the NDC targets and beyond can be achieved. If governments do not roll out low carbon development strategies and policies in response to the coming economic crisis, emissions could rebound and even overshoot previously project levels by 2030, despite having economic growth in the period to 2030.

### Sources: current countries existing generation plans

Analysing the baseline, the counterfactual scenario and the alternative scenario for the GHG-emissions can be based on different assumptions regarding what decisions will be made by governments in the coming years. The three scenarios are not mutually exclusive but rather lie between moving forward with new fossil fuel projects vs renewable energy. However, for the sake of consistency with country NDC reporting, and for keeping assumptions moderate, the base lines used for this analysis are based on the current generation plans.

It will be critical in this context to support policymakers to develop green stimulus interventions with targeted investments in clean technologies and infrastructure to ensure a smooth transition recovery. According to the IEA and estimations by the World Bank, around 40 GW and 100 GW of RE (including large hydropower) by 2025 and 2030, respectively, will need to be deployed in those nine countries as part of their plans for energy transition. This implies that a significant amount of investment is needed, estimated at USD 50 billion just for new solar and wind generation by 2030. To reach this objective, large amounts of private funding will have to be unlocked to complement the limited public sector financing available. Yet most developing countries are still not able to attract sufficient private investments in their energy sectors or other sectors. To develop one, countries must take a series of key steps to tackle risks perceived by the investors and unlock the potential for private sector investment for the planned projects.

The nine countries have been receiving support from other multilateral development banks (MDBs) and bilateral banks to deploy RE in their countries outside of SRM and of the scope of the present Funding Proposal with a coordination at World Bank sector strategy level to ensure complementary or parallel efforts. The following are examples:

The energy sector is one of the key sectors for the World Bank’s country dialogue. In this project, CASEA covers Tajikistan, Kyrgyz Republic, Pakistan and Afghanistan and includes the construction of a high voltage transmission line to facilitate seasonal trade between Central Asia and South Asia. The World Bank, along with other development partners such as Islamic Development Bank, the European Bank for Reconstruction and Development (EBRD), EIB and USAID, contribute to the project in the four CASA countries. ### e. Mongolia:
The current World Bank engagement in Mongolia’s power sector focuses on improving energy access and affordability for its growing urban population, (i) the development of new RE generation capacity as part of a gradual transition out of coal, and (ii) efficient heating in urban and peri-urban areas. The World Bank is financing the Second Energy Sector Project (ESPR), with the aim to improve energy access by expanding the national grid and improving the rehabilitation of local distribution networks, as well as support for solar generation in the remote western region of the country. The World Bank has also provided TA on power sector and renewable energy projects under the framework of the Mongolia’s Energy Sector Support Program (MESP). Ongoing TA is providing recommendations to the government on future investments in the power sector. The ADB has been financing a number of solar projects, which financed all the three wind farms currently in operation (155 MW) and also some solar projects. The ADB financed small size renewables (solar and wind) outside the central grid and is planning a 120 MW battery storage project. Possibilities for support for SRM in Mongolia are to support the deployment of 100 MP of PV and 100 MW of Wind via technical assistance support and VRE integration investments to reduce curtailment (in particular a pumped hydro project), and support the grid to become more resilient by investing in new transmission infrastructure that would be following climate hazards specific technical standards while allowing for VRE integration.

## a. Seychelles: The World Bank support on energy focuses on ensuring the Seychelles meets its NDC commitments. Specifically, the work will focus on developing a path to decarbonization and mitigating the effects of climate change on the Seychelles, which is particularly vulnerable to these effects. The primary objective of this project is to support the Government of Seychelles (GOS) in implementing the World Bank partnership secretariat and development partners working on climate resilience and transition to renewable energy, such as ADB, GIZ and AFD. Each of these hold regular meetings to ensure stakeholder alignment and coordination. The main activities for this project are to support the deployment of 18MWp with BESS IPP-owned solar PV mini-grids in Seychelles. These mini-grids will be designed to be resilient to climate shocks via investments in new grid infrastructure that would follow climate hazards specific technical standards while allowing for VRE integration. Critical technical assistance and capacity building will accompany this investment.

## b. Somalia: The World Bank supports the rebuilding and development of Somalia's energy sector (both physical and institutional infrastructure) through an eight years' programmematic Multi-Phase Approach (MPA), which constitutes the main lending operation in the energy sector since the eligibility of Somalia to IDA was confirmed in 2015. The Sector Support Program for Energy Recovery Project (SSERP) is conceived in phases and builds on the experience acquired by the World Bank in the past years under the Somali Electricity Access Project - supporting the scale-up of renewable energy mini-grids, including solar PV mini-grids, and the development of a national micro community mini-grid installations via the ADRA-implemented Somali Energy Transmission (SET) Project. Phase I of the SSERP will provide support for the identification of greenfield mini-grids. The identification process will be based on the results of the original World Bank Least Cost analysis identifying the optimal technology mix for electricity access provision and additional assessment of the feasibility of each identified project. In addition, in line with the objectives of Africa Regional Integration for Sustainable Energy Supply project, support will be provided to regional connectivity of Somalia to Ethiopia through the establishment of transmission interconnections between the two countries. Phase II of SSERP will provide support for the design, additional power supply. Major development partners active in the energy sector include ADB, UK FCDO, DFAT, GIZ, and AFD. The World Bank is also supporting the implementation of the United Nations Development Program (UNDP). Priority activities for SSERP in Somalia are to support the deployment of 30 MWp with BESS IPP-owned mini-grids via infrastructure financing and a new guarantee facility. These mini-grids will be designed to be resilient to climate shocks via investments in new grid infrastructure that would follow climate hazards specific technical standards while allowing for VRE integration. SSERP will also support the development of a national micro community mini-grid installations via the ADRA-implemented Somali Energy Transmission (SET) Project powering their cooling needs to face increased heat waves. Critical technical assistance and capacity building will accompany this investment.

Tajikistan: Several IFIs and bilateral development agencies are currently implementing projects aimed at rehabilitating existing RE capacity and construction of new capacity with the main focus on hydropower. In addition, an RE-based rural electrification program in Gorno-Badakhshan Autonomous Oblast (GBAO) region of Tajikistan is financed by several

### Tunisia
The World Bank Group has established a diversified and integrated program to help Tunisia meet its energy challenges, including the development of renewable energy sources. The program provides in-depth analysis and technical assistance through a programmatic technical assistance program financed by ESMAP to improve the performance and financial viability of the Tunisian energy sector. This includes (i) support for the implementation of the National Energy Efficiency measures and communications; (ii) diagnosis of the financial, technical and commercial performance of the utility (STET) together with a Performance Improvement Plan (PIP) to improve the technical and commercial performance of STET; (iii) support for the establishment of the Electricity Regulatory Authority. The World Bank together with the International Finance Corporation (IFC) is also working in partnership on the 500 kV interconnection project. The World Bank is also working in partnership with the International Finance Corporation (IFC) to help strengthen the renewable energy and private sector engagement program. Several projects have been supported by the World Bank, including a pumped storage project. KfW is also supporting the development of two PV with battery storage pilot projects. Germany has provided technical assistance for the project. The group of donors (mainly AFD, ADB, EBRD, EU, GIZ, IFC, KW, USAID, the World Bank and other bilateral cooperation (Switzerland, Japan and Germany)) involved in Tunisia with the objective to avoid overlap and duplication of efforts. This group has established a common framework (resource assessment, grid integration, studies of production and storage sites, support for the private sector); (i) Energy efficiency in the various sectors of industry, building transport and the territory; (ii) Renewable energy: solar PV, wind power, biomass, geothermal and hydroelectric. (iii) Smart Grid through its different components (forecasting the distribution of RE, Demand- response, smart metering, etc.). Priority activities for SRM in Tunisia are to support the deployment of 400 MW IPP-owned solar and wind via technical assistance and VRE integration investments, and support the grid to become resilient to climate change shocks via investments in new grid infrastructure and capacity building. The project will also develop a regulatory structure that would be follows Climate hazards specific technical standards while allowing for VRE integration. Critical technical assistance and capacity building will accompany this investment.

Outside of the scope of the present Funding Proposal, is to facilitate the development of the SRM Facility's core objectives; a collaborative multi-stakeholder group has been established. The multi- stakeholder group comprises ADB, KfW, and EBRD in addition to AFD, ILO, SEAMEO, and IRENA. The multi- stakeholder group will work together with the SRM Facility to ensure that it is aligned with the core principles during workshops sharing each stakeholder's vision to support the development of renewables in developing countries. A high-level collaboration with the Initial stakeholders is ongoing (including the World Bank, KfW, and EBRD) on the development of a work plan for the SRM Facility to Power and their Sustainable Energy Fund for Africa (SEFA) initiative on developing RE aligned with SRM's principles – in particular in the Sahel region, and AFD's work program on RE deployment – such as in Mozambique. SRIM-Resilience objectives are threefold: (i) reduce GHG emissions by tackling the lack of access to clean energy for targeted vulnerable people; (ii) increase resilience of targeted vulnerable people by providing access to modern energy to power cooling appliances; and (iii) increase resilience of grid infrastructure by supporting the development and implementation of technologies that enable climate-resilient change is the entire Programme and draws on the lessons learned from the successes and failures of national electricity policies and IPP selection processes in developing countries as well as how to ensure resilient infrastructure. The SRIM-Resilience Program is designed to support this objective by developing resilient infrastructure and adapting its population to climate change. It was critical knowing the current state of grid infrastructure, which is not only inadequate for the growing demand of population and infrastructure while supporting the mitigation agenda. The nine countries are facing important risks with regards to climate change but are also almost all (large) contributors to climate change. The core project objective is to support the eligible countries to shift to low-emission sustainable development pathways and increase climate resilience of vulnerable people and infrastructure.

The overall goal of the Program is to reduce GHG emissions and increase climate resilience of targeted vulnerable people and grid infrastructure by enabling the conditions to increase renewable energy generation, improve access to electricity for populations that are not electrified, and allow for the right technical standards to be used in new grid infrastructure to become resilient in the nine countries. The Program focuses on addressing some of the most critical barriers specifically faced by these countries: (i) lack of capacity to develop and implement policies that enable access to clean energy to populations that are not electrified, and to have resilient grid infrastructure. The Program proposes a three-pronged approach to address these barriers: (i) increasing renewable energy generation through electricity building are deployed through the operationalization of SRIM Resilience THEN RE projects, increased access and grid infrastructure that contribute to climate mitigation, resilience and adaptability to climatic threats; (ii) increasing private sector investment in low-emission residential development. Because both governments and private sector will be able to build capacity, private investments will be increased to support a sustainable and business case for the SRIM-Resilience Program. The third RE project is designed to ensure that data are generated and disseminated allow for governments to have resilient grid infrastructure and increase access to electricity, and population will be made more resilient to climate change.

**BESS:** That means that the RE projects to be dispatched, under "Output 1.3: Installed storage capacity for VRE integration is operating."

*Activity 1.1.1:* Generation plans with VRE integration and Resilient Mix, and Capacity Building (Technical) is a critical input to Output 1.1., as you need to have the right RE targets to ensure that the projects are technically feasible. The Green Climate Fund's Technical Assistance for Renewable Energy Advisory and Capacity Building (procurement, legal) is supporting the selection of the IPPs, and for contracts with IPPs, it is supporting the development of the Grid Infrastructure (e.g., the RE Park Infrastructure), it is supporting the development of RE Parks type of competitive selection scheme for IPPs. In addition: Activity 3.2.1:* Risk Mitigation Instruments for Grid-Connected RE Projects,* and *Activity 3.1.1.* Generation Plans with VRE Integration, Resilient Mix, and Capacity Building (Technical) are supporting the selection of IPPs by providing the right risk mitigation instruments attached to the tenders.

Two critical assumptions are linked to these: (i) Government decided to move from bilateral negotiations to competitive selection of IPP; and (ii) risk mitigation instruments in place enables leveraging of affordable private investments.

The main barriers identified that need to be addressed to leverage private investments for RE projects can be grouped into two broad categories: (i) risks occurring during the development phase, that is, prior to commercial operation; and (ii) risks occurring after commercial operation. These risks are exacerbated when integrated into the IPPs' lenders' cost of capital. These risks are exacerbated by the climate change risks.

* Development risks encompass: (i) grid risk, including connection risks; (ii) land risk, including availability, permitting, and environmental and social (E&S) aspects; (iii) resource risks (in particular for wind and geothermal); (iv) legal risk, including the applicable regulatory, dispute resolution, and judicial framework; (v) procurement risk, which usually leads to higher development costs as developers spend more time on the process than if a clear procurement process would have been in place from the outset; and (vi) integrity risk.

* Operational risks encompass: (i) off-taker credit risk (including the off-taker's record of performance and timely payment) and risk of contract termination; (ii) the IPP's country-specific risks (including including political, social, economic, and regulatory risks); (iii) grid risk in the generation phase of its construction work; (iv) market risk (including currency risk and interest rate risk); (v) climate risks on grid infrastructure and generation; (vi) operational risks related to the operation and maintenance of the generation outputs of the renewable energy systems by趁着不靠谱的曲裁，风险到的光片儿自己，和干扰上的扰动；(vii) country and macroeconomic risks, and (viii) political risk, including risks of terrorism, civil unrest, transfer restriction and currency inconvertibility/floation, war and civil disturbances.

## B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

### Programme Overall Background

Solar and wind energy generation could become game changers as they are already a least-cost source of power in most countries and are modular with shorter construction timelines than other power plants. Solar and wind energy generation can play a critical role from different perspectives: (i) climate change, (ii) energy security, (iii) energy access as it is a modular source of energy that can be deployed at the community level, (iv) economic development, and (v) social development with industrial integration and community development (including from women empowerment's perspective). Moreover, solar and wind can help countries deal with their potential power shortages.

However, large penetration of VRE is usually rapidly constrained due to grid technical limitations and lack of flexibility in the grids – which is exacerbated with climate risks to the grid infrastructure. These technologies are also intermittent, uncertain availability and that it can only produce power during the day for solar and when the wind is blowing. Three issues must be considered when deploying VRE into a grid: (i) the capacity for the generation of VRE to match demand at all times; (ii) the ability of the grid to operate within its optimum in terms of power system operating costs, after considering both cost reductions thanks to VRE and the investment costs required for deploying and integrating VRE; and (iii) the limitations of solar (PV/wind capacity to maintain grid and stability, due to its variability and its limited capacity to contribute to

---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND

## B

The balance between demand and generation. The flexibility needed in the grid to enable a sustainable penetration of VRE can be constrained due to (i) technical limitations, i.e., can the grid have the dispatch and transmission capacity to integrate such variability, and grid infrastructure resilient enough to climate risks, and (ii) economic constraints, i.e., can developers afford to build new generation capacity. As with gas plants, they may not be able to provide flexibility to the grid as ancillary services may not be priced in their power purchase agreement (PPA) or they would have "take-or pay" arrangements pushing the utility to buy from them at a price that is higher than what it could get on the wholesale market. In some cases, investment for new transmission lines will not be possible because the regulations for these services are missing in all these countries (only very few developed countries are able to provide such services).

## Investment for grid modernization, digitalization and electrification will be required to ensure large penetration of VRE in the nine countries. Taking advantage of variable renewable generation requires significant augmentation and modernization of electrical grids. Special technologies and processes may be used to reduce costs and increase efficiency. The following are some examples: (i) smart grid technology can reduce integration costs in the long term. The penetration of VRE requires power system planning and grid management that is more flexible, with better use of existing assets, improved forecasting methods and stringent grid code requirements, and increase digitalization. In addition, this will provide the opportunity for the country to also look at its grid from a climate change resilience perspective and adopt it.

If the technical aspects are critical, the deployment schemes selected to mobilize and select IPPs are also crucial. Since 2015, most countries that moved into competitive selection of IPPs directly benefited from the reduced cost in solar construction and equipment, and in cost of capital, through lower FIT prices. The following countries have announced their FIT prices for VRE under the Green Climate Fund Proposal: Vietnam, Kenya and Mongolia have FIT prices of USD 7.2 cents, USD 12 cents and USD 13 cents per kWh, respectively; Uganda has announced a FIT price of USD 8.5 cents per kWh; Ethiopia, Zambia and Philippines announced PPA prices of USD 2.46, USD 2.56, USD 3.9 and USD 4.5 cents per kWh, respectively. The main difference between FIT and competitive bidding is that under FIT, Government sets the price for all projects while in competitive bidding, the market determines the price.

Under competitive bidding, governments can access lower prices as IPPs will reflect in their proposed PPA price: (i) the capital expenditure (CAPEX) cost at the time of the bid – since 2010 CAPEX in wind and solar decreased rapidly, in particular solar module cost and it is difficult to assess its cost a few years ahead; (ii) bankable contractual arrangements with fair risk allocation between the private and public stakeholders; (iii) lower transaction costs for both government and developers as there are no FITs or feed-in tariffs; (iv) lower cost of capital, and (v) the scale of the market in the country and the region. Therefore, a bankable well-organized competitive selection can attract cost-competitive IPPs and ensure low cost of capital. In the nine countries, however, there are no established markets for VRE or ancillary services, and therefore markets for auxiliary services are not established and private investors cannot finance the main grid under the present conditions as mentioned above.

Based on these considerations, deployment schemes in and particular grid curtailment risks for ground-mounted VRE, two deployment schemes, namely subsidy-based competitive bidding and RE park competitive bidding, are recommended. Both schemes aim to reduce the development cost of integrating solar generation into the grid. The main outcome expected is a reduced PPA tariff proposed by developers. More precisely.

## B

Under the substation-based competitive bidding scheme, the government sets a target of solar PV capacity to be procured at a certain time. It identifies substations/feeders with available MW capacity, and a certain MW capacity at each substation/feeder is open for bidding to achieve the overall target. The IPPs are responsible for selecting the most suitable substations to connect their projects to the identified substations/feeders based on a set of pre-determined selection criteria comprising technical, financial and safeguard aspects. IPPs are responsible for selecting the most suitable substations to connect their projects to the identified substations/ feeders as required in the bidding documents. This scheme supports countries better manage the volume of new solar PV capacity that can be procured at a given time. The pre-arranged competition enables the utility to optimise the use of existing transmission capacity, reducing the potential curtailment and/or additional costs to integrate the solar PV and wind projects in it. It can also provide an opportunity for IPPs to develop their own grid connection solutions.

* **The RE park concept:** With pre-arranged comprehensive due-diligence measures (including land, power evacuation, resource assessments, and permits secured), can contribute to reach fully cost-effective solutions. The government provides the land, power evacuation infrastructure, and constructs shared infrastructure for the RE park that can range from the evacuation line up to basic elements, such as the fence, roads, street lighting. The government conducts the E&IS assessment and resource assessments (drilling and wind measurement masts). Once the project is ready for competitive bidding, the bidding procedure begins and the winning IPP is responsible for the financing of the remaining due-diligence measures. The RE park concept also aims at removing identifying risk of curtailment, resource risk and land ownership issues as paramount to the deployment of solar, wind and geothermal energy.

* **RE deployment in areas with an opportunity to maximize climate resilience of vulnerable populations and increase socio-economic benefits:** The lack of visibility on the local opportunities, the tight timeframe of the tenders, the existence of incumbent suppliers, and the costs to assess the project feasibility are some of the challenges that can prevent RE projects from being deployed competitively in a given country. Assessing the positioning of the local players on the solar/wind and BESS value chain and identifying the measures needed to improve their involvement in a competitive manner is one of the key elements for the successful deployment of RE projects, especially within the new RE regions. Factoring these measures in an RE roadmap and in the bidding processes launched can increase the likelihood that local players will be able to participate in the RE projects with knowledge and the skills development and allowing them to build a track record to the extent possible. In addition, expanding the RE roadmap to assess the needs of the local communities around the RE projects is another key element for the successful deployment of RE projects. This will increase the likelihoods will ensure that they effectively benefit from the implementation of the projects. Sites with good solar/wind resources and large plots of lands are usually located in areas where the level of living is below average, which makes them more vulnerable to climate change impacts and less likely for development. Including tailor-made development programs in an integrated manner in the RE roadmap of a given country can help to ensure that local communities around the RE projects will benefit positively from them. During the mini-grid operators competitive selection some criteria can be compulsory or optional, for higher ranking including pre-determined selection criteria can also include measures to support the development of local communities and/or improve existing distribution networks as and if required by local communities, with an option to include energy services suitable for increasing resilience.

These proposals aim to maximize the socio-economic benefits of the RE projects from the local industry as well as from the local community perspectives will pay a special attention to women empowerment. Employment opportunities created by large-scale solar and wind infrastructure projects often bypass rural communities, particularly women. In contrast, in the remote areas where large projects are developed, women, if employed, are more exposed to lower-paid, low-quality employment in the informal sector. As a result, without a proactive policy to promote women’s participation in these projects, there is a risk of creating new inequalities and not generating new employment opportunities across the RE value chain.

SRMI aims at addressing in a comprehensive manner the different challenges that are limiting this uptake, including (i) the lack of private sector interest in RE and the associated socio-economic benefits. Launched in 2018 for the COP 24 under the leadership of the World Bank in partnership with the AFD, IRENA and ISA, SRMI aims to support countries in developing sustainable solar and wind projects that are financially viable and socially inclusive. The project is structured around three pillars: (i) maximizing the customer; climate resilience and socio-economic benefits triggered by the projects deployed; (ii) ensuring the development of a robust enabling environment for RE deployment through technical assistance to help countries develop evidence-based RE targets, implement a sustainable renewable energy program, and set up and maintain transparent and competitive procurement processes that are conducive to private sector participation in RE projects, including large-scale solar/wind park infrastructures, and increase access to electricity; and (iii) risk mitigation instruments to cover residual risks perceived by private investors. Its framework is supporting the creation of jobs, the development of skills, and the empowerment of women, particularly in the livelihoods of the local communities around the projects with a specific focus on women empowerment.

Three of the SRMI-Resilience countries (Indonesia, Guinea-Bissau and Somalia) are seeking support to develop large-scale solar/wind projects that will be financially viable while also enhancing household’s resilience to climate change by enabling the population to access cooling systems as well as improved irrigation systems and access to information (such as early warnings, weather forecasts, etc.) to better manage their agricultural activities. In addition, these countries rely heavily on subsistence farming and traditional biomass for their survival. In Indonesia eastern provinces, people rely mostly on fishing for their living. Indeed, increased access to reliable electricity provides a means for households to diversify their income sources by engaging in small-scale businesses or agriculture. In addition, access to reliable power improves citizens’ and businesses’ ability to cope with economic shocks and climate-related disasters. Access to reliable power is also associated with higher overall populations more resilient. Reliability of electricity is positively associated with uptake across and within countries while countries with high uptake of electricity also tend to have a higher level of reliability and vice versa. However, the quality of service provided by the utilities in many developing countries poses a significant constraint on economic impact as well. The quality of service may be worse than what is perceived, and differences in the level of quality mirror income inequality.” In addition, reliable power can also improve access to health services, education, and other public services, thereby enhancing the quality of the health services provided, and citizens’ perception of the quality – encouraging more people to use the services. As such, health systems become more resilient. The vulnerabilities stemming

from environmentally disproportionately affect millions of rural poor in developing countries, limiting their capacity to escape the vicious cycle of poverty and cope with pandemics like COVID-19.¹

Climate change is expected to affect the entire energy sector, including fuel mining or production, fuel transportation to power plants, electricity generation, high voltage transmission through grid networks, distribution to consumers, and end-use consumption. The demand for coal will grow in some regions while increasing wildlife activity in some regions due to warmer temperatures and drier conditions could damage the distribution network. Climate change effects on the grid could cost utilities and customers billions of dollars annually.

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is a multilateral climate fund established under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). As part of the Facility, the Project design, feasibility studies and implementation will be informed by climate data using an approach to design resilient infrastructure by developing technical standards that are inclusive of climate change impacts. The project will also include a resilience assessment for each component. Specifically, the sizing choices, operations and maintenance protocols as well as materials used for construction will all be designed with climate change in mind.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 30

## Capacity Building for Countries Reliant on Hydropower

Countries relying heavily on hydropower such as Ethiopia or Tajikistan will need to adapt their energy mix to increase their power system resilience. Currently, both countries have plans to diversify their future mix with emergency diesels and coal plants which will adversely impact their mitigation efforts. The design of the projects under the SRMI funding mechanism is critical since the various ambitions of the countries are crucial. For example, failing to climate-proof infrastructure investments could result in significant electricity price fluctuations by over 35 percent in Ethiopia.

### Designing Climate-Resilient Projects

Designing climate-resilient projects is a key component of the SRMI funding mechanism. It ensures that infrastructure design will be climate resilient by providing technical standards that are encompassing the country-specific climate hazards. Very little countries have climate resilience design standards or guidelines, and even fewer have developed them in conjunction with their national energy sector design and new framework. In Indonesia, in addition to ensuring that the assets built under the project are climate resilient, guidelines will be developed for and with the utility to be applied to all future generation projects.

### Resilience of the Project

To assess the resilience to climate change and natural hazards of the projects that will be financed under the SRMI Facility, the Resilience Rating System methodology (RRS) will be followed. The RRS is a tool for assessing the potential impact of climate change on project outcomes. Resilience of the project design is the extent to which a project's assets have considered climate and disaster risks in their design. This design includes incorporating appropriate adaptation measures and anticipating future changes that may affect the long-term viability and value of the project. Resilience of the Project can help characterize the confidence that project outcomes will be achieved despite possible climate risks. It measures how climate and disaster risks are addressed during the project's planning phase, implementation phase, and operation phase through project outcomes reflects whether a project’s objective is to enhance the targeted sector’s and beneficiaries’ resilience.

### The SRMI Projects

The Projects go through a climate and disaster risk screening, stress testing is performed, risk management measures are identified and included in the project, and a strong rationale is developed to justify why residual risks may be acceptable. The resilience through the Project dimension characterizes how well the project addresses climate change and disaster risks at all levels: households, communities, asset networks, or even countries. The SRMI Projects have a cross-cutting nature where they address mitigation and adaptation in complementary ways, by nature of the renewable energy interventions that are being implemented. The SRMI projects will be designed to demonstrate how the project approach promotes in partner countries. By extension, the SRMI Projects will need to have a rating of at least B+ (50 points) on the RRS. This is because the SRMI funding mechanism is intended for climate and investments - that is, by helping people manage shocks brought on or exacerbated by climate and disaster risks. These project activities – as well as the project overall objectives - are intentionally designed to contribute to climate change mitigation and adaptation in a way that enhances power system resilience.

Most SRMI projects aim at supporting the grid to enable VRE integration. In doing so, these projects contribute to improve the reliability and the resilience of the power network in the country and to reduce the large costs incurred by power outages.

SRM aims to support countries to:

- develop sustainable RE programs to leverage private investment at scale for grid-connected and off-grid RE projects;
- implement the country’s RE programs through transparent and robust competitive tenders;
- improve the national capacity to integrate VRE;
- improve grid infrastructure's climate resilience;
- leverage private investments for HiP, grid-connected and off-grid projects;
- build a strong enabling environment;
- increase the resilience of vulnerable population by providing access to affordable and modern electricity;
- avoid GHG emissions; and
- maximize the socio-economic benefits triggered by the RE projects developed.

### Country Energy Sector Context

The SRM approach aims to support countries in implementing a comprehensive multi-dimensional financing approach, in a flexible manner adapting to their current situation and constraints. Therefore, it is critical to understand the different conditions and starting points of each of the countries currently identified to benefit from the Facility:

- **Ethiopia**: To meet its rapidly expanding electricity demand and to meet contingencies during dry seasons, Ethiopia has been focusing on large-scale hydropower projects in conjunction with other renewable sources such as solar, wind and geothermal—as diesel is now being used when the hydro generation is not able to produce as needed due to limited water availability. Within this context, the Ethiopian government has recently launched a tender for the development of competitively bid solar projects at Gadi and Diche. This is seen as a strong indicator of Ethiopia’s prospects for developing large-scale renewable energy with the involvement of the private sector. However, the lack of a well-developed financial market in Ethiopia and the limited forex availability in the country to be a significant hurdle in securing private finance for the large portfolio of renewable energy projects currently at various stages of preparation. In this context, the Ethiopian government has been considering the use of an EGP as a financing instrument that would act as a forex liquidity bridge for the sponsor if GoE cannot avail funds in a timely manner or if the sponsor is unable to convert its EGP-denominated revenues into foreign currency servicing payments. The eligible IPPs would draw on the forex liquidity support mechanism only if they are unable to convert their EGP-denominated revenues using normal market means and if GoE is not able to provide the required funds in a timely manner. The terms of the EGP will be determined. If the IPP draws on the liquidity support mechanism, GoE would then have the obligation to replenish it. The GCF reimbursable grant would be used as seed funding for the forex liquidity bridge and would also be used to provide technical assistance to the financing instrument and in promoting the development of 600 MW of solar projects under Round 2 of GoE’s solar program.

The strong pipeline of solar, wind and geothermal investments would necessitate suitable investment structures to generate power from these projects into the national grid network. (i) manage increasing volumes of VRE by strengthening the transmission grid; (ii) expand the national grid and develop distribution network to deliver electricity access to all; and (iii) develop transmission

**Guinea Bissau:** Only 29% of people in Guinea-Bissau's population has access to electricity, with large disparities between urban and rural areas. The country as a whole is around 60 percent of the population have access to electricity in Bissau, the capital. The current peak demand is 78 MW for the whole county, 35 MW for Bissau. This demand cannot be met with the sole existing power generation capacity (124 MW). A total of 24 MWh of rented heavy fuel oil (HFO) generators are currently in operation to meet this demand. The country's electricity infrastructure is non-existent, and the distribution grid is concentrated in Bissau. Decades of underinvestment have led to a lack of maintenance and modernization of the power sector.

Guinea-Bissau has not been able to operate generation assets and manage a fuel supply chain. The power barge is expected to bridge the gap until the Organisation pour la Mise en Valeur du Niger (OMVN) completes its 10 MW solar photovoltaic (PV) project in Bissau. This project will allow Guinea-Bissau to fundamentally shift its energy mix to a lower-cost and cleaner hydroelectric import, together with the development of solar energy. In its updated NDC, Guinea-Bissau expects the implementation of four key actions: (i) the deployment of an integrated grid expansion plan of around 90 MW by 2030, compared to 5 MW currently; (ii) a significant reduction in electricity grid losses from 48% to less than 10%; (iii) the installation of an energy-efficient lighting system in the residential and commercial sector and (iv) the implementation of energy efficiency actions in public and commercial buildings and in industry. The Least Cost Generation Expansion Plan (LCEP) for Guinea-Bissau is currently being developed by the World Bank. This plan will be based on low-cost electricity imports, domestic solar PV with batteries, and hydro, together with a few small-scale renewable power supply projects.

The country's current grid capacity is 124 MW, with solar energy limited to a 312 kWp project in the grid in Bissau. The country's experience with solar energy has been limited to a 312 kWp project on the grid in Bissau. ## Background

country ranks in the bottom 10 percent in most of the 2019 World Bank's Worldwide Governance Indicators (WGI), including on control of corruption, government effectiveness, rule of law and regulatory quality. These institutional weaknesses translate into a poor execution of government programs and policies, which has led to low levels of private sector mobilization and increase the share of renewable sources of energy, which is expected to decrease the high cost of service currently observed in Guinea-Bissau (at around US$ 40 per kWh). One of the main objectives of this project is to support the Government of Guinea-Bissau in the preparation and implementation support for utility-scale solar projects, off-taker guarantee support, and technical assistance. The project will be implemented through a series of mini-grids in the Bijagos islands (which includes i) site preparation activities, including sites identification and preparation for construction; ii) construction of power generation infrastructure and distribution network; iii) procurement, transportation, storage, installation and commissioning and installation of all equipment to connect households, small-to-medium-size enterprises, and community infrastructures). As already noted, GCF is a major source of funding for the implementation of this project. The project will contribute to: (i) increasing access to affordable electricity access and quality of service; (ii) expand electric supply at a lower cost, and (iii) reduce the high cost of service currently observed in Guinea-Bissau through the development of competitively procured renewable energy and electricity access programs.

## Indonesia

- Indonesia has over 600 island-grids to manage – with very diverse size and demand. Indonesia's energy sector's achievements are vast, from having reached almost universal access in very challenging circumstances to having greatly improved energy availability and reliability.
- Although Indonesia is a large country, it has a population of only about 250 million people. However, the Government has set itself an ambitious target: to reach 100 percent electricity by 2024. Currently, the official electrification rate is 98 percent.
- The major challenge for Indonesia in achieving this goal is the lack of access to electricity live in the Eastern Islands. According to the Government of Indonesia's access rate, the lowest electrification rates are at NTT with 86 percent, South Maluku with 91 percent and Central Sulawesi with 92 percent.
- The government has allocated US$ 2.4 billion is needed to reach universal access with a mix between grid connection, mini grids and decentralized renewable energy projects. This amount represents about 30% of the total CAPEX required mobilizing private capital. The main constraint to mobilize private investments, however, is the difficulty to implement a viable business model for private investors as the off-grid population is not able to pay for electricity at current tariffs or even with subsidized connections.
- In the Eastern Islands, for example, the lower electrification is paired with high generation costs reflecting the high share of diesel generators. In 2018, the average generation cost in the Eastern Islands was US$ 0.35 per kWh compared to US$ 0.14 per kWh in Java-Bali. It is thus critical to support PLN in reducing the high costs of generation via the development of renewable energy projects and improving the efficiency of existing thermal power plants.
- Indonesia has a large potential for hydropower, geothermal, solar and wind resources. Indonesia is also the fifth largest producer of coal and the twelfth producer of gas in the world. In 2018, 88 percent of the installed capacity was based on fossil fuels (coal, oil and natural gas). The country has significant renewable sources – including large hydropower generation. Current generation plan targets an increase to 25 percent of RE by 2025 but coal generation is still going to be the main source of generation in Indonesia.
- The development of renewable capacity has also been constrained by the fact that there is no competitive scheme in place. The GCF grant would be used to finance technical

B

Assistant needs for the Indonesia Sustainable Least-cost Electrification (ISLE) Framework jointly developed in 2019-2020 by the World Bank and PLN. The ISLE Framework aims to provide a set of solutions to PLN through a framework approach integrating a step-by-step methodology for: (i) developing a comprehensive grid expansion plan; (ii) increasing renewable generation and increase PLN's technical capacity to integrate more VRE into the grid, (iii) improve grid reliability by developing clear steps on how to assess electricity losses and lack of redundancy in the grid, and (iv) develop a framework for inter-island transmission. The ISLE Framework is implemented to enable replication of the framework to other islands, thus being a program of major importance for Indonesia's energy transition. This will be achieved through increased renewable generation and VRE integration. The TA would aim at supporting the planning efforts within PLN through increased trainings and state-of-the-art planning tools; finance analysis needed for feasibility studies; technical assistance in developing REPPs, and support for the development of technical standards for climate resilient infrastructure.

## Kyrgyzstan

Energy sector occupies a prominent position in the development dialogue on the one hand, because of the supply risk pricing constraints to economic development, and, on the other hand, because of the high level of debt. The energy sector's heavily managed sector represents a heavy fiscal burden - accumulated debt USD 1.5 billion or c. 20 percent of GDP. It is due to below-cost tariffs for residential customers - considered one of the lowest in Central Asia. This has led to an unsustainable situation where the government has been at the root of a structural deficit. The tightened fiscal situation resulting from the COVID-19 crisis makes reform even more urgent.

## Mongolia

Despite excellent solar and wind resources, limited absorption capacity of the transmission grid, and lack of interconnection with neighboring countries, there are significant challenges to scale-up of VRE sources like solar and wind. Outside the Central Energy System (CES), which supplies Ulaanbaatar and the central region, there are four regional power systems, each with its own generation mix. The CES is currently at full capacity, so no additional power exchange is possible. For this reason, VRE generation cannot presently be located in the eastern and western parts of the country despite presence of good resources. Technical impact assessments have been conducted to identify potential interconnection points where VRE can be integrated into the national grid. With 245 MW (155 MW wind and 90 MW solar) connected as of late 2020, the government has set a target for 3 GW by 2030. The current capacity expansion plan in NDC often must curtail the solar and wind plants to maintain grid stability. Future solar and wind projects will therefore have to be put on hold until the capacity of the network to absorb more VRE is increased. The government's renewable energy target for the period between 2019-2020 was mostly due to the favorable incentive mechanisms in place, including the generous FITs for solar and wind development - average solar PV FIT price was USD 16.2 per kW for 17 years on a 30-year contract. The government has also been able to attract private sector REPP projects secured project financing from international financial institutions on concessional terms, either directly or through their domestic partners with EBRD the biggest MDB financier in the sector. However, the Government of Mongolia realized that the FITs were at risk.
