same one much more generous than needed to attract investors and expensive for the consumers who pay for the RE via a green surcharge and it has been difficult for the Government to meet its payments obligations. Following a change in the Energy Law in 2019, Mongolia will gradually increase the share of renewable energy in new generation capacity to encourage more efficient investment by project types and locations.

- **Seychelles**: The energy bill of Seychelles represents around 25 percent of its total import bill as most of the energy needs of the Seychelles are met from imported fossil fuels. The Seychelles relies primarily on diesel generators for electricity generation, with only a small portion being met from fossil fuels and only 2.5 percent coming from renewable sources. The national energy policy (Seychelles Energy Policy 2010-2030) focuses on the need to reduce this dependency through increased use of RE in new generation capacity. The government aims for 40 percent of national electricity production by 2020 and 50 percent by 2030 (as per the NDC). With plans to further increase the use of RE beyond the set targets to 100 percent by 2050, ongoing projects in development include a 6 MW solar PV plant, a 4.8 MW wind farm and a 7.5 MW tidal power project. The recent launch of the country's first IPP which is expected to be the world’s largest salt-water floating solar plant and account for about 2 percent of the country’s total generation upon completion.

- **Somalia**: Traditional biomass accounts for 90 percent of energy sources in the country. This high reliance on biomass has caused both profound deforestation and environmental degradation across many areas, with an estimate of about 83 percent deforestation between 1965-2015. The system is defined as a dispersed generation system of small-scale power plants supplied by isolated generation providers. Private Electricity Service Providers (EPSs) are the main service provider in Somalia and account for approximately 74 percent of total electricity consumption at a low access rate of 36 percent). The total estimated installed capacity in the major load centers is about 150 MW (2017) most of which is derived from high-speed diesel fuel powered generators HSDGs. These are typically used to meet peak demand and provide back-up for unreliable renewable energy in their generation mix. However, these are of small-scale capacity due to inadequate transmission infrastructure and limited access to the national grid systems. Based on field data collected in 2017, the cost per kWh with Somalia excluding Somaliland is US$35-40/kWh with a weighted average of about US$61 per kWh. In Somaliland, the cost per kWh for electricity generation from diesel generators is estimated at US$80-90/kWh. There is significant untapped renewable energy resource potential as identified by numerous assessments including the recent “Somalia Power Sector Master Plan and renewable energy opportunities in Somalia” which highlights that there are several opportunities for electricity generation particularly solar and wind energy. Mini-grids could provide a basis for an integrated approach to rural electrification, providing access to power supply, lighting and cross-network power sales. There is increasing demand for electricity and the required generation capacity for the country is forecasted to increase to 1,000-4,600 MW by 2037 in business as usual scenario. The government has set a target of 50 percent RE penetration in Somalia to reduce their average electricity tariffs by about 40 percent making electricity more affordable.

- **Tajikistan**: Tajikistan’s electricity supply mix is dominated by hydropower. The total installed generation capacity of Tajikistan is 6,058 MW and hydropower plants account for 98 percent of the mix. The largest power plant in Tajikistan is the Rogun Hydropower Plant (RHP), a large generating plant. It generates 50 percent of the total annual energy requirements and is also the balancing plant in the system. The thermal power plants are primarily operated in winter to supply

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 36

B

electricity and heat given: (a) high winter electricity demand, which accounts for 60 percent of annual demand; and (b) limited generation by hydropower plants due to reduced winter flows.
Electricity demand increased by 10 percent in 2018-2019 and winter electricity shortages were announced. The country is also facing a challenge with the increasing share of imported fossil-fuel based heating. Electricity exports have been increasing, and regional connectivity is improving. Going forward, the Government has set out ambitious targets for energy security, including to meet the increase in electricity demand and also diversify the sources of electricity supply and improve the mix of technology employed.
As Tajikistan continues to rely on hydropower as its primary source of electricity supply, a strategy is being developed to diversify this. Results from the government experience and literature study indicate that solar PV is part of the least-cost/supply cost scenario for Tajikistan.

Tajikistan has also been working with the World Bank to develop a National Energy Strategy (NES) which includes a sectoral thermal generation that would rely on imported gas and coal-fired generation that would utilize local coal. The NES also includes a strategy to diversify energy sources, including solar PV, based on four strategic development goals, among which is having energy security and an efficient use of energy.
Considering the increasing frequency of negative hydrological events, the Government is supplementing its renewable energy portfolio with solar photovoltaic (PV) systems and wind power plants. The NDS-2030 particularly sets forth the sector power subsector indicators of 10 to 15 percent share of non-hydroelectric generation in total electricity production by 2030, reduction of technical and commercial losses in distribution networks to 10 percent, power generation diversification for 10 percent as well as generation and energy savings of more than 500 million kWh per year.
These targets have an important role in addressing some of the main challenges the power sector currently faces. These include: (i) low access to electricity, with a national electrification rate of 98%, but Tajikistan, including the Sugd region, where demand has been steadily growing, reflecting the expansion of economic activities and urbanization; (ii) high technical losses in distribution networks, with an access ratio in the Gorno-Badakhshan Autonomous Oblast and Kalton regions, where more than 40 thousand people do not have access to electricity. (iii) Variability of electricity generation given significant reliance on hydropower, which is subject to seasonal variations in water availability, in particular with South Asia, and its financial distress of Borq Tojik, which until very recently was a relatively stable power producer, but has now been declared bankrupt. Solar PV technology, which is estimated to be cost-competitive and can help achieve the objectives of supply mix diversification highlighted above. In particular, solar power may address power supply and reliability challenges in Tajikistan.
The proposed project will support the Government's vision for the sector by financing a 100 MW solar power plant to be co-financed by the GCF. The project would pave the way for a transition to diversify the electricity generation mix of the country, which is currently dominated by hydropower. It aligns with Bank's ongoing engagement to support the Government in establishing an enabling environment for renewable energy development and improving the sector's performance and sustainability indicators for the sector, thus reducing dependence on hydro generation and mitigating hydrology risks, including climate change impacts. It is also worth pointing out that the proposed Project will also have a significant impact on the country's fiscal balance by generating additional revenue which it will support the Government's vision for the energy sector with the recent unbundling of BT, introduce new sources of revenue to improve the sector performance, diversify the energy-mix, and promote private investments in the energy sector.

- **Tunisia**: Over the last decade, the Tunisian energy landscape has been marked by a decline in primary energy resources. Continued growth in energy demand resulting in a structural energy deficit, which is expected to worsen over time. The country's energy sector faces three major challenges: namely (i) it is dependent on imports feeding the trade deficit; (ii) subsidizes that diesel demand and increase the budget deficit; and (iii) an underperforming utility. This poses a significant risk to security of supply in the medium and long term.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 37

This planning situation, Tunisia’s strategy for the energy sector aims to guarantee the security of energy supply, to preserve the competitiveness of the economy by optimizing the cost of providing energy and good governance of the sector, to guarantee access to energy for all citizens, and to contribute to the fight against climate change. These objectives are achieved through: (i) the deployment of low-cost renewable energy; (ii) upgrading of infrastructure and technological development, in particular by encouraging the emergence of emerging and digital technologies that are indispensable for a green transition; (iii) improving demand-side management and energy efficiency measures and better targeted subsidies; and (iv) a new mode of governance that is more transparent and participatory. This strategy is based on public-private partnerships with both private sector companies and public-private partnerships. In this context, the Government of Tunisia launched in 2015 the Tunisia Energy Transition Plan (TETP), which aims to reduce energy intensity by 38% between 2015 and 2030 (from 3 percent today) and reduce energy demand by 30 percent by 2030. Tunisia targets deployment of about 4 GW of clean energy capacity by 2030 by harnessing their solar and wind resources, which are expected to be commissioned between 2018-2025 (i), a further 6 GW of new renewable capacity expected to be commissioned by 2030; (ii) an additional 1.25 GW commissioned between 2021 and 2030, and (iii) a further 1.25 GW commissioned between 2026 and 2030.

### Programme's Objective & Components

SRMI-Resilience’s objective is to support the eligible countries to shift to low-emission sustainable development pathways and increase resilience of vulnerable people and infrastructure. SRMI-Resilience is developed around four components that are complementary. Depending on the specific context, some sub-components may be required in addition to the primary climate impacts, not all (sub-)components may required for each project. These components are based on the identified risk mitigation that will enable to reduce the cost of RE deployment, contributing to low-emission development pathways and increasing resilience. The scope and scale of these components can be adjusted to reflect what is specifically done under each project and which objective and impacts the activities in one project are particularly designed to focus on.

Component 1: Technical Assistance

Grant amounts will be allocated by the EE (Host Country) to the financing of the following types of activities to be carried out by their Ministry or state utility responsible for energy, on an as-when-needed basis:

Sub-Component 1.1: Generation plans with VRE integration and Resilient Mix, and Capacity Building (Technical)

- Preparation of a new national (or adaptation of an existing) least-cost power generation plan which includes:
  - (i) relevant VRE Integration analysis based on technical and commercial constraints,
  - (ii) an evaluation of the dispatch capabilities and the limits of grid integration, and
  - (iii) analysis of the potential for energy storage to increase the resilience of the energy mix and determines a cost-optimized electricity mix that can meet demand in all scenarios.
  
- Preparation of a new national (or adaptation of an existing) climate change resilient least-cost transmission/distribution plan, which includes capacity expansion models simulating generation and transmission planning under different future scenarios. The plan provides information on fuel prices, technology cost and performance, policy and regulations, and provides informed solar targets and location of where best to locate future projects as well as a list of key investments

## B

### Sub-Component 1.2: Transaction Advisory and Capacity Building (Procurement, Legal)

#### Provision of technical assistance including legal, procurement and transaction advisory services) and implementation of capacity building activities to support:
(i) the assessment of the existing legal framework and its alignment with international best practices;
(ii) the design of a financing scheme that is conducive to private sector investment; 
(iii) the design of energy projects (including key terms of the PPA and government support agreements) with fair risk allocation among stakeholders, including the identification of appropriate financial structures for the comprehensive selection of IPPs and mini-grid developers (for Somalia specifically); and
(iv) the negotiations of the final contracts.

#### Sub-component 3: The transaction advisory services will be provided by a consultant. This component includes Components 1, 2, and 3.
Sub-component 3.2 will be expected to be financed and carried out under this sub-component. The objective is to provide technical assistance in order to help the EE (Host Country) to better understand how to leverage an effective and efficient competitive bidding process (including in terms of evaluation formulae to select the IPPs) to maximize the socio-economic benefits, in particular for women and youth, and to ensure that the projects are aligned with international best practices. It will also support the operationalization of the actions plans needed to maximize the socio-economic benefits, including the identification of appropriate financial structures for the comprehensive selection of IPPs and mini-grid developers (for Somalia specifically), the participation of local players on the RE value chain in a competitive manner, fostering job creation and skills development, to enhance the livelihood of the local communities in the vicinity of the projects, ensuring gender equality and other needs as needed.

#### Sub-Component 1.3: RE Park Infrastructure and Resilience/Climate Adaptation Studies
Preparation of feasibility studies and vulnerability studies and environmental and social risk mitigation instruments for the development of solar PV, solar PV with battery storage, geothermal, hydropower, onshore wind and offshore wind parks in support of investment de-risking and investor confidence. The scope of work may include, as needed, geological analyses of the land around the substation, topography and geotechnical studies, environmental impact assessment (EIA), socio-economic impact assessment studies, solar irradiation/wind measurement/geothermal drilling analyses, adaptation/resilience study to ensure viability of the power plants taking into account climate risks on solar, wind and hydropower resources in particular, and other studies as needed.

### Component 2: Public Investments
Loan amounts (or grant for grant only countries) will be allocated by the EE (Host Country) to the financing of the following types of activities to be carried out by their Ministry or state utility responsible for energy on an as country-needed basis.


---
**Page 43**
---

Sub-Component 2.1: Public Investments in RE Parks Infrastructure
- Carrying out infrastructure investments to support the development of RE parks (solar, wind and geothermal), including construction of approach roads, transmission lines, fencing of such infrastructure, and drilling of wells to establish resource availability.

Sub-Component 2.2: Public Investments for VRE Integration, Grid Upgrades and Resilience
- Investment for grid modernization, digitalization, and resilience to improve grid flexibility to integrate variable renewable energy (VRE) sources. This includes investments in grid-related stocks, including power system planning, grid management, strengthening of forecasting methods and grid code requirements. Grid reinforcements, which will be built using technical specifications that are compatible with VRE integration, will also be implemented. These may include: (i) addition or replacement of lines and transformers for grid extension and capacity enhancements (both for answering growing demand and for integrating VRE power); that would be as necessary to ensure the reliability of the grid; (ii) investments in storage technologies such as capacitor banks, battery storage and other reactive power compensators, together with flexible assets such as gas turbines, combined cycle plants, and pumped hydroelectric power generation operation, such as monitoring systems, demand and production forecasting systems, and automats for controlling generation units and grid operations through automatic generation control (AGC) systems. The use of energy storage in the commercial setting may not have been established yet such as load shifting battery storage.

Sub-Component 2.3: Public Investments for Electrification for Community Adaptation to Climate Change
- Investments are needed to support households to adapt to an increased number of heat waves via increased electrification, in partnership with the private sector when feasible, including through solar home systems, mini-grids and grid connection, as best appropriate to the country context, with a public subsidy.

Component 3: Risk Mitigation Instruments
The proposed financial instruments for Component 3 are guarantees and reimbursable grants. Reimbursement of such grants will be conditional and modular based on verified performance of private-sector investments in RE projects that follow the mitigation RE risk investment risks and enhance financial sustainability in the following areas:

Sub-Component 3.1: Risk Mitigation Instruments for RE Mini-grids and Off-Grid Private Investments
- Provision of reimbursable grants to mitigate the critical risks related to private capital investment in the off-grid space (mini grids and/or SHS – for which Multi-Tier Frameworks (MTF) data will be used when available), such as for instance demand off-take risk affecting the investors for the first few years of operation and consequently financial risks affecting pricing and loan or financing available to the investor.

Sub-Component 3.2: Risk Mitigation Instruments for Grid-Connected RE Projects

        * **a. Impact potential: Adaptation**
            * The project will support the Host Country in the development of guidelines for climate-resilient infrastructure design, adoption where necessary, to this Host Country specific climate hazards.
            * If the proposed activities under Sub-Component 2.2 and/or climate risks are a threat for the power infrastructure in the Host Country, the project will aim to support the construction of grid infrastructure that is resilient against these threats.
            * If the project includes activities under Sub-Component 2.3, (i) the project will offer electricity services at Tier 2 or above as per the Multi-Tier Framework of the World Bank, and (ii) the project will aim to support the development of a grid infrastructure that is resilient against climate risks connected countries to adopt improved and/or new climate-resilient livelihood options by providing cooling appliances.
            * If the project is in a Host Country that is expected to experience reduced hydrology because of climate change, it will aim to promote a more diversified energy mix by increasing solar and wind penetration instead of increased fossil fuel-based production.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 41

## Private Capital Mobilization

- The project will support the Host Country in its effort to leverage private investments in a competitive and sustainable manner, which is heavily dependent on the current security and macro-fiscal constraints that the Host Country may have.
- If the project includes activities under Sub-Component 3.1, the RE pie infrastructure (which may include new transmission lines, substations upgrades, battery storage, etc.) as well as the grid, fencing, security system and all relevant infrastructure needed to complete the park, as the case may be) will be made available to support private capital mobilization.
- If the project includes activities under Sub-Component 3.2, it shall include a capacity building plan in particular demand green mini-grids, that will allow for increased resilience to climate change of the newly connected customers.
- If the project includes activities under Sub-Component 3.2, it will cover risks related to long-term mobilization of private capital (1) in utility scale renewable energy investments and (2) that is selected in a competitive manner.

### Impact Potential Ranges

- The estimated installed capacity (MW) as a result of infrastructure financed under the Facility shall not deviate by more than 15 percent less than the initially planned installed capacity for the whole Facility as indicated in the logframe.
- The estimated impact on GHG emissions as a result of infrastructure financed under the Facility shall not deviate by more than 15 percent less than the initially planned mitigation impact for the whole Facility as indicated in the logframe.

#### b. Paradigm shift potential

- The project will focus on increasing RE deployment to set the path toward sustainable energy transition while promoting resilient infrastructure and increasing resilience of customers.
- The project will include a capacity building plan to be financed under the project to support the long-term mobilization of private capital.
- The project will address systemic barriers to investment, including policy and regulatory barriers, to enable future investment in low carbon energy generation, energy access, or energy infrastructure.

### c. Sustainable development potential

- The project will, to the extent possible, support the development of an industrial integration plan to support local industries aligned with the SDGs: Industry, Innovation and Infrastructure objectives, as well as the long-term jobs creation aligned with the SDG8: Decent Work and Economic Growth! objectives.
- The project will, to the extent possible, support the development of a development action plan in partnership with the private sector to leverage the investment of the private companies.
- The project will aim to support the Host Country meet their SDG7: Affordable and Clean Energy; SDG13: Climate Action.


---
**Page 46**
---

d. Country ownership:
- The relevant Host Country will demonstrate a strong willingness to develop a comprehensive and streamlined strategy to deploy and integrate VRE and leverage in a sustainable manner private investment by: (i) providing no objection letter, demonstrating their full commitment to the SRMI ambition in RE deployment; and (ii) requesting the World Bank to provide financing for a new transformational project that will be aligned with the Host Country’s national development priorities and to the new challenges with regards to the energy transition.
- The VRE targets and revised policy support by the project will be aligned with the NDC low-emission development plans as referenced in the no objection letter issued by the Host Country.

e. Recipients' needs:
- The project is located in a Host Country that is expected to be vulnerable to climate change and in need to fundamentally adapt its energy systems by harnessing its domestic renewable resources.
- The project will identify climate change induced hazards and elaborate on how the project contributes to increased climate-resilient sustainable development.

1. Efficiency and effectiveness:
- The project will reduce GHG emissions in the Host Country compared to BAU in the energy sector and support the achievement of the Host Country’s NDCs.
- The project's expected ratio of total USD per carbon dioxide invested is maximum 101, and per GCF Proceeds: maximum 25.
- Adaptation impacts' efficiency will be reflected in expected economic internal rate of return and/or financial internal rate of return, depending on the needs of the project, that are higher than 10 percent.
- The project will build on best practices in the power sector with regards to infrastructure climate resilience.
- GCF Proceeds allocated per Host Country shall not deviate more than 15 percent from the indicative allocated amount per Host Country as indicated in Table 4.
- The project will be co-financed by a maximum ratio of 1:2 (GCF Proceeds: Co-financing) in each Host Country - with the exception of Indonesia that will not have co-financing.
- The project will have been prepared in compliance with the Accredited Entity's applicable policies and procedures and the relevant provisions of the AMA and the FAA.
- The project will be approved by the World Bank within 5 years from FAA’s effectiveness.

g. Gender:
The project will contribute to closing gender gaps in the energy subsector by (i) identifying gaps relevant to the four pillars of the World Bank Group (WBG) Gender Strategy™, and (ii) aiming to address these gaps through specific actions supported by the project.
- The nine-point concept as part in this Facility are aligned with the eligibility criteria proposed, as illustrated in the feasibility study annexed to this Funding Proposal. The World Bank will work with each Executing

# Guinea Bissau:

The project aims to support the deployment of 86 MWp in utility-scale solar projects, combined with a 20 MW wind farm and 30 MW biomass power plant, all of which will be deployed on the islands that are deployed in the Bijagó Islands. The project is estimated to leverage USD 15 million in private investments and provide access to 50,000 people. Total funding needed is or USD 12.5 million from the GCF and at least USD 37.5 million from other sources.

# Sub-Component 2 Public Investments for VRE Integration Grid Upgrades and Grid Resilience (USD 21m). The funding under this sub-component includes grid modernization and upgrade to ensure large penetration of VRE, such as equipment for smoothing the voltage map and a SCADA system. It is worth noting that the project will also include the development of a new transmission line which has a significant funding need for the deployment of VRE and new grid infrastructure. The infrastructure configuration will follow technical standards that are taking into account climate hazards in Guinea Bissau, ensuring that the new investment will be made resilient to climate change.

# Sub-Component 2.3 Public Investments for Electrification for Household Adaptation to Climate Change (USD 10.5m). The funding under this sub-component considers the extension of the distribution


---
**Page 49**
---

network in Bissau and the electrification of the Bijagó islands through the deployment of solar-powered mini-grids. These are directly supporting the population to become more resilient to climate change by allowing them to have access to modern and reliable electricity that can power cooling equipment, refrigerators, and other appliances. The project will also be designed to provide access to electricity and improve the reliability and sustainability of service in around 50 priority facilities. Additionally, on about 62 solar household kits will be distributed to staff residences without electricity. A classic model for the operation and maintenance (O&M) of the solar systems, and for providing training to local staff for basic maintenance and repair is being developed as part of this sub-component. This model includes a service and operations management (SOM) of the solar systems, and for providing training to local staff for basic maintenance and repair is being developed as part of this sub-component.

In addition, a program for the retrofit of solar pumps for irrigation of small farms will also be developed under this subcomponent. Stakeholder consultation will be initiated during the project implementation phase in order to ensure that the design and implementation process is inclusive and transparent. In addition, it will support the detailed design, supply, and installation of distribution network infrastructure including medium-voltage (MV) and low-voltage (LV) lines, substations, and so on to expand and connect at least 10,000 households to the grid. This includes the procurement, supply, and installation of last mile connection equipment, including smart meters for large consumers, prepaid meters, and load management systems.

- **Sub-Component 2.3 Risk mitigation instrument for RE connected (USD 2m)**: The funding under this sub-component will be used for guarantees payments to cover the off-taker liquidity risk. This guarantee will be proposed for a 30 MW project (the first IPP project in the country). It aims to cover the liquidity risk of the power purchase agreement (PPA) and the performance risk insurance (PRI) that is outside this WB financed project. The size of the project to be guaranteed and the exact terms of the guarantee are still being discussed and are expected to be refined at a later stage.

Additionally, the following sub-components will also be included in the project:

- **Sub-component 2.4 Generation plans with VRE Integration and Capacity Building (Technical) (USD 0.5m)**: It is important to conduct a VRE integration study prior to the addition of the 86 MWp solar projects into the grid, including technical standards for grid infrastructure resilience to climate hazards and extreme weather events. The current transmission plan in place does not consider the capacity on their capacity. It is worth adding that although a recent least-cost generation and transmission plan was approved by the Government in February 2020, it is already outdated, as it does not consider the solar projects being proposed under this project. This plan also assumes that the hydroelectric power will be the second lowest source of electricity in the country after PV), and would also need to be revised in light of the development of the solar projects. The current transmission plan also assumes that 100 percent of the power supplied after 2025 would come from imports when the interconnection would be expected earliest 2026 and will not be able to provide 50% generation needs. A new generation plan will be developed as part of this VRE integration analysis is being conducted, but such outside the present project co-financed with GCF.

- **Sub-component 2.1 Transaction Advisory and Capacity Building (Procurement, Legal USD 3.5m)**: Transition advisory is needed to support the government in structuring and implementing the proposed 86 MWp solar projects. The transaction advisory will be used for setting the right legal and procurement framework with the Government to allow for the first IPP project in the country to materialize. In addition, the advisors will work on framing the risk mitigation instruments that are needed to cover the off-taker liquidity risk of the power purchase agreement (PPA) and performance risk guarantee under Sub-component 3.2, a potential PRI, or a MIGA PRI, or other MDB guarantees (such as AFD or AIIB).

## Sub-Component 2.1 Public investments in solar and wind park infrastructure (USD 5m)

The funding under this sub-component includes the construction of infrastructure to connect solar parks to the grid, as well as the connection of wind farms to the grid.

## Indonesia

Indonesia's GCF technical assistance is being developed in parallel to a Program for Results (PforR) – which is not considered co-financing due to the fact that this World Bank instrument cannot be co-financed by GCF. The expected results of the PforR are however being translated in the technical assistance, and will be monitored through the same indicators.

The project will have two different kinds of impact in Indonesia: expanded and reliable access to electricity for 12 million people (60% of the population) and increased energy security and resilience of the power system and improve energy security through the reduction of the reliance on fossil fuel (mostly coal and diesel).

### Sub-Component 1.1: Generation plans, tools and trainings (USD 3m)

The GCF-funded technical assistance (USD 10m) that is being proposed under the present proposal aims to finance the following:

#### Sub-Component 1.1.1: Generation plans, tools and trainings (USD 3m)
The funding under that Sub-Component will allow for PLN to be trained on state-of-the-art planning tool that will allow them to better plan their generation capacity in line with Indonesia's energy demand and renewable production etc. They will be trained within PLN HQ but also at the regional level too, and have license to use the tool after training. The funding is also being used for the development of tools based on their yearly reviews of their 10 years generation and transmission plans across the 600 grids (called the RUPTL).

#### Sub-Component 1.1.2: Transaction advisory USD (test)
The funding will be used for experts (legal, procurement etc) to assist PLN in developing a bankable RE program to attract private investors.

#### Sub-Component 1.1.3: RE park infrastructure and resilience/adaptation (USD 6m)

The funding will be used to develop guidelines for technical standards for grid infrastructure to include the specific location's climate hazards. It will then fund trainings for PLN staff to be capable of applying such new standard. The funding is also being used to support feasibility studies and EAs instruments for solar, wind and geothermal projects.

## Sub-component 2.2 Public Investments for VRE Integration Grid Upgrades and Grid Resilience (USD 50m)
The funding under this sub-component includes grid modernization and upgrade to ensure large penetration of VRE, such as equipment for smoothing the voltage map and a SCADA system. The project will also include the development of an enabling environment for the adoption of appropriate policies and institutional arrangements in line with the NDC. This project directly yield a range of significant adaptation and resilience benefits, and vice versa. Energy sourced from renewables means enhanced energy security for Seychelles islands as well as a less reliance on imported fossil fuels. Renewable energy resources are also more resilient to climate changes and communities, encourages the production of local farming productions; it also considerably improves the quality of life in rural areas. The project will be implemented under climate considerations that follow technical standards that are taking in account climate hazards in Seychelles, ensuring that the new investment will be made resilient to climate change.

### Somalia:

This will provide access to 750,000 people. The proposed project (USD 168.5m) that is being proposed under the present proposal aims to finance the following:

The funding under this sub-component considers the extension of the distribution network and the deployment of solar-powered mini-grids. The reimbursable grant will be structured in a way that the demand risk for mini-grid IPPs is covered and such to allow for the development of a framework potentially other MOB's instruments, and the Sub-component 3.1 mini-grid demand risk instrument. # Tajikistan

This project will enable a 300 MW solar PV IPP in Sughid, estimated to require US$140 million**, financed by the private sector. The GCF-WB financed project (USD 40.5m) aims to do the following:

- **Sub-Component 1.2:** Transaction advisors for Solar PV and Capacity Building (at least USD $1 million IDA + ESMAP funding). Considering the much-needed technical support to improve the government’s capacity in planning, designing, implementing, and operating solar power plants, this component will provide transaction advisory services to support the GoT of the development of the solar plant. This will include the technical assistance for project design and implementation such as the technical and legal advisors for the tender.
- **Sub-component 2.1:** Public Infrastructure for solar park (up to US$32 million IDA). This component aims at providing public infrastructure for the solar park, including a nearby substation, access roads, water supply and drainage, and telecommunications infrastructure.

- **Component 3.2:** Guarantee for risk mitigation (up to USD $150 million GCF guarantee to leverage USD $4740 million from private sector). This component will provide risk mitigation support to the private sector investors in the project. Considering the Tajik country and sector risks, investors and lenders are expected to rely on MDBs’ risk mitigation instruments such as guarantees, syndicated loans, or other forms of credit enhancement to avoid financial close or incur higher risk premium. Such security is typically 6-12 months of the IPP’s expected revenue stream from operations and maintenance (O&M) revenues before any potential default. The GCF guarantee will be structured based on the underlying criteria and procedures of the World Bank guarantee. The design and size of the payment security and guarantees required will be informed by ongoing market sourcing and discussions.

## B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

The following section describes the overall Facility management, the management process to ensure the most transformational projects are selected, and to deploy the legal arrangements between GCF, World Bank and the countries.

### 1. SRMI-Resilience as a GCF Program

The SRMI-Resilience is a program to support a series of projects that proposes under this second phase to support nine new projects subject to the results of their appraisal. All projects which will ultimately be supported by the SRMI-Resilience are expected to have significant scale and/or transformative impact.

This Program aims to enhance coordination and strategic alignment of the projects in a manner that can increase the prospects of transformational change, provide operational efficiencies and accelerate project implementation.

This Funding Proposal includes indicative information for each of the currently proposed nine projects. Under the programmatic approach, the GCF would rely on the AE’s due diligence on the proposed projects included in the Program. Subject to the outcome of such due diligence, the World Bank will approve funding for such projects that meet the eligibility criteria as presented in Section B.3.


---
**Page 57**
---

The EE are responsible for ensuring the implementation of the projects and the fulfillment of their development objective. The AE will provide implementation support to the EE to the extent permitted under its policies.

# Implementation Arrangements

As part of its role as GCF AE and co-financer of the projects to be supported under the Facility, the World Bank will assess and confirm the eligibility of each such Project to be included in the Facility and benefit from the resources provided by the Facility. The EE will provide all necessary information for this assessment.

The World Bank team will comprise energy and climate finance experts to manage and be responsible for the Facility's overall objectives and ensure that the projects selected meet all the eligibility criteria as prescribed under the GCF, FAA, and AMA.

Following the GCF Board approval, the World Bank and the GCF will base on the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA), enter into one Funded Activity Agreement – FAA for the provision of the funds under all GCF Instruments supporting the Program. The World Bank will be solely responsible for the management and implementation of the Facility and its projects in accordance with the terms and conditions set forth by it, consistent with its policies, procedures and practices, and the relevant provisions of the FAA and AMA. The World Bank will apply its own fiduciary principles and standards relating to any integrity checks, anti-corruption, conflict of interest, and other related matters.

The World Bank will inform the GCF Secretariat about projects selected in accordance with the Eligibility Criteria as part of the periodic reporting outlined in Section E7. During the implementation of the Facility, the World Bank will be responsible for providing the necessary governance, oversight and quality assurance in accordance with its policies, procedures and any specific requirements in the FAA and AMA.

The World Bank will use its operation systems and established processes to review individual project proposals against the eligibility criteria set out in the present Funding Proposal and approve allocations. It would also facilitate the reporting in an integrated way by the GCF on the SRN Facility's performance as required under the terms of the Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) and any related Subsidiary Agreements with the EE or other counterparties as applicable, with any liqentary PIEs and guarantee obligees and beneficiaries. These transactions will make available World Bank finance as well as GCF resources.

There is no capacity assessment of the countries presently in the Facility, as those will be conducted during the appraisal stage of each project. When the projects go through the appraisal stage, some of the capacity assessment information will be summarized in the projects' appraisal documents and will be publicly disclosed on the World Bank website. The EE will provide all necessary information for this assessment.

The World Bank will ensure that adequate fiduciary (procurement and financial management [FM]), E&S safeguards, legal and monitoring & evaluation (M&E) frameworks are in place: (i) the different experts within the World Bank team have the required competencies to perform their tasks; (ii) the capacity of the EE is sufficient and not a project management unit (PMU) with the core expertise is usually excepted (in case of guarantees) part of the legal covenants for EEs to meet. Figure 5 presents the indicative implementation arrangements for Components 1 and 2.

B

The indicative mechanism contemplated for the reimbursable grants under Sub-Component 3.1, subject to future due diligence, market sounding, and World Bank management approval, are as follows:

a. Eligible sub-projects: BE private sector projects selected by the Executing Entity with the technical assistance of the World Bank to ensure that they meet both the eligibility criteria, as well as the Bank's mandatory requirements (including safeguards and fiduciary) for sub-projects, as stated in the GCF financing agreement between the Accredited Entity and the Executing Entity.

b. Executing Entity(ies): A Host Country, expected to be Somalia.

c. Beneficiaries: private sector developers preparing a public-private partnership for the development of mini-grid sub-projects with an agreed business plan

d. Covered Risk: mitigation of the critical risks related to private capital investment in the off-grid space - many of which are unique and not covered by insurance, for the first few years of operation and consequently financial risks affecting pricing and tenor of financing available to the investors (to support viability during the initial years of the sub-projects)

e. Financial terms: Disbursements to the project company established by the private developers for the implementation of its sub-project, during a pre-agreed number of years at the beginning of the operation of the mini-grid sub-project. The disbursement amount may not be sufficient to reach viability of the mini grid projected in the pre-agreed business plan.

Reimbursement during a pre-agreed number of years starting not later than the date when the demand for electricity from the mini-grid sub-project reaches the level of demand as forecasted and verified in the pre-agreed business plan

Triggering events: The disbursements are expected to be set by reference to the verified level of the project company revenues above the agreed viability level in the audited business plan during initial (and riskier) stages of the operation of the sub-project.

Topping-up payments: Topping-up payments are to be set by reference to the verified level of the project company revenues above the pre-agreed viability threshold

## Covered Risk: Two of the three structures (the letter-of-credit payment guarantee and direct covered payment) could support payments under a power purchase agreement (PPA) to an Executing Entity (EE). The third structure, the commercial bank guarantee, and the nature of the direct covered payment would depend on the project as it takes shape. This means that the commercial bank guarantee could provide appropriate support to commercial lenders against public-sector risks related to long-term obligations to mobilize capital to private investors (that is, Executing Entity RE project companies) for a RE project.

## Financial terms: The conditions of guarantees would reflect World Bank's policy and procedural requirements but given their nature, guarantees would be specifically negotiated with the participating parties. In addition, the World Bank would not provide any financial resources; it would only be provided to the extent necessary to mobilize private financing for the projects and/or to mitigate payment risks of the projects, taking into account country, project and market circumstances.

### Sub-Component 3.3 is an important complement to Sub-Component 2.2 in countries where FORDEX availability is limited and is as critical to the bankability of private RE projects as credit enhancement instruments. The eligibility criteria for inclusion of PPP projects under any such facility shall include alignment with the least cost generation expansion plan and a competitive bidding process for selection of the project developer.

The indicative mechanism contemplated for the reimbursable grants under Sub-Component 3.3., subject to future due diligence, market sounding, and World Bank management approval, are as follows:

a. Eligible sub-projects: RE private sector projects selected by the EE, with the technical assistance of the World Bank to ensure that they meet both the eligibility criteria as well as the Bank's mandatory requirements (including safeguards and fiduciary) for sub-projects, as stated in the GCF financing agreement between the AE and the EE.

b. Executing Entity(ies): A Host Country, expected to be Ethiopia.

c. Beneficiaries: (i) private sector RE project companies and/or (ii) commercial lenders to the private sector RE project company

d. Covered Risk: following a period to be determined, liability to convert payments received in local currency into foreign exchange to fulfill FOREX obligations, including but not limited to operation and maintenance (O&M) costs, debt servicing, and distributions to private investors. In countries where FOREX reserves are insufficient to support local currency commodity and fuel availability on a timely basis critical to mobilize project capital and ensure continued receivables in the long term to commercial financiers of RE projects. The forex liquidity support mechanism would thus provide for an ongoing commitment by the Government to replenish the facility each time a draw is made. More detail on the proposed structure is found in the Feasibility Study.

### Projects Arrangements and Reporting Requirements

World Bank-financed projects follow a robust framework to design, approve, implement, and supervise projects consistent with the World Bank's policies and procedures. All the potential Facility-supported projects will follow the WB project cycle, as well as the Bank’s operational policies and procedures. For each Project included in the Program, including those for which a GCF financing would be requested, the World Bank will provide technical assistance to ensure that the project is designed and implemented with due regard for quality control covering the IFRIDDA financing and GCF financing, following standard WB procedures. Once all the relevant internal World Bank’s requirements are met, the project moves to negotiations and World Bank Board approval.

The implementation of the Facility-supported projects (project implementation through completion and enforcement) will be managed by a Project Management Unit (PMU). The PMU is responsible for the execution of guarantees projects), a project management unit (PMU) will be established with a team expected to include at least a lead team, technical expert, procurement specialist, financial management specialist, environmental specialist and social/gender specialist, and a lawyer. Each project will have its own M&E and reporting process, and the EE will be accountable for its implementation.

### B.5 Justification for GCF funding request (Max 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

The instruments proposed under SBIRI-2 Resilience have been developed based on discussions with the EE. The request for funding is at the core of their needs which is (i) publicly owned grid infrastructure to enable private generation for all countries; (ii) two innovative risk mitigation instruments for Somalia and Ethiopia, and (iii) proposes guarantees for utility-scale solar for two countries that requested them. Guinea Bissau has taken a similar approach.

* Kyrgyzstan has requested an IDA guarantee;
* Ethiopia has already a line of IDA guarantees under an arrangement with the World Bank;
* The Philippines has requested a credit facility from ADB, which is linked to the FX risk mitigation instrument, which requires an initial cash funding; (iv) Togo, Mongolia, and Seychelles are assessing their needs for guarantee and guarantees could be provided either by WB, ADB, or IDA. Indonesia has requested a line of IDA guarantees under an arrangement with the World Bank;
* Indonesia does not require guarantees as it has a bankable utility.

Therefore, all in together we are requesting GCF risk mitigation instruments for (i) products that are not provided by other entities; and (ii) to cover countries that have no access to other sources of financing.

We believe that GCF funding will be critical not only to reduce the cost of the investments but also
to (i) allow innovative products such as the FX risk or the mini-grid demand side to be developed;
(ii) finance innovation in the energy sector; and (iii) support the development of de-risking programs for IPPs with technical assistance and improved grid make more resilient and with reduction in coalburn. GCF funds are key for the development of the projects.

GCF funding is critical to address the current RE market failures identified and to ensure that countries can access financing for their energy transition. The main challenges are (i) being constrained due to lack of existing government strategy to adapt the current energy sector to the new demands; (ii) limited access to finance due to high interest rates, low credit ratings, and limited financing constraints;
(iii) conflicting views on short-term vs medium-term economic growth needs; and (iv) domestic production/import of fossil fuel. VRE deployment is also expensive when accounting the integration costs and grid reinforcement. The cost of solar PV has been reduced significantly but it remains competitive with coal generation. This may lead to a tough political-economic decision when a government has to choose between promoting least-cost coal production versus slightly more expensive and more complex renewable energy.

This situation was aggravated by the COVID-19 crisis, impacting the RE deployment. It is more difficult for the governments to spare public financing for critical public investments needed to unlock private investments as public financing is prohibited to tackle those challenges including health and jobs.
addition, the utilities themselves are facing unprecedented challenges with payments issues and lower


---
**Page 65**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 61

## B

demand affects their revenues, and therefore, private investors are perceiving them as riskier, increasing the premium on their cost of capital which in turn would increase the electricity price.

To face these challenges, SRMI-Resilience is providing an integrated risk mitigation framework to (i) reduce the cost of privately-owned generation (lowering the risk premium embedded in the tariff) making the electricity sector more attractive for private investment; and (ii) provide a structured approach to public investments needed to enable private investments making them more affordable for the countries, which would be sufficient in some cases despite for back-up power supply, and (iii) maximize socio-economic benefits from the transition by supporting the development of local content and capacity building processes.

### Catalyzing GCF concessional financing will be critical. GCF funding is needed as it: (i) comes sooner when the poorest in the world are undergoing dramatic tensions on their budgets due to COVID-19; (ii) will turn towards the cheapest source of energy with the lowest CAPEX under the current budgetary constraints except if it does not immediately impacting their budgets; (iii) with the investment environment improving, countries will be more open to concessional financing and are likely to have a better credit rating which usually are not financially viable. Under the current COVID-19 crisis and the risk of having coal and gas stranded assets, there is an urgent need for GCF funding to support developing countries in their energy transition are more critical than ever. Access to concessional public climate financing and DFI financing would enable solar and wind generation to be cheaper than the replacement cost of fossil fuel plants.

The use of deal with the very real impacts of COVID-19 on the health and welfare of their population, countries may fall into a fast recovery strategy that would promote fossil fuel generation unless there is strong parallel support for energy transition. The SRMI approach will provide a structured framework to help countries identify and technically revise their current plans and implemented the revised ones to avoid building new coal and gas plants and instead invest in renewable energy projects. This will also enable countries to redirect the funds spent on fossil fuels subsidies. In addition, by using concessional financing to reduce perceived risks by private sector investors, countries would be able to unlock at scale privately financed RE projects especially if they are not yet commercially viable or have a high risk profile. The SRMI approach, which is aligned with its mandate and scale, will enable a scale-up of the SRMI approach that will enable real impact. In addition, supporting the scale-up of RE through a systematic, integrated and open approach such as SRMI will ensure the transition to clean energy is not only economically viable but also socially inclusive.

The use of climate concessional financing under the Initiative is also essential to support innovative technologies, with a significant mitigation potential that are not yet commercially viable such as battery storage. The current market conditions are challenging for these technologies and the renewable industry supporting these paradigm-shifting disruptive technologies is at a nascent stage and will also suffer due to lack of market uptake unless there is continued demand from countries. Without additional investment in these technologies, they may never reach commercial viability which would increase the risk of load shedding and systems' difficulties. However, given the high cost of technologies and the risk arising from their experience in deploying these technologies at scale in the context of developing countries, private sector investors are unlikely to invest unless there is strong public support that development support and strong risk coverage.

Private investments in the off-grid RE space require adapted risk mitigation instruments that can be provided by SRMI-Resilience. This will be more flexible than multi DFI guarantees. A risk mitigation mechanism, as developed under Component 3.1, that covers critical risks such as demand off-take risk will be an important catalyst in helping attract more private sector investment and scale up mini grids and SHS.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 62

This demand loan is not covered by traditional DFI risk mitigation mechanisms like MIGA insurance or WB guarantees. GCF's support will be crucial to ensure that the right risk mitigation instrument is being provided.

With the exception of countries which receive grants from the World Bank for infrastructure due to their level of GDP per capita or post-conflict status (such as Guinea Bissau, Tajikistan and Somalia in the current portfolio), the decision on whether a demand loan or grant is appropriate for a country and its needs and loans is done between technical assistance/capacity building and infrastructure. IDA is providing grants to countries with low per capita GDP and post-conflict status (such as Afghanistan, Burundi, and Conflict [ICS] countries) and Tajikistan is recovering from major domestic political and security crisis.

The three countries are among the poorest countries in the world. The other six countries are split between ten and eleven of the least developed countries (LDCs). Given the limitations associated with the SRI methodology, the activities promoted under the technical assistance component are critical requirements for the development of a well-organized, sustainable and affordable generation mix as well as demand-side management. These activities are also critical to ensure that the investments will not be expensive or complex in general for the countries to do without concessional support. Therefore, without support from GCF, these countries would not have the capacity to undertake such activities on their own and make a decision with regards to new generation or transmission investments and they are usually not compatible with the current climate urgency. GCF's financing will be needed to ensure that the most fragile on the planet can access renewable energy.

Public investments are critical to enable the sustainable unlocking of private investments. However, governments are often stretched in their budget due to overwhelming social sector priorities. In this context, catalytic climate financing will help support more investments in RE. The approach promoted under the GCF is to provide concessional funding for RE projects that would otherwise be limited to critical public investment required for a higher penetration of renewables such as VRE integration and grid expansion. This will allow for a more efficient use of public resources by enabling better allocation between different sectors, especially driven by pressing needs in the social sectors which are being exacerbated by the COVID-19 crisis. The possibility to access climate financing for those critical infrastructure projects is also important because it allows for a more balanced approach when allocating funds for education or health sectors.

The level of GCF participation in the financing of the currently proposed projects has been determined based on discussions with countries and their possibility to increase their financial contribution over time.

Another critical aspect of SRI-Resilience that will support an increased public investment allocation towards adaptation measures is the concessionality of GCF compared to IBRD and to a certain extent, IDA. The concessional terms for the proposed projects are as follows: 100% grant will be sought to be able not to increase the cost for the IDA countries while being attractive enough for the IBRD countries to make the investment towards adaptation measures and low-carbon infrastructure testing long-term solutions.

The GCF high concessional terms will enable the investments to be viable for both IDA and IBRD countries – and in turn the concessionality will fully benefit the electricity households of these countries. The concessional terms are as follows: 100% grant for IDA countries, and a loan with an interest period of 25 years at an all-in-of 0.25 percent (i.e., 0.00 percent interest rate, 0.25 percent service fee (and waiver of 0.50 percent commitment fee) will result in an increase of 0.5-1 percent in project internal rate of return (IRR). The concessional terms for IDA countries are as follows: a loan with an interest period of 30 years and the total increase in the IRR could go up to 2 percent (but with an average of 1 percent increase in financial

B. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Sustainability is at the heart of GCF, mainstreamed in a cross-cutting integrated approach. Supporting countries to design and implement a sustainable low-emission development pathway, from a multi-dimensional perspective, fostering a transformational change with projects replicable at the level of the country or region.

From a technical perspective, ensuring through comprehensive platforms that sound RE targets are defined based on least cost planning, factoring in integration of VRE and grid resilience, and promoting energy efficiency and demand-side management. 

From a financial perspective, reducing reliance on public sector financing by supporting the design and implementation of bankable projects that could be financed by the private sector.

First, strategic alignment with national development plans, ensuring coherence aligned with the country’s priorities and strategic considerations (including items in terms of risk allocation and roles and responsibilities among stakeholders).

From an environmental perspective, financing countries’ NDCs in an economically viable manner while ensuring their implementation. 

Finally, from a capacity-building perspective, strengthening the countries' framework and developing the skills needed (including by financing the transaction advisors for the first round of projects under the RE program defined allowing “a learning by doing” with hands-on experience), to give to the


---
**Page 68**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 64

B

courage me to replicate the projects, and fully integrate the changes they are facing in our energy systems into their long-term strategy.

f. From a socio-economic perspective, ensuring that the positive impacts on the local industries (positioning them as leaders in the solar/wind value chain) in a competitive manner is essential for supporting local community development (through actions increasing their resilience and enhancing their livelihoods).

g. From an economic perspective, ensuring that men and women benefit from the RE projects launched with a proactive policy to promote female employment throughout the RE project cycle—as otherwise men may dominate the sector.

h. From a financial perspective, SRM's core investment in infrastructure for the grid and with regards to electrification are long-term investments that have long-term potential impact and will be critical to the economic development of the countries involved. The availability of the 500 MW solar PV instrument will enable us to unlock the pipeline under Round 2 of Ethiopia’s Solar Program, with 750 MW of solar projects that are currently unable to move forward—the current allocation under Sub-Component 1 is committed to allow for around 600 MW of PV. Further detail on the proposed Facility is provided below.

Therefore, the approach used by the SRM Facility is supporting countries in developing a multi-dimensional sustainable RE program and operationalizing the first round of bankable projects. By having a strong pipeline of projects that have been developed through design workshops, capacity building will be able to capitalize on the work done, the track-record built, and the skills developed for their next round of RE projects.

The SRM Facility is expected to act as a catalyst to unlock these sustainable RE programs in the countries and pave the way to operationalize them through bankable projects privately financed. With a strong pipeline of projects that have been developed through design workshops, capacity building would be in a position to replicate these projects and to attract private investment in optimized conditions. Beyond its well-known benefits, increasing the share of the private sector can also be the sole solution for a country to achieve sustainable development. In many cases, however, the lack of a government guarantee can be a significant hurdle in securing private finance.

The SRM-Resilience Facility helps bridge the gap between the inability of governments to provide guarantees and the high risks perceived by the private sector, being enabled by GCF guarantees, which are provided on a risk-sharing basis with the private sector.

In Ethiopia, a FOREX liquidity support facility would ensure that forex-denominated debt payments are paid on schedule, thus ensuring that projects are bankable. A USD 20 million facility would be enough to cover the current allocation under Sub-Component 1 of Round 2 of Ethiopia’s Solar Program. The rationale for which the FOREX liquidity support facility would be in place to cover solar projects which are to be awarded under Round 2 of the Ethiopian Solar Program, reaching a total mobilization of around USD 480 million.

## Promoting a sustainable environment for private investors as part of the energy sector is critical to the SGR approach.
Capacity building is key from that perspective to ensure that local stakeholders are effectively empowered in relation to the value chain to the extent possible. A multi-dimensional capacity building program is considered under SRMII for that purpose, covering from the design of the RE strategy to their implementation.

To increase the effectiveness of capacity building, the approach promotes a strategic analysis upfront on the RE sector and its development potential with respect to the key stakeholders and assesses the local industries' potential as to their positioning on the value chain. The objective being to define a concrete action plan to develop the targeted skills of the key stakeholders and improve their chances for success in the RE sector.

The SRMII program is designed to support the implementation of the RE strategy by building a program to position the local players on the operation and maintenance of the solar/wind plants, allowing them to benefit from the transfer of knowledge needed as well as from the hands-on experience gained through the implementation of the projects. The SRMII program will be supported from the support of transition advisors financed under SRMII to accompany the countries in this pathway towards a more sustainable and inclusive energy sector.

In addition, integration of VRE is a key aspect. SRMII is also promoting a transfer of knowledge in favor of key local stakeholders in relation to storage and more particularly with respect to battery technology. The SRMII program will support the development of a pilot project at the World Bank in collaboration with partners to cost this technology, not yet mature but presenting a significant potential to enable higher penetration of VRE in the grid. The SRMII Facility is directly contributing to the successful implementation of the SRMII program by enabling the countries to implement it, building a track-record as well as crucial knowledge.

The outcomes expected from Component 1 focused on capacity building are threshold:
- technical capability with regards to planning and ability of the utility's dispatch team to manage variable generation and battery storage;
- procurement capacity with regards to selection of IPPs and such by incorporating VRE into their bidding process, including a focus on the development of local distribution capacity with regards to developing projects and managing a program of solar/wind parks.

# EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF’s [Investment Policy](https://www.greenclimate.fund/investment-policy).

## D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The nine proposed projects are expected to contribute to the shift to low-emission sustainable development pathways and increased resilience of infrastructure and population with the following:

- **Directly support the development of an estimated 2.16 GW of RE**, ((i) 1.76 GW of solar PV, (ii) 300 MW of onshore wind, and (iii) 100 MW of geothermal). The 2.16 GW RE capacity is expected to be built during the lifetime of the projects.
- **Directly reduce GHG emissions by an estimated 55.01 metric tons of CO₂e**, reflecting fossil fuel and coal displacement by 2,160 MW of RE built under the project over the 25 years of their operation.
- **Unlock an estimated USD 1.8 billion of private investments**, for the 2,160 MW of RE built during the lifetime of the projects.
- **Directly increase the climate change resilience of an estimated 3.2 million people** through access to low-emission electricity. In heat wave and extreme temperature-prone countries, access to cooling solutions will be critical to improve vulnerable populations’ adaptive capacities.
- **Strengthen institutional and regulatory systems for energy planning and development integrated with climate adaptation**, as well as the capacity building of national institutions. The infrastructure built under the project will be designed taking into account climate risk (specific to each country and region). Capacity building (through, e.g., the adoption of a National Energy Strategy) is expected to continue beyond the lifetime of the program. This approach is necessary to ensure that public infrastructure services will be made more resilient to climate change and will be able to fully play their role.
- **Finance the USD 18 billion needed for energy systems in Africa to become resilient to climate shocks**, taking into account long-lived, climate-vulnerable infrastructure by increasing resilience of grid infrastructure and improving generation plans to integrate climate impacts on the power sector. The infrastructure built under this program will be designed with a view to indirectly support over 22.5 million people to become more resilient to climate change, with an emphasis on economic resilience of businesses that were accounted for in the beneficiaries.

D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

By tackling in a coordinated manner planning, procurement, development and curtailment risks to attract affordable private investments, SIFM aims at supporting countries in their low-emission energy pathway transformation towards a future where more than half of the projects have been implemented. To move from a situation with a low VRE penetration to one where the majority of the electricity is produced from RE sources, governments will need to put in place sustainable governance and regulatory frameworks that enable private sector investment at scale. This represents large investments from the public sector to enable such framework and will require strong governments buy-in and ownership. The SIFM Facility aims at addressing these commitments to their path to low-emission development while ensuring it does not become an economic burden.

Building on a sound methodology[1], SIFM supports countries transform their strategy to low-emission energy pathway, bringing them in line with the Paris Agreement. The first step is to source demand and identify long-term targets for RE based on concrete analysis for demand, domestic resources and grid flexibility and stability. These targets will be the basis for a realistic but also ambitious plan to deploy RE in the given country. Through the SIFM[2] methodology, countries can use this approach to define their RE strategy and roadmap and identify the critical investments needed to integrate VRE into their grids while improving its

[1] “A Sure Path to Sustainable Solar: Solar Deployment Guidelines” was released during the 2013 United Nations Climate Summit in New York and are accessible online through the following link: http://www.greenclimate.fund/en/download/0945217768432241-A-Sure-Path-to-Sustainable-Solar-Solar-Deployment-Guidelines

## Reliability and resilience. These targets will then need to be integrated into their NDC revisions ensuring they are implementable and that would integrate adaptation criteria.

- After the targets are set, the SRMI Facility is supporting the nine countries to develop a long-term strategy to implement those targets. The strategy looks at who and how to finance the deployment of RE and the associated infrastructure. It also identifies all the legal changes that need to be implemented in order for the country to be able to access the SRMI Facility. In addition, it will look into financing the debt part of the privately-owned power plants to reduce the risk of foreign exchange to the utility companies. The resulting benefits can be integrated into the long-term strategy. It can support countries in leveraging the deployment of solar and wind to enable the population to gain opportunities for jobs and development.

- The first pillar to the paradigm shift is capacity building. As part of SRMI, it is critical to increase capacity within the government on all fronts so that afterwards they will be able to conduct analysis without SRMI support. This will ensure that the countries are in a position to implement their NDCs.

After supporting the development of the country's long-term targets, its strategy to implement them and increase capacity, the SRMI Facility is financing the implementation strategy. It is critical to consider the combined effect of all these pillars as they are interdependent. If one pillar is missing or such combined approach, the countries may not be able to finance the targets or have the right target in the first place.

- As a consequence, countries will be able to operationalize their NDCs in a sustainable and timely manner in spite of the effect of the COVID-19 crisis, reducing significantly their GHG emissions trajectory by shifting to a low-emission development pathway while adapting its systems to climate change. The SRMI Facility is designed with a multi-dimensional sustainability approach at its heart. This low-emission development pathway will be replicable, fast-tracking the road towards the Sustainable Development Goals for these countries.

The nine countries are representative of different types of markets/energy sector, with low to high electrification levels and varying degrees of access to RE technologies. The lessons learned from those nine countries will enable to replicate the SRMI approach across countries and enable to unlock sustainable RE markets for private investments. SRMI is currently being implemented/considered for a number of other countries in Africa and Asia, which will also benefit from the replicability of the SRMI Facility.

Tailored support for each country will be provided under the digital approach. The status of RE development is different from one country to another and will be further assessed at Project level to leverage the existing related activities. The technical assistance deployed under the SRMI Facility will be tailored for each country, with a view to supporting the transition on the current systems already deployed in coordination with all stakeholders (including other DFIs as well as the energy sector system). This will include for example adjusting existing least cost plans in place if needed or including the lessons learnt from existing RE projects in terms of site allocation, government support, public to private creation etc.

### D.3 Sustainable development (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

Sustainability is at the heart of the multi-dimensional approach of SRMI. The SRMI approach aims at anchoring RE projects of the targeted countries in a sustainable ecosystem ensuring a lasting and replicable

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 71

## Low-emission development pathway, fast-tracking in particular the SDG 13 Climate Action and the SDG 7 Affordable and Clean Energy.

Sound planning associated with adequate grid investments to enable a higher penetration of renewables while enhancing resilience will lead to the development of sustainable RE targets. It will avoid stranded assets and ensure long-term sustainability. Renewable energy resources such as solar or wind for generation will also reduce the need for fuel imports and the potential electricity demand on fossil fuels. It will increase the energy security of the countries, diversifying their mix and increasing their energy independence.

The operationalization of the RE targets through bankable projects with a fair risk allocation, backed by appropriate financial instruments and regulatory frameworks with adequate risk mitigation arrangements, aims to ensure the timely implementation of these projects. It aims to leverage private investments at scale, ensuring the availability of the commercial financing and the appetite of the private sector for investing in renewable energy projects. The RE targets will be operationalized through bankable projects that could be directed to other sectors in need such as healthcare, in particular in the context of COVID-19.

The strategic analysis upfront to define an action plan to maximize the socio-economic benefits of the RE targets and to ensure their sustainability is a key element for the success and the operationalization of the projects. It will enhance the involvement of the local players on the solar/wind value chain in a competitive manner to the extent possible, maximizing job creation, skills development and transfer. The project implementation will be based on a phased approach with an emphasis on the sustainability of the projects through actions increasing their resilience and enhancing their livelihoods. A specific attention will be brought to women empowerment as women often may benefit disproportionately from new renewable energy investments.

Capacity building will be mainstreamed throughout the RE projects cycle, strengthening the countries’ framework and developing the skills needed (including through “learning by doing” approach with the support of international partners) for the implementation, monitoring and evaluation of the projects. Building on the strong foundations of the program, key public stakeholders will be able to capitalize on the work done, the track-record built, and the skills developed for their next round of RE projects.
