### Ethiopia

The energy sector is the fourth most important driver of overall emissions in Ethiopia (after livestock, LUCF and industry) – as per the country's updated NDC. It is expected, however, that emissions from this sector will peak around 2035 and then decline due to a combination of factors: higher urbanization while the electricity generation is almost entirely comprised of hydropower and wind power. The energy sector is also one of the largest emitters in Ethiopia, with the majority of energy towards overall emissions emanates from the widespread use of biomass for non-electric energy services. By prioritizing renewable energy development to enhance access to modern energy sources, the country can reduce its reliance on fossil fuels and improve air quality by reducing the extensive use of wood for fuel. As a matter of fact, shifting from unsustainable biomass energy demand to renewable biofuels and electric stoves is one of the policy interventions targeted by Ethiopia's NDC. The country has set itself an ambitious target of 10 GW of installed renewable energy capacity in 9 MTCO2e by 2030 under the conditional scenario, equating a relative reduction in emissions from this sector to around 50% compared with business as usual (BAU) projections alone. Although most mitigation contributions in the energy sector concern energy efficiency and fuel consumption in the transport sector, it is noted in the updated NDC that renewable energy could have a significant role in reducing emissions from the energy sector. In addition to the above-mentioned adaptation contributions include, for example: the increase in the number of households that use renewable off-grid sources for lighting. Coupled to this, there is also an adaptation measure which aims at increasing the resilience and diversification of the power system.

With the increase in energy demand and economic growth foreseen in Ethiopia, renewable energy projects will become even more important as Ethiopia starts facing the impacts of climate change with regards to water scarcity, as noted in Ethiopia's National Adaptation Plan. Renewable energy projects are also expected to play a key role in reducing the country’s emissions established through its Climate Resilient Green Economy (CRGE) strategy, from which Ethiopia's updated NDC is set. The projects have the potential of having an impact on the grid connected and related access to electricity will increase the resilience of communities and diversification of the power mix will increase the resilience of the power system and improve energy security through the reduction of the reliance on emergency fuel use during droughts that are more and more common due to climate change (mostly diesel). In 2019, the cost of power disruptions in the country amounted

To ensure a 2.5 percent[16] increase in energy access and reduce power outages, hence reducing the burden caused by an unreliable power sector on the economy.

#### Guinea Bissau: Guinea Bissau is a Least Developed Country (LDC) and Small Island Developing State (SIDS). In recent years, the Government of Guinea-Bissau has undertaken a number of action plans with a view to increase access to energy and expand electricity supply infrastructure. These include: (i) the National Action Plan for Energy Efficiency in Guinea Bissau (Plano de Ação Nacional das Energias Renováveis na Guiné-Bissau, PANER), 2017; (ii) the National Strategy for the Development of Electricity (Estratégia Nacional para a Eficiência Energética na Guiné-Bissau, PANEI); and more recently, (iii) the Master Plan for Energy and Infrastructure Development for the Generation of Electricity, 2020 (Estratégia Nacional de Desenvolvimento da Geração Elétrica, ENDEGE). The Government also adopted the National Strategic Plan (TERIA RANCA 2015-2025). These documents set the basis for Guinea Bissau's updated NDC, submitted to the UNFCCC on 12 October 2021. In its NDC, Guinea Bissau aims at increasing access to electricity from 34 percent in 2019 to 78 percent by 2030 and electrify by 2050, and to reduce emissions from the energy sector by 44 percent by 2030 compared to business as usual (BAU). The NDC sets out a number of measures including the following: (i) set-up of an installed renewable energy capacity of around 90 MW by 2030, compared to 5 MW currently; (ii) significant reduction in electricity grid losses, which would drop from 41 percent in 2018 to below 26 percent by 2030 and 17 percent by 2050; (iii) the private sector and commercial sector and (iv) the implementation of energy efficiency actions in public and commercial buildings and in industry. Additionally, the NDC highlights the importance of blended financing to improve access to electricity as a national priority. The proposed project thus aligns with Guinea Bissau's targets and climate strategy, and coherent with the cited national policies. It will contribute to the achievement of the following Sustainable Development Goals: (i) it will increase the resilience of communities and reduce the pressure on forestry and diversification of the power mix; (ii) it will increase the resilience of the power system and improve energy security through the reduction in electricity grid losses, which would drop from 41 percent in 2018 to below 26 percent by 2030 and 17 percent by 2050. The project is also aligned with the need for climate funds and that Guinea Bissau aims to do so with share reduction in emissions.

#### Indonesia: The Indonesian Government is committed to accelerating its mitigation efforts but is also wary of the cost that it will represent. In 2018, Indonesia was the tenth largest emitter of greenhouse gases (GHG) globally. According to the NDC[17] submitted by Indonesia on 3 October 2020 and updated in 2021, the government committed to an unconditional reduction of GHG emissions by 26 percent relative to business-as-usual scenario (BAU) by 2030 and to a conditional reduction by 41 percent. The NDC also sets out a number of measures including: (i) increasing the share of renewable energy in total electricity generation from 7 percent in 2018 to 25 percent by 2030, and 60 percent by 2050; (ii) committing to a net-zero economy by 2060. According to the government, the net-zero investment needs would be around $4 trillion over the next three decades. The NDC also sets out that Indonesia is committed to a 2030–2030, and 20 percent of total investment thereafter through 2050. It is also concerned that mitigation efforts could undermine growth and jobs and slow down its development. The energy sector is the largest contributor to GHG emissions in Indonesia, accounting for around 64 percent of total emissions. Of this, 57 percent of Indonesia's GHG emissions were the results of land-use change and peat and forest fires, and 33 percent were due to the energy sector. Both forest & land use and the energy sector are also major contributors to deforestation in Indonesia. In addition, by 2040, emissions from the energy sector are expected to become the highest contributor to GHG emissions

[16] Source: http://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/3967115879723631/pdf/UtilizedPotentialTheBusinessCostsOfUnreliableInfrastructureInDevelopingCountries.pdf

In Indonesia, one of the highest power grid emission factors is found in the region. The country’s National Energy Plan for Electricity (RUPEN) and the National General Plan for Renewable Energy/Power (RUPEN/RENEN) set out major objectives and targets for the sector: the share of renewable energy in electricity generation will increase from 10% in 2025 to reach 23 percent of the primary energy supply. In addition, under the net-zero scenarios, the RE targets would need to be raised to 60 percent of power generation by 2030 and 52 percent by 2050. The country has also made commitments in the Paris Agreement to reduce its emissions by 29% compared with 2017 levels by 2030, and it has made voluntary commitments on coal moratorium; no new coal-fired power plants after 2022 and no more coal after 2026. The country is also committed to the 3GW of coal capacity that was already contracted for the most part and will come as an addition to the 3GW already operating. In the previous generation plan, the RUPEN 2018-2028, the new coal capacity will be commissioned during this period. The country is also committed to phasing out all existing coal-fired power plants until the expiration of the PPAs. However, discussions are ongoing for an accelerated retirement of some coal plants. As stated by authorities during the COP26 in Glasgow, Indonesia has made a commitment to reduce its emissions intensity by 41% compared with 2019 levels and to achieve net-zero emissions by 2060. The country is also committed to international financial and technical assistance. In the short term, as per the government’s announcement, the coal power plants will be replaced by natural gas-fired power plants. This compares to 2GW in the current plan, increasing by 6 GW the removal of coal power plants from the grid. The project will have two different kinds of impact in Indonesia: expanded and reliable access to electricity for millions of people living without it; and a reduction in emissions intensity that will increase the resilience of the power system and improve energy security through the reduction of the reliance on fossil fuel (mostly coal and diesel). The project will directly support this objective by funding the replacement of coal-fired power plants with natural gas-fired power plants.

d. Kyrgyzstan 60 percent of all emissions in Kyrgyzstan are found in the energy sector, being thus one of the key sectors under which mitigation actions are found. According to the updated NDC, the mitigation capacity in the energy sector should be realized both by the replacement of fossil-based electricity generation with renewable energy sources and by increasing energy efficiency.

Under the contribution to mitigation, the country intends to focus on a decrease of coal consumption, reduction of emissions from the transport sector (including road traffic, aviation, maritime transport), including hydro, biogas, geothermal, wind and solar), among others. Under its conditional scenario, where international support is assumed, GHG emissions are expected to decrease by 37 percent compared with business as usual in 2050. The country’s energy sector accounts for around 64% of total emissions according to the updated NDC. By 2050 respectively, emission are expected to decrease by around 50 percent, highlighting the growing importance of the energy sector. The updated NDC is also aligned with the Paris Agreement and its goals. It includes a number of adaptation actions in line with the Paris Agreement, including increasing adaptive capacity (e.g., improving water management systems; strengthening climate change and gender issues), strengthening climate resilience by diversifying the energy mix due the climate change impact on country’s hydropower resources and improving infrastructure, as well as to improve the efficiency of energy use. The NDC also includes a number of actions related to the development of tools to solidify the accounting and control over the best rational use of energy resources, as per the updated NDC. Additionally, under the international support, the Kyrgyz Republic will be able to implement a number of projects in the energy sector. For example, in 2019, the cost of power disruptions in the country amounted to around 3.3 percent of the GDP.

The project will contribute to achieving this goal through development platform.

### Mongolia
The mitigation cost of Mongolia's NDC will be a 22.7 percent reduction in total national GHG emissions by 2030 compared to the projected emissions under a business as usual scenario for 2010. If additional measures such as energy efficiency and waste-to-energy technology are implemented, then Mongolia could achieve a 27.2 percent reduction in total national GHG emissions. In recent years, RE has been getting more attention as a key solution to determining an quick transition from fossil fuels. By the end of 2021, the total installed capacity of RE plants in Mongolia was 238 MW, including 155 MW of wind and 83 MW of solar power. The government is committed to increasing the share of RE expansion of coal-based power generation is indispensable for meeting the increasing demand for heating. RE scale-up is considered a key component in a gradual transition toward a more diversified energy mix. Renewable energy sources are expected to represent more than 2/3 of the total emissions reduction while the unconditional measures that are required to meet this target include strong straws. The proposed actions that are most relevant for the proposed activity are on energy production which includes: (i) development of renewable energy sources; and (ii) improved efficiency of energy production. At the COP28, the president of Mongolia made a commitment to implement mitigation measures with a total cost which has been included in the updated NDC and noted that through introducing advanced technology and innovation and increasing necessary funding, there is an opportunity to increase the commitment to 27 percent.

### Seychelles
For its renewable energy transition, the Government of Seychelles has committed to reduce its GHG emissions by 25 percent by 2030, according to its updated NDC. The Energy Act paved the way for a focus on renewable energies. Boosting electricity generation from renewable energies is one of the main objectives of the National Energy Policy. In addition to this objective, there is a 100% commitment to use renewable energy by modernizing the electricity sector, as well as increase energy efficiency and electricity generation from renewable sources as already noted in total, modernization of the electricity sector will be achieved through the introduction of RE sources in comparison to the business as usual scenario. Additionally, the National Energy Policy pursues, in the long term, a 100% share of electricity production coming from renewable energy sources with a target of 15 percent to be reached by 2030. By supporting the installation of VRE in the Seychelles power mix, the project will contribute to the Energy Policy ambition and will also increase the country's resilience against climate change. The transition towards a diversified mix away from fossil fuels coupled with the climate change vulnerability of critical infrastructure (road, ports) participating to the fuel supply chain makes diversification of the power mix a critical aspect to increase the country's resilience.

### Somalia
In its updated NDC, Somalia has set a target to reduce emissions by 30 percent by 2030 in comparison to the business as usual case, totaling 54.2 MCO2eqs. The updated NDC is in line


---
**Page 81**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 77

With its National Planning processes, strategies and actions in the energy, agriculture, forestry, transport and waste sectors. In the energy sector, in particular, Somalia has set different priorities and targets, mostly acting on five levels: (i) development of renewable energy electricity (Solar and Wind), (ii) promotion of distributed renewable lamps, (iii) promotion of use of energy efficient light bulbs and (iv) promotion of energy efficiency in transmission. The energy sector alone should represent 20 percent of total emissions reductions by 2030. In order to achieve this target, a number of actions are needed. The Somali government adopted the Somali Power Sector Master Plan (PSMP) for the period 2015-2040, which is supported by the World Bank and funded under the Green Climate Fund through the Power Sector Development Support Project (PDS618). By supporting the installation of hybrid mini-grids system, the project will optimize renewable energy generation and reduce greenhouse gases emissions. The PSMP also aims at reducing the number of households without access to electricity, thus putting pressure on the biomass resource in the country. At the same time, access to expanded, reliable and affordable electricity is critical to enable the delivery of basic social services. It also enhances economic development by providing energy for productive activities. Agriculture remains very important in Somalia, given the predominance of rural farmers and the economy dependence on the agriculture sector.

### h. Tajikistan

In its updated NDC, Tajikistan has committed to reduce carbon emissions to 504.60 percent of the level in 1990, representing a total reduction of 7.45 percent in comparison to the business as usual scenario. Targets include the Energy, Industry, Agriculture, transport and solid waste sectors. The international target translates into an annual emission reduction of 2.2 (CO2eq) per capita by the conditions set between 1.9-3.0 (CO2eq) per capita by 2030. One of my mitigation actions with regards to the energy sector includes the extensive use of renewable sources of energy, which is in line with the National Energy Strategy (NES) and the National Renewable Energy Strategy (NRES) until 2030. The development of renewable energy sources is seen as one of the main adaptive measures needed. By supporting the installation of VRE in the power mix, the project will contribute to the NDCs and climate targets more generally speaking, which are set in the updated NDC.

### Tunisia

Tunisia has engaged in an energy transition policy aimed at achieving an efficient, diversified energy system, reducing dependence on fossil fuels and meeting the requirements of the economic development. The country is committed to a 45 percent reduction in carbon emissions by opting for clean energy solutions. Tunisia ratified the Paris Agreement and committed to its NDC to reduce the carbon intensity of its economy by 45 percent in 2030 compared to that of 2010. A reduction of 67% is expected from the energy sector, which represents the largest share of total emissions, with a target of 80% being attained, conditional on financial support from abroad, capacity building and technology transfer. Three third parties have been identified as potential sources of funding: the World Bank, the EU and the African Development Bank (AfDB). According to the updated NDC, around 80 percent of financing needs for mitigation are attributed to the energy sector, placing renewable energy and energy efficiency at the center of climate targets. Also, Tunisia has adopted a National Energy Strategy (NES) and a National Renewable Energy Strategy de développement des énergies renouvelables à horizon 2050 (Projet TUNIREP), highlighting the importance of this sector for Tunisia's climate strategy.

In addition, under each SREP Project, EEs will be required to ensure that stakeholders are provided with timely and accurate information on project implementation, and will be consulted in a culturally appropriate manner, which is free of manipulation, interference, coercion, discrimination and intimidation. Stakeholder engagement will take into consideration various factors which may inhibit

participation of women, gender inequality, literacy, ethnicity, disability, and other exclusion factors amongst vulnerable groups. Hence, consultations will be targeted to ensure a tailored engagement approach.

Environment and social risks and benefits generated and/or associated with the Projects will be considered in the environmental and social assessment (ESAS) process, as well as during project design, in consultation with the initial stakeholders, including vulnerable groups.


The engagement with stakeholders will begin as early as possible to gather initial views on the proposed projects. This will continue throughout each Project's life cycle. Throughout the engagement process, stakeholders are encouraged to provide feedback on all activities and on the identification and mitigation of environmental and social risks and impacts. A document summarizing stakeholder consultation will be prepared for inclusion in the ESAS. The environmental and social assessment, including a description of the stakeholders consulted, a summary of the feedback received and a brief explanation of how the feedback was taken into account, or the reasons why it was not.


Each project will have its own Project SEP Pat will be developed and owned by the EE, in accordance with ES103. The plan will be publicly disclosed by the EE and will be updated as needed. The stakeholder engagement process will be documented.

The engagement process will use any approach that removes obstacles to participation, including differentiated measures to allow the effective participation of those identified as disadvantaged or vulnerable groups. This will include providing information in a format accessible to such groups with such different affected groups so that they can obtain the information they need regarding the issues that will potentially affect them, including how their view will be captured. Project stakeholders will be provided with sufficient time and opportunity for consultation modalities and/or approaches and retain the rights to refuse participation despite such options.


Different methods of communication are used to: (i) ensure easy, transparent, direct, open and interactive communication with all stakeholders; and (ii) get feedback in the implementation process. The Project SEP will include a description of how these methods have been used during project design and stakeholder participation during Project implementation; and (b) transparent feedback and grievance mechanisms for addressing issues and concerns raised by stakeholders. As far as possible to allow for stakeholder participation and their early feedback to be fully integrated as part of the overall Project design and implementation. Project communication and stakeholder engagement will follow into project implementation.


The methods vary according to the target groups such as but not limited to:

* Regular coordination meetings with relevant government agencies – central, provincial and district;
* Public information dissemination and disclosure;
* Interviews and consultations of local communities, facility administrators, relevant government agencies and organizations;
* Public consultations (at provincial, district, sub-district), workshop, and/or focus group discussion (FGD);
* Survey and questionnaire.


Each EE will develop its own engagement program following the outline approach based on their stakeholder analysis, and include it in the Project SEP. The line and venue of any proposed public consultation meetings be a process by which meetings will be notified, communicated, and reported.

# D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately one page)

## Cost-effectiveness

The GCF plays a catalytic role to move VRE at scale through the comprehensive and integrated risk mitigation framework promoted under SRM by: (i) financing the critical public investments needed in an affordable manner and such by reducing the burden for the countries in particular in the context of the GCF; (ii) providing concessional funding for public investments that are key enablers to meet the needs of the private sector through the availability of this concessional financing (e.g. solar/wind park infrastructure to mitigate the development risk); (iii) adapting to the specific needs of countries that need investment in their energy sectors; and (iv) providing a platform for public-private partnerships with the GCF, i.e., to unlock large-scale mini-grid businesses; and (v) unlocking scalable and bankable projects under SRM at scale.

As described in BS6, the concessionality of GCF financing will be critical to ensure that the countries are to pre-emptively address the challenges associated with the high cost of solar and wind power generation. The cost of the public loans is a crucial parameter in the political decision to implement or not a more sustainable path. The GCF additionally is therefore paramount to a successful result. To change the course of a given government, it will be necessary requesting financial support through their NERDs as part of our ongoing dialogue. Therefore, the SRM Facility is requesting the highest level of concessionality for the mini countries that are part of the Program.

The financial structure is a mix between grants for technical assistance; concessional loans for critical public infrastructure – with the exception of Guinea, Bissau, Somalia and Tajikistan which will get full grants due to their low capacity to absorb concessional financing; and equity investments. The GCF has three main goals in order to achieve the Program’s objective to support countries on their low-emission pathways: (i) grants for key enabling projects that are to be targeted at specific right targets, i.e., are set and implemented fully within the government(s); (ii) concessional financing for public investments which are opportunities in increasing their penetration of VRE and improve grid reliability and resilience; and (iii) risk mitigation instruments which are enabling the mobilization of scale private investments. These are critical especially in countries where the counter-factual is cheap coal production such as in Indonesia, Tajikistan, Kyrgyzstan and Mongolia.

The Facility aims to unlock 2,160 MWh of RE. The expected GHG generation reduction is 55.017 million tons CO2eq for the generation built during the project lifetime which represents an overall GCF dollar per GHG emission reduced of USD 2.93 and USD 20.43+ compared to the total public investments.

## Co-financing, leveraging and long-term investments

The USD 500 million GCF will be complemented by an equivalent USD 1 billion in World Bank financing which it turns is expected to unlock USD 6 billion in private investments in the medium term. The indicative leveraging between public investments (World Bank and GCF financing) and private investments

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 PAGE 80

The private investments are a result of the project’s public investments and therefore, are not considered as part of the co-financing and the Funded Activity.

#### Financial viability

GCF concessional financing will be critical to improve the economic and financial rate of returns of the projects. It is estimated that the GCF would subsidize any included in the program and up to what data is available, the GCF financing increases the financial IRR on average by around 1 percent but more importantly makes possible those projects that would not be accepted by the countries without the highly concessional climate financing support. GCF financing is a game changer for the governments as because it is concessional countries which would otherwise select polluting energy would be incentivized to join the program.

#### Application of best practices

To ensure that the best available technologies are considered, the government will hire transaction advisors that will develop the request for proposals to IPPs and ensure that the IPPs only select Tier 1 technologies. The best practice with regards to selection of IPPs will also be implemented to ensure financial sustainability of all the contracts and develop a track record within the countries in selection of IPPs.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Besides the arrangements laid out in the AMA between the World Bank and the GCF, the implementation of the SRMI Facility involves (i) monitoring of program performance indicators (as per Section E.5); (ii) periodic progress reports; and (iii) midterm review of implementation progress.

It is noted that the end targets of the Programme are best estimates as it is not known upfront exactly the results of each project that will be financed, and the impact on the environment and social dimensions may vary from project to project. The SRMI Facility will monitor and report on the implementation status at both Facility and country level by aggregating specific country-level information and data.

### Monitoring

Specifically, the implementation of each project under the SRMI Facility will be managed and monitored at project and Facility level by World Bank’s in-house staff with inputs from, as applicable, Project Implementation Units (which may include staff and consultants). The World Bank has dedicated staff in its energy team, operation departments, Environment and Social departments and country offices that will conduct due diligence, sector compliance and performance risks, implementation of the Environmental and Social Compliance Plans and the stakeholder engagement plans.

The World Bank will follow its standard monitoring and reporting policies and procedures, which will include semi-annual reports on the implementation status and performance of each project. (By the end of each project supported by the SRMI Facility, the Bank will conduct jointly with each implementing agency a Joint Evaluation of Project Implementation and an Operationalization completion report (OCR) which will review the performance of the project, assess effectiveness and efficiency of project implementation, the achievement of the project development objective, and provide relevant lessons learned. Financial reporting is further discussed in Section G.3.

### Reporting

Reporting of EEs to World Bank: As specified in the subsidiary agreements between the EEs and the World Bank, the EEs are obliged to report on the implementation of the Project and the use of proceeds of the Facility in accordance with the terms and conditions of the subsidiary agreements, including on the environmental and social performance of the project and the achievement of the indicators agreed between the AEE and the EE. It is to the World Bank’s satisfaction that the EEs will be responsible for overall M&E of the project, including collection of project performance information and reporting on project impacts and results.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Political and Governance: As Guinea Bissau and Somalia, are facing complex political situations, it may impact their political commitment to the projects and the governance aspects of the implementation of the components:

- **Guinea Bissau**: Guinea-Bissau has a history of political and institutional fragility dating back to its independence from Portugal in 1974. The country is one of the most coup-prone, and politically unstable countries in the world. Since independence, there have been 20 unsuccessful coups recorded in Guinea Bissau, with another 16 attempts, attempted, plotted, or alleged. Held in December 2019, the last Presidential election was marred by allegations of fraud and irregularities, which led to a Supreme Court decision that the results were invalid. Simões Pereira, alleging evidence of fraud and irregularities, filed a motion to challenge the election results which decision by the Supreme Court is still pending. In April 2020, ECOVAS, followed by the European Union, suspended aid to Guinea Bissau in response to the Supreme Court's ruling, and called for the appointment of a new Prime Minister and government based on the results of the legislative elections of March 10, 2019. Outcomes of these legislative elections indicate a contributory role played by the ECOWAS mediation committee. Following the extraordinary session held on June 29, 2020, a new majority seems to be in place after the approval of the de facto Government's program.

- **Somalia**: A sustained period of political and institutional fragility reflects a country transitioning out of fragile statehood into crises. The 2011 Provisional Constitution, the 2012 establishment of the federal government, and the subsequent formation of four new Federal Member States are re-drawing Somalia's new federal map and creating the space for a political settlement. Upcoming elections in 2023 will be critical to determine whether Somalia can move forward with its new model for the 2020/21 period. Although key constitutional questions remain open, federalism offers a new means of governance that could help address some of the country's most pressing challenges while the more stable northern regions and those in the south still emerging from conflict. Somalia’s private sector remains a source of resilience and innovation and political relations in the Horn of Africa and Red Sea region are increasingly important for Somalia to benefit from its proximity to the Ethiopian market for trade and regional integration.

Macromacroeconomic: This risk category includes external and domestic macroeconomic risks that may derail the achievement of operations development results during either preparation or implementation. Overall, the net foreign exchange reserves in 2018 were estimated at $3 billion in Guinea Bissau (around 1 and 3 percent in Tunisia, Somalia, between 4 and 5 percent in Guinea Bissau, Kyrgyzstan, Mongolia, Seychelles and above 7 percent in Ethiopia and Tajikistan. Their inflation levels are aligned with their GDP growth levels with rates in 2018 between 0 to 5 percent. However, it is important to note that it is currently


---
**Page 107**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 103

unknown because the COVID-19 crisis impacted their economies with very large gaps between countries such as Seychelles that is highly dependent on tourism and Mongolia that managed quite well with the crisis.

**Sector Strategies and Policies:** It is critical to have in place the right regulatory frameworks (i). Indonesia, Tunisia, Mongolia are working on new legislation to implement competitive selection of IPPs. (ii) Somalia does not have the full policy in place to enable privatised-open mini-grid operations, and(ii) Guinea Bissau, Kyrgyzstan, Tajikistan, and Vietnam have weak institutional capacity for implementation of projects linked to it.

**Mitigation Measure(s):** The World Bank team is working closely with the different Governments to ensure the sustainability and relevance of the projects in each given country. In general, good governance principles of the projects will be ensured by the adoption of transparency, accountability, and good governance principles and attention to fraud, corruption and other unethical practices resulting from government failures. These will be enabled thanks to the World Bank fiduciary requirements and M&E systems.

The projects are expected to strengthen the country's macro-fiscal situations as through the deployment of large-scale renewable energy projects they will reduce the cost of imported fossil fuel while reducing energy security risks and improving national capacity.

In addition, the World Bank is working with the nine countries to ensure that a comprehensive regulatory framework is in place when the new investments come in play aiming to ensure the sustainability of the new generation projects.

**Selected Risk Factor 2**

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description:** Technical Design of projects. As solar and wind installation are well known technologies and there are clear investments related to VRE integration, the technical design risk is minimal. In addition, with a clear and low risk allocation between the private and the public stakeholders, IPPs projects are least cost compared to other renewable energy projects.

**Institutional Capacity for Implementation and Sustainability:** The institutional capacity of the implementing agencies, implementation arrangements, and M&E arrangements is quite different from one country to the next. Indeed, Ethiopia, Indonesia, Mongolia and Tunisia have strong implementing agencies whereas Guinea Bissau, Kyrgyzstan, Seychelles, Tajikistan have weaker institutional capacity.

**Risk of RE Projects on Materialization:** Most RE power plants under the Facility will be prepared thanks to public funding; however, the financing for the power plants themselves will be financed by private investors. The private investment is not covered under the Facility.

**Mitigation Measure(s):** To ensure that this risk is mitigated, the World Bank is supporting the proposed nine countries in their selection of the private investors. For the countries with limited capacity, larger capacity building components

will be provided additional external support for project implementation. A transparent and well-organized selection of private investors combined with the reduction in risks from curtailment, land & E&S perspective thanks to the park scheme will ensure that private investment is indeed leveraged as a result of the Facility's projects.

Each country has a different fiduciary risk and it is being mitigated by previous experience with each client, and World Bank procurement and financial management requirements.

GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The World Bank has a new Environmental & Social Framework (ESF) that will be used by all projects.

The ESF is divided into ten E&S standards (ESS) that are each assessed based on their impact depending on the project component and stage of implementation. In this stage, the E&S personnel are summarized below. However, each project will have its own assessment completed during project preparation phase as a part of the Environmental & Social Management Plan (ESMP). Projects that are part of the Facility will be in Category A following the GCF classification and may be classified at a high or substantial risk as per the World Bank E&S classification.

Depending on the design of the projects to be financed under Component 3, the World Bank Group Performance Standards on G.45 may apply to some of the financial activities under these projects, in accordance with the World Bank’s relevant policies and procedures.


#### ESS1: Assessment and Management of E&S Risks and Impacts

The projects are expected to bring substantial direct environmental benefits by increasing the deployment of renewable energy. Transitioning to a higher percentage of renewable energy in the electricity mix has been identified as one of the key strategies for reducing greenhouse gas emissions, air pollution, and water use.

The projects are likely to result in significant employment generation, including construction jobs, followed by a smaller number of long-term jobs associated with CSM activities. There are substantial opportunities to promote gender equality through the implementation of these projects.


#### ESS2: Labor and Working Conditions

Project workers are expected to be mobilized under all the projects. The EEs/implementing agencies will have to comply with the ESF requirements as presented in Labor Management Procedures (LMP) to be prepared for each project.

#### ESS3: Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention and Management

The downstream risks and impacts related to potential water scarcity (also considering seasonal water availability and any cumulative effects by multiple demands), the release of pollutants, waste generation, the management or disposal materials and hazardous wastes, impacts on communities, and resource use


---
**Page 110**
---

efficiency will be assessed for each project. However, most of these potential impacts are low to moderate in intensity/significance and are reversible and localized in nature, and therefore can be easily mitigated.

During the construction phase of the solar and wind parks, there may be some potential negative environmental and social impacts including, air quality deterioration due to dust and exhaust emissions during construction activities. These impacts require appropriate assessments and management measures to contain them:

- **ESS4: Community Health and Safety**
ES58 requirements will be taken into account in the implementation of project's activities. While the construction and operation of solar/wind power plants has risks accompanying both normal operations and potential accidents, there have to date not been any accidents of the severity that have occurred in other forms of energy production (e.g., hydropower). There is a possibility of noise emissions from the running of wind turbines and solar panels. These may cause annoyance or even nuisance etc. These impacts require appropriate assessment and management measures to contain them.

- **ESS5: Land Acquisition, Restrictions on Land Use and Involuntary Resettlement**
Land needs for solar and wind parks are substantial. Any potential land acquisition or land use restriction will be assessed and managed in a manner consistent with ES58 requirements, in order to avoid, minimize, reduce/mitigate and compensate at replacement cost land acquisition based on due diligence and plans prepared in accordance with ES58.

- **ESS6: Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources**
The proposed solar/wind projects, if not properly designed and sited, may result in loss, degradation or fragmentation of natural habitats and other impacts on biodiversity and living natural resources. Care will be taken to ensure that the impacts of such projects on natural habitats, terrestrial and aquatic ecosystems and species are well assessed and managed.

- **ESS7: Indigenous Peoples/Sub-Saharan African Historically Underserved Traditional Local Communities**
The presence of indigenous people in the project areas or their collective attachment to such areas will be separately examined by the World Bank in consultation with the national authorities. Once it is confirmed, consistency with ES57 objectives and requirements will be integrated into the design of the project and E&S documents, including the development of an indigenous peoples' development plan or other types of plans developed at the country and project context, and Free, Prior and Informed Consent in the circumstances specified in ES57.

- **ESS8: Cultural Heritage**
Each project will screen the project area and assess potential impacts on cultural heritage, both tangible and intangible. The site selection for each project will be mindful of cultural heritage and the application of ES58 will be further assessed under each project.

# ESS9: Financial Intermediaries

The projects are not expected to be financial intermediary projects.

## ESS10: Stakeholder Engagement and Information Disclosure

Project stakeholders can include communities or individuals affected by the project and their formal and informal representatives, national or local governments, religious or community organizations, indigenous peoples groups with special needs, civil societies, and others. The identification of project-affected communities or groups will also include those who may be adversely affected by the project impacts and/or more vulnerable than others in their ability to take advantage of the project benefits.

A Stakeholder Engagement Plan will be developed consistent with the project’s context and requirements concerning the project’s objectives and expected participation levels. Engagements and consultations will be conducted throughout this project cycle. This will include discussions of project design and impacts as well as multi-stakeholder discussions on these issues during the preparation phase. The SGP will be updated to reflect any changes in stakeholder engagement plans, including the contents of ESS10 and will be treated as a living document to be regularly updated as needed during project implementation.

## WB Performance Standards

This policy applies to a Bank supported activity if: (a) the activity is designed, constructed, operated and/or owned by a Private Entity (or consists of technical assistance in preparation for such activity), is productive and involves significant financial risk; or (b) the objective of the activity could be achieved only through the involvement of a private entity. In either case, the Private Entity will be solely responsible for identifying, assessing and managing the environmental and social risks associated with the activity (and in the case of an activity involving a financial intermediary, to identify, assess and manage these risks associated with the specific role played by such financial intermediary). The Bank applies the WB Performance Standards to the Bank supported project, or it may comprise part of a Bank supported project whose other parts do not constitute a Private Sector Activity.

The Bank applies the WB Performance Standards as follows: (a) if the Private Sector Activity constitutes the entire Bank supported project, the Bank applies the WB Performance Standards to the entire project; and (b) if the Private Sector Activity is only part of such project and the Private Sector Activity has “substantial influence” of such project whose other parts comprise a Non-Private Active, then the Bank applies: (i) the WB Performance Standards to the Private Sector Activity; and (ii) the WB Safeguard Policies/ESP to the rest of the project.

The Private Entity is responsible for developing an environment and social management system (ESMS) and for identifying, assessing and managing environmental and social risks and impacts associated with the Private Sector Activity.

Financial intermediaries are required to develop an ESMS acceptable to the Bank, which the intermediary applies in identifying, assessing and managing environmental and social risk and impacts under its Bank supported portfolio or subprojects.


---
**Page 112**
---

The Bank supports the Private Sector Activity, the EGMS, and environmental and social assessment and management plans prepared for activities under the EGMS for consistency with the WB Performance Standards. The Bank categorizes the activity on the basis of the nature of the potential environmental and social impacts. For example, the activity may be categorized as a project or program in accordance with country environmental studies, its country assistance or partnership strategies, sector strategies, or comparable Bank-financed projects or programs. The Bank ensures that, where applicable, the notification requirements under the World Bank Safeguards (WPDS) are met and that the environmental and social aspects of the Private Sector Activity with respect to the applicable WB Performance Standards are addressed.

### G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The Facility aims to enhance female economic participation in the energy sector and create income-generating opportunities for women in project countries. Proposed interventions under the Gender Action Plan include both:

- **a.** Equalizing between men and women through gender migration and adaptation actions.
- **b.** Mitigating gender-related risks in climate change action.

Employment opportunities created by large-scale solar and wind infrastructure projects often benefit men more than women, as women are less likely to be employed in the construction of these projects. This can at times also be limited in target countries, and specific attention to women’s connectivity will be key in ensuring men and women equally gain from energy services. Under the SRMI approach, issues related to female employment in the energy sector are addressed through gender analysis and action plans.

The Gender Action Plan is designed to ensure that women benefit equitably from the project, starting with the procurement of the RE project up to its implementation, going beyond whenever possible to also enhance livelihoods in affected areas. These issues will be addressed in a sustainable manner, raising awareness, improving gender policies, building capacity, and generating knowledge on the program’s differentiated impact on men and women.

Preliminary gender assessments for each of the nine countries under the Facility highlighted the following gender gaps and their potential mitigation actions:

- **a.** Female employment and skill gaps in the energy sector: The main barriers to women's participation in the energy sector are related to social norms, lack of access to education and care work, occupational role segregation, limited capacities and skills, as well as discriminatory work environments.
- **b.** Female employment in areas of implementation of RE projects: RE projects are frequently located in remote areas with good solar/thermal resources and large plots of land; but where living standards are below the country average. Women, if employed, are more exposed to lower-paid, lower-quality employment in the informal sector.

Under the SRMI approach, the socio-economic


---
**Page 113**
---

needs of local communities will be assessed, and actions empowering women prioritized. Those may include investments in education and skill-building, for instance in traditional home-based activities such as handicrafts, or in agricultural activities. Special attention will be paid to the promotion of local women-run businesses along the project development cycle.

### Gender-based violence: Women routinely experience sexual harassment and intimate partner violence. The risk of gender-based violence is likely to increase during the realization of large-scale RE projects. This risk will be mitigated through implementing mitigation and response approaches and the adoption of internal prevention and response mechanisms in compliance with the World Bank’s Safeguards Policy. These measures will also include awareness-raising actions empowering local women and men and aim to create a safe environment at the project site and in communities.

### Women access to services as energy consumers among project countries with low electrification rates and under-developed grids, women suffer from time-poverty and increased health-issues, as they bear the responsibility of food preparation, as well as fuel and water collection. Limited access to electricity and poor quality of service (e.g., frequent power outages) can also be explained through affordability constraints regarding electricity connection or use of energy services. Improving access to electricity services (off-grid and on-grid) may reduce the time and labor spent by women in collecting firewood, as well as improve their health status, participation and income. For these reasons, the EEs will be required to (i) pay attention to women and men’s differentiated needs in the design of electrification programs, (ii) conduct inclusive stakeholder consultations with women and men, including through participatory planning processes, (iii) undertake outreach and capacity-building initiatives to increase women's knowledge about new forms of energy, and (iv) prioritize FHI in the design of actions to assist poorer households.

The Facility will follow a systematic approach and provide cross-support to projects interventions with a focus on data collection, policy engagement, project level actions and capacity-building activities. The WBG ESWG Gender and Energy Program will be available to provide assistance to the SFGF as well as to other stakeholders such as governments, civil society organizations, employees and entrepreneurs. The Facility will encourage the recruitment of a balanced female-to-male employment ratio within the Field Office.

Obtaining stakeholder buy-in will be key in ensuring that interventions to promote women’s empowerment and improve livelihoods are effectively implemented with measurable outcomes. Countries adherence to international agreements and national frameworks normative on gender equality, will be a good starting point to strengthen government's support. At the project and program level, the following actions should be considered:

### Data collection and initial assessments:
- Build upon existing country gender diagnostics and action plans;
- Document the baseline situation and identify gaps through collection of sex-disaggregated data;
- Conduct participatory consultations involving female participants, to understand men and women differentiated needs;


---
**Page 114**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 110

Include an analysis of the differentiated impact of the SRMI project on men and women in initial assessments, through the involvement of gender and energy experts (Environmental and Social Impact Assessment, Resettlement Action Plans etc.).

## Identifying and implementing relevant actions:

- **Resettlement**: Identify and implement relevant interventions to narrow gaps in terms of employment and energy access for women and girls.
  - This includes:
    - Actions to promote female employment in the renewables sector (including capacity building and programs for women to gain hands-on experience in the energy sector such as support for women-owned businesses).
    - Capacity-building activities for women in the area of implementation of the RE projects (such as activities generating revenues).
    - Implementation of gender-sensitive measures to mitigate GGR in the area of implementation of the RE projects.
  - Facilitating access to finance for FHRs.

## M&E and Knowledge management:

- Monitor results throughout the project cycle to assess impact on men and women and allow for real-time adjustments; and
- Explore a knowledge base by documenting lessons learned from prior projects, knowledge production, and exchange.

### Strengthening female participation and voice during Stakeholders Consultations:

- Organize stakeholders' consultations throughout the project cycle. Ensure effective participation of women in these consultations to address their concerns about project preparation, and monitor impacts and allow for real-time correction during implementation.
  - Use local languages and involve female facilitators/local women leaders, as well as women representatives from the utility company whenever possible; and
- Organize separate focus groups if appropriate. These consultations and focus groups at a convenient time for women to create partnerships with women's groups and nongovernmental organizations (NGOs) to assist in the process and ensure the project’s sustainability.

## G.3 Financial management and procurement (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Every project supported under the SRMI Facility will be required to conduct financial management and procurement according to the World Bank's policies and procedures.

### Procurement

Each project supported by the SRMI Facility will follow the World Bank’s Procurement Regulations. World Bank procurement staff provide implementation support to the EEs throughout the procurement process. They work with governments to achieve the highest bidding and contract management standards to get the best development results. Each project is developed and supervised by a World Bank team that includes a procurement specialist. Each project (with the exception of guarantees)


---
**Page 115**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 111

Also is subject to the World Bank’s Anti-Corruption Guidelines, dated October 15, 2006 and revised in January 2011 as well as of July 1, 2016.

## Financial Management

Each project with the exception of the guarantees supported by the Facility is required to maintain financial management arrangements (including planning and budgeting, accounting, internal control, funds flow, financial reporting, and auditing arrangements) of the EE and other entities responsible for project implementation. The EE shall appoint a qualified person as the Financial Officer (FO), who will be an accredited Financial Management (FM) specialist who provides support during the entire project cycle (preparation, supervision and implementation) in accordance with the Bank’s operational policies and procedures. The FM specialist is also required to provide advice on financial management arrangements and to supervise and support the performance of Bank-financed operations. The team's FM specialist closely monitors the performance of the EE’s FM arrangements and their continued capacity to provide reasonable assurance that financing proceeds are being used for the purposes intended.

Financial reporting will also be required for each project and (with the exception of the guarantees) is specified in each project’s Disbursement and Financial Information Letter (DFIL). Unless otherwise agreed by the Bank, the DFIL shall specify the date on which financial statements are to be submitted at the close of the financial year and unaudited interim financial (IFR) reports periodically. Audits are carried out by auditors with independence and capacity acceptable to the Bank, under terms of reference acceptable to the World Bank.

## G.4 Disclosure of funding proposal

- ☐ No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.
- ☐ With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full and any such disclosure will be made on a need-to-know basis in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:
  - full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an explanation of the nature and extent of the confidentiality and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity’s disclosure policy; and
  - redacted copy for submission to the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

Re: Funding proposal for the GCF by World Bank regarding Sustainable Renewables Risk Mitigation Initiative (NRMI) Facility (Phase 2).

Dear Sir,

We refer to the programme NRMI Facility (Phase 2) in Guinea-Bissau as included in the funding proposal submitted by the World Bank on or after April 1, 2021.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of Ministry of the Environment and Biodiversity, the National Designated Authority/Local point of Guinea-Bissau.

Pursuant to GCF decision 8.06/15, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no-objection to the Programme as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no-objection, it is implied that:

(a) The Government of Guinea-Bissau has no objection to the Programme as included in the funding proposal;

(b) The Programme as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with Guinea-Bissau’s national policies and plans; and

(c) In accordance with the GCF's environmental and social safeguards, the Programme as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no-objection to the Programme as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

We also confirm that our no-objection applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of this programme.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Secretariat’s assessment of FP204

Proposal name: Sustainable Renewables Risk Mitigation Initiative (SRMI) Facility [Phase 2 Resilience focus] [SRMI-Resilience]

Accredited entity: World Bank

Country(ies): Ethiopia, Guinea-Bissau, Indonesia, Kyrgyzstan, Mongolia, Seychelles, Somalia, Tajikistan and Tunisia

Project/programme size: Large

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The Sustainable Renewables Risk Mitigation Initiative (SRMI) Facility provides a one-stop-shop approach, with technical assistance in building the critical public investment case and the enabling environment needed to enable private investments at scale. This initiative is particularly timely and relevant, especially when countries are prioritizing the secure supply of energy during the response to coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) (also known as SARS-CoV-2). | The SRMI Facility and the projects for investment in renewable energy and related infrastructure, including implementation arrangements, need to be harmonized with other World Bank initiatives. This will ensure that the impact is indicated in the proposal. It would be important to ensure that all countries strictly comply with the agreed set of parameters in the term sheet throughout the process. |
| GCF funding is critical to address the current renewable energy investment gap identified and ensure that countries continue on their low-carbon development pathway. | The initiative will require a differentiation of different types of markets/energy sectors, with various levels of readiness for private sector participation (e.g., large scale generation, small to large grids, and so on). The success in one country may not be replicable in other countries as enabling conditions vary across countries. |

2. The Board should consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the term sheet and addendum X, titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendations,” respectively.

## Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### Project background


---
**Page 135**
---

3. This programme is a subsequent phase of the approved FP163 submitted by the World Bank, expanding the programme to cover an additional nine countries that are greatly impacted by climate change: Ethiopia, Guinea-Bissau, Indonesia, Kyrgyzstan, Mongolia, Seychelles, Somalia, Tajikistan and Tunisia.
4. Achieving the global goals of climate change mitigation and universal access to electricity will require rapid growth in the share of variable renewable energy (VRE) used in global power generation. The International Energy Agency (IEA) World Energy Outlook 2020 projects that by 2050, VRE will account for approximately 43% of total global electricity generation. This represents an additional 770 GW of solar and 370 GW of wind over the level in 2019. As per the IEA’s analysis, the number of countries with national renewable energy targets (NRETs) across – when they have variable renewable energy (VRE) targets – are not ambitious enough to meet these targets. To achieve this objective, large amounts of private finance will have to be unlocked to complement public sector financing and support the development of VRE projects in countries facing rapidly growing populations, infrastructure, and economic systems in these countries, leading to increased adaptation costs.
5. The falling technology costs have made investment in RE commercially viable in more and more countries. Nevertheless, global investment remains below the targets needed to meet the Paris Agreement’s goals. A number of constraints are preventing private sector financing from reaching RE projects, and many of these constraints are closely related to the development of VRE projects. These include: (i) limited access to finance; (ii) the inability of utilities to organize and contract future generation, which has resulted in additions to power generation capacity that are not aligned with demand growth; (iii) lack of adequate public-private partnerships or independent power producer (IPP) regulatory frameworks that enable IPPs to enter into long-term contracts for power generation; (iv) low competitive procurement capacity to select IPPs, which has often led to structured competitive bidding; (v) limited financial and technical expertise in the design, development, financing, construction, operation, and sector investments; and (vi) grid integration challenges in supporting the uptake of more VRE because of insufficient transmission infrastructure.
6. The proposed Sustainable Renewables Risk Mitigation Initiative (SRMI) aims to address these challenges by creating an enabling environment that is more conducive to private investment in VRE projects in the nine countries, while also providing support for the countries in the context of coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19), which has increased challenges and facility. The SRMI will be implemented through a multi-year programme with identified constraints, with a one-stop-shop approach from technical assistance to financing the critical public investments needed to enable private sector investment and to enable private investment at scale. SRMI offers climate financing for four core aspects:
(a) Technical assistance to help countries develop evidence-based VRE targets, implement a consistent regulatory framework, establish an effective procurement programme, and maintain robust procurement processes with transaction advisors;
(b) Critical public investments to enable the integration of VRE, finance solar/wind park development, and support grid expansion;
(c) Risk mitigation instruments to cover residual risks perceived by private investors; and
(d) Project management at country level: management of the projects and coordination and implementation.
7. The SRMI Resilience programme will cover nine countries, namely Ethiopia, Guinea-Bissau, Indonesia, Kyrgyzstan, Mongolia, Seychelles, Somalia, Tajikistan and Tunisia. The nine countries were selected based on their willingness and interest of their respective governments

The project is being implemented with a total cost of USD 959 million in concessional finance from the accredited entity (AE). On top of AE co-financing, the AE projects are expected to mobilize an estimated USD 75 million of private capital to implement the RE projects downstream. The majority of GCF concessional financing (USD 642 million out of USD 690 million) will be developed in Ethiopia and Guinea-Bissau for the purpose of supporting the integration and unlocking private sector finance for the RE plants that could be made commercial. This constitutes an incremental cost at system level when investing in RE, leading to a tough political and economic decision when a government sets priorities. The concessional finance provided by GCF is less than 10% of the total project cost, which has been challenging and have become fragile, and would thus prioritize public expenditure on the health and education sectors.

In addition to the concessional financing, the AE proposes to put in place a technical assistance grant of USD 37 million, out of which GCF has been requested to provide USD 31 million. This will be used for the following purposes: (i) to build institutional capacity of countries for energy planning; and (ii) to put in place the legal framework for IPP

## Component-by-component analysis

### Component 1: Technical assistance (total cost: USD 37 million; GCF cost: USD 31 million)

#### 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

Component 1 (technical assistance) focuses on the country/macro-level risks and aims at supporting countries in transforming their strategy for low-emission development, allowing them to proceed with the implementation of the Programme.

The first step is to sound a deployment in it to set a sustainable long-term target for renewables based on concrete analysis of demand, available resources and grid feasibility and stability. Subcomponent 1.0 therefore aims at supporting countries in developing their renewable RE capacity in the respective countries. Subcomponent 1.1 is therefore designed to support the nine countries in revising their generation plans and identifying the critical investment needs


---
**Page 138**
---

For large-scale VRE integration while maintaining grid reliability and resilience. The targets will then be integrated into revisions of the NDC to ensure that they are implementable.

As an important aspect of the technical assistance, the SRMI will also identify all the legal changes that need to be implemented to attract private investments, and how the domestic financial sector can be mobilized to finance projects in order to reduce the foreign exchange risk to the utility. Subcomponent 1.2 will therefore focus on the legal framework for VRE integration and the development of a competitive bidding scheme to implement the competitive and transparent tendering of privately financed PV projects.

Complementary to setting out the legal framework for IPPs and the competitive bidding scheme, this component will also support the government in preparing feasibility studies and environmental and social risk mitigation instruments in support of the deployment of VRE. The SRMI will provide technical assistance on feasibility studies may include geophysical analysis of the land around the substation, topography and geomorphology, soil mechanics, hydrogeological, meteorological/wind measurement analyses, all of which will be made available for bidders.

**Component 2: Public investments (total cost: USD 996 million; GCF contribution: USD 79.5 million)**

This public investment component focuses on the grid- and infrastructure-level risk.

### Infrastructure Projects

Solar and/or wind park common/Shared infrastructure, ranging from the evacuation line to basic elements such as fences, roads and street lighting. The SRMI Facility will use the subnational planning process to identify priority sites for the deployment of renewables. The government will identify project sites, conduct land ownership surveys, and prepare a detailed site selection report for each park. The government will also conduct the environmental and social assessments as well as the geotechnical studies. Once the solar/wind parks are ready for auction, the bidding process will be conducted in accordance with the tendering guidelines for design, construction and operation of the RE projects. This approach can help achieve a fully cost-efficient project delivery through the integration of management of renewable land ownership issues.

### VRE Integration grid upgrades (including publicly owned transmission assets and substation infrastructure)

Deploying renewables will require the significant expansion and modernization of electricity grids. The programme will support grid reinforcements through the development of a national grid plan, which includes new transmission lines and capacity enhancements with and with regard to infrastructure made resilient to climate-induced extreme weather events. This will include technologies such as capacitor banks and other reactive power compensation, together with flexible alternatives for demand response and energy storage systems that are suitable for efficient grid operation, such as monitoring systems, demand and production forecasting systems, and automatic generation control systems for generation units and grid operations. Much of the investment in grid infrastructure is expected to be directed at the impacts of climate change, which is expected to increase the incidence and severity of extreme weather events. This will include investments in resilient transmission infrastructure under greater strain. The majority of GCF finance will be devoted to this activity, as it represents a critical enabling condition for VRE deployment. (i) constitute an incremental system-level requirement that is not covered by IPPs and thus poses a significant risk.

Electrification to increase resilience of population. This subcomponent aims to invest in solar home systems, mini grids and grid connection, providing access to 640,000 households across Gunaie-Bissau, Indonesia and Somalia, which, as a result of climate

change, can expect to have increasing cooling needs as well as needs for enhanced management of water and food systems in general, for which electricity services will be paramount. This subcomponent is critical to enable public investments in clean energy mini-grids in connection to the grid, which will be enhanced by the other interventions under the programme.

**Component 3: Risk mitigation (total cost: USD $8 million; GCF cost: USD 60 million)**

25. This component focuses on the residual risks for the private sector (excluding risk related to the project’s own financial structure) and aims at mobilizing public and commercial capital for investment in renewables through (i) reimbursable grants in Somalia; (ii) the purchase of a portion of the output from the mini-grid power purchase agreement mechanism in Ethiopia; and (iii) guarantees in Guinea-Bissau, Mongolia and Tajikistan.

### 2.2.1 Reimbursable grant (Somalia and Ethiopia)

22. In Somalia, the project aims to support the deployment of large private mini-grids, and the reimbursable grant will be used as a risk mitigation tool for demand risks for mini-grid IPPs is covered and thus allows for more risk-averse private investments to be leveraged while reducing the out-of-cost risk. This innovative instrument was developed under the Phase 1 SSMG Facility in Somalia and has been successfully implemented by the Ministry of Energy. In this implementation it will be included in the implementation of the Somali mini-grid tender.

The reimbursable grant will be provided as a loan to the selected IPPs, repayable over the initial years of operation. It is expected that this risk mitigation instrument will be made available as part of the tender for competitive bidding to select large scale mini grid developers. Bidders will be required to demonstrate their ability to manage the project and their ability to pay and financial flows, and demonstrate how the expected returns from targeted clients will be sufficient to cover the reimbursable grant. The business plans of the pre-selected bidders will be audited and agreed in advance with the AE to ensure that the risk mitigation instrument is used appropriately. If the IPP fails to achieve a minimum revenue level, the reimbursable grant (from the special purpose vehicle) will be triggered. This will be repaid by the IPP over time as it generates revenues from the project but will also reduce the risk premium, ultimately ensuring a more affordable tariff in Somalia.

24. In Ethiopia, foreign exchange availability is limited and is critical to the bankability of projects that require large amounts of foreign currency (e.g., USD 10 million to USD 30 million). The project will establish a foreign exchange liquidity support mechanism to partially mitigate the risk associated with the need for large amounts of foreign currency. This will be in currencies for select RE IPP projects in the relevant Host Country (Ethiopia), reimbursable grant to mitigate the critical risk of timely availability and convertibility of foreign exchange to support project implementation. The establishment of a dedicated foreign exchange liquidity support mechanism for energy sector public-private partnership (PPP) projects is innovative, as it will provide a more reliable source of liquidity on a timely basis in critical to mobilize private capital and provide the comfort level necessary in the long-term for RE IPPs. The establishment of this foreign exchange liquidity support mechanism would thus provide the needed foreign exchange support to RE projects and/or the project facility. This will be provided by a government’s commitment to replenish the Facility each time it is withdrawn.
