### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

As a mitigation project, the proposal estimates 55 MCO2-eq in GHG emission reductions in the target countries as a result of a 2.16 GW of RE built during the project implementation. These impact figures exclude the indirect GHG emission reductions resulting from additional RE enabled by the project and the avoided emissions associated with the generation facilities that are outside the scope of the GCF project.

Adaptation benefits resulting from the programme include the infrastructure's increased resilience to climate change, which will enable it to provide electricity to an additional 3.2 million people in areas where the population is under severe from droughts and floods.

The programme’s impact potential in the long term is notable. Impacts resulting from direct and indirect investments under the Facility are further broken down in the proposal, and therefore not included here. It should be noted that the GCF has not yet decided on how to attribute at this moment if any of the GHG emission reductions achieved by the programme will be converted into carbon credits or other financial instruments.

In the absence of a universally acceptable methodology on attribution, it is agreeable to the AE that in case any EE seeks to convert into carbon credits any GHG emission reductions achieved by the Facility, the GCF will continue to explore possible ways to deal with this matter for future transactions.


#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

The key pillar of the programme's paradigm shift potential lies in the comprehensive approach taken to (i) mitigate the risks identified in the renewable development sector in developing countries; and (ii) design power systems that can sustain climate change through


---
**Page 141**
---

## Sustainable development potential

### Scale: High

At the country level, the implementation of the SBMI will keep countries from becoming locked into long-term high-emission development pathways. Deploying domestic renewables will reduce the need for fuel imports and the potential economic impact of fuel cost fluctuations. It will increase energy security and reduce exposure to volatile international fuel prices while improving their energy independence while reducing price volatility. Clean grid-connected RE parks, RE mini-grids, and decentralized RE systems can provide considerable environmental and health benefits thanks to reduced air and water pollution.

In areas with land degradation, climate change will be less disruptive for people who directly depend on the land for food security, income generation, and have limited adaptation options, including women and youth. Land degradation reduces land productivity and increases vulnerability of rural communities in already fragile ecosystems and regions. Land degradation and climate change act as threat multipliers for already precarious


---
**Page 142**
---

## Needs of the recipient: Scale: High

Although the nine countries have different economic development levels, they are all experiencing dramatic tensions in their budgets due to the COVID-19 crisis. This means they are likely to face budget constraints for the next few years and will need to make difficult choices about how to allocate resources.

## Country ownership: Scale: High

The RR reports that the nine countries targeted under the Facility in this proposal were selected based on the willingness and interest of their respective governments to embrace RE deployment and continue to pursue ambitious GHG emission reduction objectives as part of their NDCs and their current energy policy in place. The programme aims to help the countries achieve their NDCs by providing technical assistance, supporting the countries' NDCs and increasing ambitions in the coming years through the technical assistance offered by the RR. This will be achieved by assisting with the development of national renewable generation plans and identifying the critical investment needed to integrate VRE into their grids while implementing the necessary reforms. The RR also notes that this programme is a revision of the NDC that will guide the countries' mitigation and adaptation actions in the following phase.

In each country, the World Bank has extensive engagement with relevant stakeholders, and the funding proposal package contains a solid stakeholder engagement plan to ensure continued engaging stakeholders throughout the project cycle.


---
**Page 143**
---

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

Scale: Medium to high

The proposed GCF contribution of USD 100 million will be complemented by an expected additional contribution of public funds from the World Bank and other donors through GSMAP, which in turn is expected to leverage USD 1.8 billion of private finance during the project implementation. The financial structure is a mix of grants for technical assistance, concessional loans for infrastructure development, and equity investments (with a preference for public investments because of the financial and economic situation), and risk mitigation instruments such as guarantees, insurance, and co-financing (for example, through the GCF Fund, IDA, and guarantees for Guinea-Bissau and Tajikistan). Each instrument is designed to address specific types of risks. The project will contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) by supporting the sustainable low-emission pathways. The expected GHG emission reductions to be achieved during project implementation are 10 million tonnes of CO2 equivalent per year, which represents a reduction of 3/4th of one carbon dioxide equivalent for GCF, which is a low abatement cost.

The AIF also presented country-specific economic and financial analyses. The economic and financial analysis shows that the proposed project activities will generate positive net present value (NPV) for activities under components 2.1 and 2.2 realized in financial internal rates of return (IRR) of 16 per cent (Tanzania), 30 per cent (Guinea-Bissau), 45 per cent (Mozambique), and 78 per cent (Tunisia). However, it is worth pointing out that the financial analyses show that the GCF concessional financing increases the financial IRR on average by around 1 per cent and more than doubles the NPV for activities under component 2.2 of the project investment.

The Secretariat considers the programme's efficiency and effectiveness to be "High".

### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

Facility brief: Phase 2 of the GSMF Facility, which was approved in March 2021 by the Board, aims to provide technical and financial support for resilient infrastructure in the nine target countries. The project will finance investments in water supply and sanitation, transmission and distribution lines, including substations, solar PV parks and battery storage facilities (including mini-grids), as well as energy efficiency measures such as LED lighting and insulation of buildings, as well as geothermal energy and their associated facilities. Among the key environmental co-benefits are the reduction in greenhouse gas emissions from fossil fuels and the substitution of fossil fuels with renewable energy sources. The project will also contribute to reducing air pollution by substituting fossil fuel-based power generation with renewable energy sources.

Each subproject will go through the AE's subproject cycle process individually, including safeguards review and the appraisal and approval process.

- Impact: This project falls under the GCF EASD category. The Facility is proposed as category A by the AE. The Secretariat confirms this categorization and that this fits within the AE’s EAS risk accreditation framework. The environmental risks associated with the programme include those related to community safety, climate change and other transboundary or global risks; impacts and threats to the protection, conservation, management of natural resources such as forests and wildlife; and the use of living natural resources, such as fisheries and forests. Among the social risks and impacts includes those related to land tenure and access to land; displacement and resettlement; conflict crime or violence; subproject impacts falling disproportionately on individuals or groups with low adaptive capacity (e.g., women, children, elderly); and any form of possible prejudice or discrimination toward individuals or groups in providing access to development resources and benefits, particularly in the case of those who may be disadvantaged or marginalized.


---
**Page 144**
---

### Safeguards facility

The Facility will cover nine countries, which would have their own safeguards instruments.

### World Bank Group Environmental and Social Safeguards

The World Bank Group ESS requires subprojects to comply with its Environmental and Social Standards (ESS) as well as the Stakeholder Engagement and Grievance Redress Mechanisms requirements. The following paragraph provides an overview of how the ESS will be applied in the context of the project, including the relevant safeguards instruments for each subproject level.

#### ESS 1 (Assessment and management of environmental risks and impacts)

The ESS discusses the need for substantial direct environmental and social benefits as regards GHG emissions reduction, improved air and water quality, more efficient use of water resources, as well as creation of employment opportunities. The assessment will cover land acquisition, infrastructure investments to support the development of RE parks (such as solar, wind and geothermal), transmission lines, security arrangements to protect such infrastructure, and the exploratory and production drilling of geothermal wells that could entail adverse eHS risks and impacts. To ensure the identification of potential environmental and social risks and impacts for each of the subprojects to be funded under the Facility, the subproject will be subjected to a risk screening and mitigation plan preparation phase. The ESS assessment (including an review, a social conflict analysis, and a gender-based violence analysis) will identify and assess the potential adverse eHS risks and impacts identified under the AEs standards and requirements. The assessments and management of environmental and social risks and impacts will be carried out in accordance with country-specific content in terms of legal and institutional frameworks, capacities, as well as the local country context.

#### ESS 2 (Labour and working conditions)

The subprojects to be funded by the Facility are expected to comply with the World Bank Group’s Labour Management Procedure (LMP). The E&S assessments for these subprojects will include issues relating to labour and working conditions, as well as possible violations of workers’ rights, child labour exploitation, discrimination, harassment, and gender-based violence. Aside from having an environmental and social management plan (ESMP), the subproject will also be required to have a Labour Management Plan (LMP) in place, as per the World Bank Group’s environmental, health and safety guidelines, and a Labour Management Procedure (LMP). This will also require the establishment of a dedicated grievance


---
**Page 145**
---

### ESS 3 (Resource efficiency and pollution prevention)

The Facility is expected to contribute to resource efficiency through the use of resources in terms of adopting RE sources and generating GHG reduction benefits. Moreover, energy efficiency technologies and processes as well as waste minimization measures will be applied at all stages of project implementation for all subprojects. The EAS assessment and management plans will also cover the downstream risks and impacts associated with the construction and operation of the infrastructure on water supplies, noise and air emissions; soil pollution; and waste generation, including management and disposal of potentially hazardous waste materials.

### 10.5 (Land acquisition and resettlement)

The infrastructure to be funded under the Facility may require the acquisition of land and could result in the involuntary resettlement of communities or individuals. The acquisition of land for the construction of the infrastructure may require large tracts of land. Any potential land acquisition or land-use restrictions will be assessed as part of the EAS assessment, and appropriate mitigation measures will be developed in order to avoid, minimize, mitigate and compensate at replacement cost the land to be acquired. An indicative Resettlement Policy Framework and outline for the preparation of a resettlement action plan (RAP) are provided in this document. The indicative Resettlement Policy Framework serves as a guide to the extent that subject-related land acquisition or restrictions on land use will impact communities, including indigenous peoples, and how these impacts can be mitigated. Economic displacement (i.e. loss of land, assets or access to assets), leading to loss of income sources for individuals and households is also considered.

### ESS 6 (Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources)

High voltage transmission lines, solar PV parks, onshore wind as well as geothermal facilities may have adverse impacts on the conservation status of species and the preservation of natural habitats and cause other adverse impacts on biodiversity and living natural resources. Care will be taken to avoid or minimize these impacts by ensuring that all terrestrial and aquatic ecosystems and species are well assessed and managed. All subprojects will include a biodiversity action plan.

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy & ESS 7 (Indigenous peoples)

The presence of indigenous peoples in the project areas or their collective attachment to such areas will be screened as part of the EAS assessment, and appropriate mitigation measures developed. Once it is confirmed, consistency with ESS 7 objectives and requirements will be integrated into the design of the project. The project will also include a consultation process with Indigenous Peoples' plan or other types of plans depending on the country and project context, and free, prior and informed consent in the circumstances specified in ESS 7. An Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework (IPPF) will be developed to ensure that all projects funded by the Facility respect and implement Indigenous Peoples Plans consistent with the requirements of the Indigenous


---
**Page 146**
---

### Gender policy

The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan, and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.

The AE has provided information on the existence of an enabling environment for all micro-enterprises to participate in the project, including economic equality and equal social development. An initial review of each country's economic and social development reveals the differences in levels of development in each of the countries, in terms of addressing gender-related issues. The GCF Gender Policy recognizes that there are significant differences in implementation of gender-related policies and frameworks that each of the


---
**Page 147**
---

## At the national level, most of the countries (with the exception of Somalia) are signatories to the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action, as well as the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action. In addition, most have also had constitutional provisions on gender equality or women's rights. However, progress towards gender equality, with the exception of Seychelles, where constitution does not explicitly acknowledge the principle of gender equality, is slow. The reason for this is that the implementation of laws is weak. While national-level commitments exist in many of the countries, customary law and discriminatory norms prevail, curbing the advances that women and girls can make in the social, economic, political and cultural spheres.

### The AFM demonstrates its experience, having worked in the energy sector in two of the targeted countries (Indonesia and Seychelles). It notes that significant progress has been made on efforts being made, but also attests that there are many challenges. It is also made clear that early lessons from the implementation of Phase I of this programme is not forthcoming, as implementation of Phase II is still ongoing. The assessment provides information for each of the countries that shows that women suffer more in the energy sector than men and that they have limited access to resources and opportunities. They are mostly responsible for household chores. Moreover, women are found to have limited employment opportunities due to their lack of education, qualifications and skills. Even in Indonesia, which has nearly closed the gender gap in education, women still lag behind men in terms of access to land, credit, technology, finance and catering jobs. Similarly, in Seychelles, where girls outperform boys, including science subjects, they are still subject to sex segregation at job and life stage gaps. Women have limited political participation and representation. There is a lack of female access and participation in energy-related decision-making. Women in all countries lack access to credit and land ownership. The prevalence of gender-based violence (GBV) and intimate partner violence (IPV) is high. This reality has been aggravated by the COVID-19 crisis, with an increase in GBV and IPV cases reported across the targeted countries. Women are also underrepresented in employment. Although more data are needed in order to assess the prevalence of GBV, studies indicate that women are disproportionately affected by GBV and IPV compared to men.

### Furthermore, the assessment indicates female-headed households (FHHS) as being significantly more likely to be economically poorer than male-headed households. FHHS are characterized by their lack of access to resources and opportunities due to their lack of control over resources. They are more dependent on unreliable sources of income for subsistence, such as remittances from family members living abroad or informal employment. The share of FHHS resulting from male workers' migration adds to the already existing burden on women in Ethiopia. 47 per cent of FHHS are in the bottom two spending quintiles, significantly higher than the 20 per cent of men who live with their families and are in the bottom two quintiles. Financial resources of FHHS impact their ability to invest in grid connectivity. However, among

### 4.3 Risks

#### 4.3.1 Overall programmatic assessment (medium-high risk)

The project aims to address the lack of sustainable and bankable pipelines for RE projects in developing countries to shift out low-emission development pathways. The project consists of three main components: (i) grants for capacity-building, generation plans, transaction support, and grid integration; (ii) technical assistance for policy reforms and grants for public investments in solar and wind park infrastructure, grid resilience, grid VRE integration, and energy storage; and (iii) financial guarantees for risk mitigation instruments for mini-grids and grid-connected RE projects.

The total project cost is USD 1.12 billion, of which USD 160 million is GCF financing. GCF co-financing will be provided by the World Bank through a loan of USD 598 million and grants of USD 447 million in loans and USD 3 million in guarantees. Co-financing is provided by several World Banks entities, including IFC, IDA, and IFAD. The total project cost is USD 1.12 billion, of which USD 160 million is GCF financing. GCF co-financing will be provided by the World Bank through a loan of USD 598 million and grants of USD 447 million in loans and USD 3 million in guarantees.

However, the project proposal under section A.5 states that the total project cost is USD 1.2 billion, with an additional USD 100 million for co-financing provided by the GCF. This leads to increased uncertainty about the impact materializing as envisaged by the AE. This also leads to increased uncertainty about the impact materializing as envisaged by the AE.

## Project viability and concessionality

The above document discusses project viability and concessionality within a broader context of financial arrangements for developing countries under various United Nations Security Council Resolutions (UNSCRs). It highlights differences in credit profiles among countries, the current limitations on financing terms due to the global economic situation, and the need for differentiated financing terms across countries. The World Bank's role is emphasized as ensuring adherence to its fiduciary principles and standards related to anti-corruption, bribery, money laundering, and counterfeiting.

### Compliance risks (unmeasured risk)

The document outlines twelve beneficiaries in a project where at least two are subject to targeted restrictive measures under UNSCRs. It references the World Bank's advice on applying its own fiduciary principles and standards related to anti-corruption, bribery, money laundering, and counterfeiting.

### Fiduciary requirements

The World Bank refers to the accreditation master agreement (AMA) and the Financial Intermediary Facility Agreement (FIFA), ensuring that the GCF Proceeds are not being used for any payments or other transactions in violation of applicable laws. It also mentions the UN Charter, Chapter VII, and the Subsidiary Agreement.

### Results monitoring and reporting

#### 4.5.1 Results monitoring and reporting

The project is a cross-cutting program focusing on the development of the infrastructure and support of investments in RE from public and private sources in nine countries in Africa and Asia. The project will focus on the development of grid-connected renewable energy (wind and solar parks) and upgrading of grid infrastructure and financial guarantees instruments that are required to ensure the sustainability of these projects. These projects aim at reducing GHG emissions by 55 MCO2e over 25 years and reach 25,700,000 beneficiaries (L2030). The project will also contribute to increasing access to reliable electricity to national grids and the improvement of the resilience of grid-connected beneficiaries.

The AIE has developed a detailed GHG emission estimation methodology, which is attached as Annex 1. The methodology is based on the IPCC guidelines for GHGs. This approach is generally transparent, conservative and aligned with the grid emission factors and methods recommended by the International Energy Agency (IEA) and the International Working Group (IWG) on Harmonization of GHG Accounting, with the exception of Ethiopia. Following discussions with the AIE after analyzing the power sector situation in the country, it was agreed that the methodology should be adapted to reflect the specific context of Ethiopia and the emission factor of an efficient diesel generator, as the impact of the project will continue to be presented against the baseline scenario.

In relation to the estimation of adaptation beneficiaries, the Secretariat is of the opinion that further assessment needs to be conducted by the AIE to clearly identify the adaptation benefits. The estimated number of people who will benefit from improved grid resilience is estimated against that particular adaptation benefit. Therefore, a condition is proposed to add to the project document (PDP) requiring the AIE to conduct an additional study on grid resilience as well as a separate condition to conduct a study to determine the baseline in relation to existing grids, which will also help to identify how specific national standards improve grid resilience.

The legal framework is aligned with the GCF integrated results management framework and generally follows the guidance provided by the Secretariat. The appropriate indicators from HBM have been selected and targets have been set accordingly. However, there are no indicators that capture the adaptation benefits of the project that will allow for the measurement of the increased resilience of infrastructure and improved adaptive capacity in the context of climate change. This is due to the lack of data from the underlying projects. Therefore, the Secretariat would like to suggest an additional condition that the AIE include additional indicators capturing other aspects of improved adaptation capacity and resilience prior to each disbursement.


---
**Page 154**
---

As this is a multi-country project covering nine states, Annex 17 has been developed and the breakdown of impact per country has been provided.

The project activities and sub-activities have also been clearly articulated. It is noteworthy, however, that a large share of the impact will be generated by activities that are enabled through the implementation of the GCF.

Annex 11 (Monitoring and Evaluation Plan) has also been completed, and there is not a clear budget allocation for monitoring activities that are linked to project indicators.

Prior to submission of the funding proposal to the Board, the Secretariat consulted with the GF additional conditions in relation to monitoring and evaluation to reflect PDD recommendations.


## 4. Legal assessment

The AMA was signed with the AE on 13 November 2017 and it became effective on 1 February 2019.

The AE has not provided a legal opinion/letter confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals necessary for its participation in the funding programme. It is recommended that, prior to submission of the funding proposal to the Board, that (a) the AE have obtained all internal approvals necessary for its participation and (b) provide a legal opinion from the AE in form and substance satisfactory to GCF confirming that all required approvals by the AE have been obtained and that it has the authority and capacity to implement the programme.

The proposed programme will be implemented in nine countries: Ethiopia, Guinea Bissau, Indonesia, Kyrgyzstan, Mongolia, Seychelles, Somalia, Tajikistan and Tunisia.

The GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities with Mongolia, which has yet not entered into force.

The GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities in the eight other countries. This necessitates that the GCF is not protected against risks of expropriation in these countries; the risks of which need to be further assessed.


### With respect to Ethiopia, the GCF provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities with the Government of the Federal Democratic Republic of Ethiopia on 20 April 2016. The agreement is under review by the Government.

With respect to Guinea Bissau, the GCF provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities with the Government of the Republic of Guinea-Bissau on 7 April 2014. The agreement is under review by the Government.


### With respect to Indonesia, the GCF provided an updated draft agreement on privileges and immunities with the background note from the Government of the Republic of Indonesia on 2 April 2019. The agreement is under review by the Government.

With respect to Kyrgyzstan, the GCF provided an updated draft agreement on privileges and immunities with the Government of the Kyrgyz Republic on 13 September 2022. The agreement is under review by the Government.


### With respect to Seychelles, the GCF provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities with the Government of the Republic of Seychelles on 7 April 2016. The agreement is under review by the Government.

With respect to Somalia, the discussion on the privileges and immunities agreement with the Government of the Federal Republic of Somalia has not yet started.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP204

## Proposal name: Sustainable Renewables Risk Mitigation Initiative (SRMI) Facility (Phase 2 Resilience focus) [SRMI-Resilience]

### Accredited entity: World Bank

### Country(ies): Ethiopia, Guinea-Bissau, Indonesia, Kyrgyzstan, Mongolia, Seychelles, Somalia, Tajikistan and Tunisia

### Project/programme size: Large

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential Scale: High

#### 1.1 Global deployment of renewable energy (RE) power generation at larger scale than we see today will be key in meeting the objectives of the Paris Agreement.

- The proportion of RE generation has increased every year since the Paris Agreement was adopted.
- The world's electricity generated by wind and solar (both considered variable renewable energy (VRE), renewable biomass and waste-to-energy) grew from 1.6% to 20.5% in 2019, up from just over 4.8% in 2010 to 12.9 per cent in 2018.
- More aggressive growth in RE generation infrastructure will be needed to meet the Paris Agreement's goals of limiting global warming and ensuring energy security is developed and developing countries and ensure that we stay below the Paris Agreement's goal.

#### 2. The Paris Agreement also called on all the signatory countries (developed and developing) to establish clear pathways for financial flows that will facilitate the lowering of greenhouse gas emissions from their economies.
- All the nine countries included in this proposed World Bank financing programme have submitted their NDCs to the UNFCCC.

#### 3. The strategies that each country will utilize to reduce national GHG emissions and adapt to the impacts of climate change are outlined within their respective NDCs.


---
**Page 157**
---

The International Energy Agency (IEA), in its World Energy Outlook 2020, concluded that developing nations who declared intentions to adopt RE generation as a means of delivering GHG emission reductions in their NDC targets did not have adequate RE generation targets or plans to achieve them. The IEA estimated that by 2030, the world will need over 1,600 gigawatts (GWt) of solar photovoltaic (PV) capacity and 650 GW of wind capacity will need to be installed by 2025 in developing countries to stay below the 2 °C limit. This amount is in addition to the 370 GWt of solar PV and 140 GWt of wind capacity already planned for installation in developing countries. This is a far cry from the intentions of these countries expressed in their NDC documents, which are often constrained by policy, regulatory, or financial barriers that will not allow the establishment of RE generation capacities to be catalyzed. These barriers include: limited access to financing; lack of adequate grid infrastructure and transmission investment in the power sector value chain (especially transmission and distribution), which has allowed for slow progress on RE integration; inadequate electricity market design; inadequate transport pricing to electricity-use consumers even if generation assets are available; inadequate regulatory frameworks that do not allow for sufficient competition among private investments; limited procurement capacity to select independent power producers (IPPs); limited financial viability of off-takers, and VRE grid integration challenges due to weak grids that are not designed for RE.

The energy sector of the nine nations where this Sustainable Renewables Risk Mitigation Initiative (SRI) is being implemented will be a key focus area. The SRI (also known as SME Resilience) will be implemented in the main contributor of GHG emissions in each of the countries. The extent of the contributions of the energy sector of the nine countries to the country’s total emissions and the funding proposed in the funding proposal submitted to GCF by the World Bank is presented in figure 1 below.

| Country | Energy Sector Greenhouse Gas Contribution |
| --- | --- |
| Seychelles | 55% |
| Afghanistan | 27% |
| Tunisia | 38% |
| Tajikistan | 40% |
| Indonesia | 27.0% |
| Mongolia | 29% |
| Guinea Bissau | 26% |
| Ethiopia | 17.0% |
| Somalia | 17.0% |

The implication of the values displayed in figure 1, as correctly pointed out in the funding proposal, is that the energy sector represents, in most of these countries, the main contributor to GHG emissions and therefore where mitigation action is likely to be where more actionable interventions will deliver a deep carbon cleaning on the country’s emissions. Hence, the development of RE generation will contribute significant GHG emission reductions in each of the nine countries.

Furthermore, adaptation needs must be met in mitigation interventions that will be taken in each of these countries. This is because climate change is known to adversely impact population, infrastructure and economic activity. Therefore, it is important for any increased adaptation actions. Many nations globally are developing adaptive capacity strengthening strategies to reduce the vulnerability of their populations and assets of people and infrastructure to adverse climate change impacts. Globally, the energy systems of many countries have already been hit by climate change. Wildfires in many countries (e.g., Greece,

# Turkije and the United States of America have caused great damage to the power infrastructure, as have cyclones (Caribbean Islands, Mozambique), droughts (Turkiye) and heat waves (e.g., France, Indonesia, United States).

* For the nine countries covered by some of the hazard risks that the countries are exposed to, including: high population density; higher populations in Ethiopia, Guinea-Bissau, Somalia and Tunisia will be greatly impacted by extreme heat waves, leading to higher needs for cooling infrastructure; water scarcity in Ethiopia, Guinea-Bissau, Somalia and Tunisia; and infrastructure will be impacted by flooding, landslides and wildfires in Ethiopia, Guinea-Bissau, Indonesia, India, Pakistan, Sri Lanka, Turkey, United States and Vietnam; droughts in Ethiopia, hydrology and coal/gas power plants cooling systems will be impacted by droughts and extreme heat waves in Ethiopia, Guinea-Bissau, Somalia and Tunisia.

## The World Bank’s Resilience Rating System approach (provided in the Annex to the funding proposal) is applied by the Air to building and tracking resilience to climate change risks through a series of indicators. These are: exposure; vulnerability; and impact which means that the anticipated severity and occurrence frequency of climate change risks can be assessed.

### Evaluation of the “resilience through project outcomes”, which means that a project’s objective is to enhance the climate resilience of the targeted sectors and beneficiaries through:

* The historical trends of climate-related hazards (flooding, landslides, cyclones, droughts, extreme temperatures, wildfires) for consideration in project design and relevant for programmatic planning.
* The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change’s Fifth Assessment Report from the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change forms the basis for the evaluation of future risks, with climate change scenarios being analyzed through an ensemble of all CMIP5 models for the RCP4.5 and RCP8.5 scenarios.

### The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) considers that the approach could be effective in enhancing resilience to climate change if the following conditions are properly established and if the implementation process is managed based on the results monitoring under the project’s framework. In addition, it should be ensured that capacity-strengthening indicators are not appropriately reflected in the funding proposal’s monitoring legal framework to monitor the results at the national levels.

* In addition to the above-mentioned goals of SGM Resilience, other goals articulated in the funding proposal include: increasing the resilience of people in each of the covered countries; ensuring that the energy sector is better prepared for climate change and policy planning readiness of governments in each of the covered countries, especially for the various upstream planning needs for RE generation/supply to continue developing sound regulatory frameworks and country-specific policies and a sound enabling environments for the development of RE generation infrastructure; and the development of

## Goals of SMI Resilience

The goals of SMI Resilience can be generally summarized as follows:

- Develop a conducive environment to leverage private investment at scale for grid-connected and off-grid RE projects;
- Implement the country's RE programmes through transparent and robust competitive tenders;
- Improve their grid capacity to integrate VRE;
- Improve their grid capacity to integrate VRE;
- Improve the grid infrastructure's climate resilience;
- Leverage private investments for RE grid-connected and off-grid projects;
- Build a resilient mix of renewable energy sources;
- Increase the resilience of the vulnerable population by providing access to affordable and modern electricity;
- Avoid GHG emissions by displacing fossil fuel power generation in the status quo ante with RE;
- Maximize the socioeconomic benefits triggered by the RE projects developed.

## Programme Structure

The programme to achieve all the goals in the nine countries is structured to fit the differences in circumstances in the countries and along the following four components:

### Component 1: Technical Assistance
- Sub-Component 1.2: Transaction advisory and capacity-building (procurement; legal);
- Sub-Component 1.3: RE park infrastructure and resilience/climate adaptation strategies;

### Component 2: Public Investments
- Sub-Component 2.1: Public investments for VRE integration, grid upgrades and resilience;
- Sub-Component 2.2: Public investments in RE park infrastructure;
- Sub-Component 2.3: Public investments for electrification for community development;

### Component 3: Risk Mitigation Instruments
The proposed financial instruments for component 3 are guarantees and reimbursable grants. Reimbursement of such grants will be made to the project developer from the proceeds of the RE generation investors. The component will mitigate the following RE investment risks and enhance financial sustainability in the following areas:


---
**Page 160**
---

(0) Sub-Component 3.1: Risk mitigation instruments for RE mini-grids and/or off-grid private investors;
(0a) Sub-Component 3.2: Risk mitigation instruments for grid-connected RE projects;
(0b) Sub-Component 3.3: Risk mitigation instruments for foreign exchange risk for grid-connected RE projects;

### Component 4: Project Management - Funds allocated for this component will be utilized for the management of the projects and coordination of the implementation activities. Two key areas that will be covered by activities under this component will include:

#### (a)
It is the view of the independent TAP that SRI Resilience has been well designed, and its implementation will be carried out in a manner consistent with the objectives articulated.
- The nine countries covered by this programme are responsible for about 8 per cent of global greenhouse gas emissions. Their climate change mitigation plans for both the past and the present are continued into the future (Business-as-usual [BAU] scenario). Without these interventions, the GEF emissions for these countries are likely to double on average over the next decade.


---
**Page 164**
---

focused on how to handle the effects of the coronavirus disease 19 (COVID-19) pandemic, which has diverted even the meager funding resources of the countries, it is likely that the Sub-component 1.3: RF park infrastructure and resilience/climate adaptation measures will not be fully funded in the nine countries, unless they are met, further resulting in GHG emissions rebounds well into the next decades with potential to more than double-GHG emissions into the 2030s and beyond. This is likely to happen if investments in renewable energy (RE) capacity expansion and resilience projects under programmes (SEMI and SEMI Resilience) provide robust opportunities to proceed along the pathways of net zero carbon emissions.

(h)

SHMI Resilience is expected to deliver the following climate change impact outcomes that will enable each of the nine composite energy sectors and hence national economies to proceed towards their respective RE targets, while also contributing to the delivery of NDC targets and contribute to the achievement of their net zero carbon futures. The impacts that will be delivered are as follows:

(i) Direct support of the implementation of about 2.16 GW of RE in the nine countries that will be made up of (a) 1.76 GW of solar PV; (b) 300 MW of onshore wind power; and (c) 80 MW of geothermal power, which will last during the lifetime of SEMI Resilience in the nine countries.

(ii) This RE capacity expansion and resilience projects will result in the displacement of many status quo ante fossil fuel generation alternatives over the 25 years of operation of the projects in the nine countries. This has been estimated to be equivalent to about 10 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2eq).

(iii) Adaptation measures can also be delivered as energy infrastructure is made more resilient and the resilience of many categories of people living in the country to the negative impacts of climate change is strengthened. Key adaptive capacity-enhancing measures include:

(iv) For countries that are rich in hydro capacities and resources (Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Mozambique, Rwanda, Tanzania, Uganda, Zambia, and Tunisia) will be greatly impacted by extreme heat waves, leading to higher needs for cooling systems. In addition, many of these countries have significant water stress issues (e.g. Rift Valley countries). These upgrades, which will be financed under SEMI Resilience and will provide resilience to the energy infrastructure in the nine countries (status quo ante baseline period) after the projects are implemented. This will make them more resilient to the effects of heat waves that are expected to increase in frequency and intensity.

(v) Low rain and droughts are also common in some of these nations (Guinea-Bissau and Somalia). Improved electricity supplies that will be delivered by the energy infrastructure under SEMI Resilience and other conditions will result in improved irrigation that will reduce the stress on the agricultural sector.

(vi) Grid infrastructure will be impacted by flooding, landslides and wildfires in Ethiopia, Guinea-Bissau, and Indonesia and Seychelles (both of which also have a very high risk of cyclones), and Somalia. The implementation


---
**Page 165**
---

plans included in the funding proposal include strategies to enhance the resilience of the power supply infrastructure to these climate risks:

(5) For small island developing States (e.g., Guinea-Bissau, Seychelles, etc.), the focus of the SRII Resilience intervention will be on enhancing the capacity of the grid to withstand extreme weather events and strengthening the adaptive capacity of the main grid as well as grid connections. The resilience of the power supply infrastructure is likely to be enhanced by climate change (mostly to floods and high winds, etc.). Households’ access to reliable power will also enhance the resilience of their livelihoods.

The adaptive intervention being integrated into SRII Resilience will: (i) directly deliver increased climate-resilient energy services; (ii) increase the share of low-emission electricity, which will, in heat wave and extreme temperature-prone countries, provide access to cooling solutions powered by electricity to improve vulnerable populations’ adaptability to climate change; (iii) strengthen the resilience of the power supply system against climate change, with an emphasis on the economic resilience of businesses that were accounted for beneficiaries, via infrastructure that is made more resilient and reliable across the nine countries.

The overall impact potential is assessed as high. SRII Resilience is expected to make a significant contribution to reducing GHG emissions in the nine countries by shifting them from low-emission electricity, which will, in heat wave and extreme temperature-prone countries, provide access to cooling solutions powered by electricity to improve vulnerable populations’ adaptability to climate change; (iii) strengthen the resilience of the power supply system against climate change, with an emphasis on the economic resilience of businesses that were accounted for beneficiaries, via infrastructure that is made more resilient and reliable across the nine countries in line with the NDC targets of each country.

### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

**Scale: High**

The nine SRII Resilience countries are currently responsible for about 5 per cent of global GHG emissions, but their per capita footprints are however expected to continue to grow as their economies grow, leading to higher-country-level GHG emission footprints especially if they do not follow low-carbon pathways. It is known that their sectoral energy mix is dominated by fossil fuels and that they have a high reliance on imported fuels from other countries. Therefore, growing the power sector, especially the power generation pathway alongside the transport and industry sectors, will be critical to decarbonizing these economies as a firm way to de-carbonize their economies. This is unlikely to happen as many barriers (technical, financial, institutional) exist in these countries that are preventing them from doing so.

It is also known that fact climate change is exacerbating the impacts of weather events and extreme events (droughts, floods, heat waves), which will continue to be a major challenge for these countries. Therefore, smart and clean energy solutions must not only deliver cost-effective benefits but must also deliver climate-adaptive resilience for both the infrastructure and the people.

It is also a known fact that installed capacities of cost-competitive solar and wind power generation technologies are already available in the nine SRII Resilience countries including: (i) limited generation and transmission planning capacity; (ii) weak and in many cases aging power value chains, which have resulted in high costs and low levels of renewable energy deployment; (iii) weak regulatory frameworks that constrain the mobilization of sustainable private investments; (iv) limited procurement and project management capacities; and (v) weak institutional capacity for O&RE grid integration strategies due to weak grids, especially in many developing countries. These


---
**Page 166**
---

constraints must be eliminated by any programme targeted at increasing the penetration of sustainable RE generation that can provide an acceptable low-carbon future in many developing countries. This is important if the key goal of the Paris Agreement* and the targets that countries have set for themselves are to be met.

25. Putting funding together to implement RE generation projects in many developing countries is a very difficult task. This is because public sector funds are very scarce as a limited amount of resources are available to support such projects. In addition, most RE generation projects are preferred politically. Secondly, private sector financing is usually also not flowing into these projects due to the high risk involved and the lack of financial returns. It should be noted that the private sector often cannot see a pathway for earning decent returns on their investment in the existing RE environment they are being called on to invest in. Therefore, programmes designed specifically to support RE generation projects need to be carefully structured so as not only to include actions to reduce these barriers, but must also be transparently implementable.

24. A potential solution is described by the World Bank in its report “Scaling up Renewable Energy” paragraphs 21 - 23. Therefore, in order for the programme presented in the funding proposal to have a paradigm-shifting quality, it should deliver solutions for these identified barriers, which will then catalyse private sector investment into RE generation projects.

(a) Public funds from the governments of the nine countries and GCF funds (concessional loans and grants), with co-financing from World Bank sources, are the key fulcrum of the funding proposal. The public funds will be used to support the development of facilities to supply clean electricity ultimately to end-use consumers. Key components of the funding proposal include:

(i) Public funds from the governments of the nine countries and GCF funds (concessional loans and grants), with co-financing from World Bank sources, are the key fulcrum of the funding proposal. The public funds will be used to support the development of facilities to supply clean electricity ultimately to end-use consumers. Key components of the funding proposal include:

(ii) Grant funding from the GCF will be utilized to develop and strengthen the capacity of regulatory bodies in the nine countries to implement these projects within a country-level playing field regulatory framework that will enhance the development of RE generation infrastructure. The World Bank will provide technical assistance for this purpose.

(iii) Grant funding from the GCF will also be utilized to develop the capacity of country-level system planners to prepare power systems plans and grid expansion plans in line with the national energy strategy. Having this as a starting point for the plan is a unique inclusion that is expected to deliver a paradigm shift.

(b) A World Bank flagship RE Resilience will be public investment that will target the integration of VRE in the grid, upgrade the grid to ensure the effective functioning of the system and provide technical assistance to countries to improve their capacity to respond to climate change impacts. This will progressively result in the accelerated deployment of functional RE generation facilities in the nine countries. Activities here will include: investments in transmission infrastructure; investments in distribution infrastructure to improve the grid's flexibility to integrate larger amounts of VRE; and investments to help countries to better manage the integration of VRE into their power systems through tools, including power system planning, grid management, strengthening of forecasting methods and market code reforms. These activities will go a long way in ensuring the success of the programme.

* The Paris Agreement sets out a global framework to avoid dangerous climate change by limiting global warming to well below 2°C and pursuing efforts to limit it to 1.5°C.

### Economic co-benefits

The programme’s primary goal is to reduce GHG emissions and increase the adaptation and resilience of power systems in each of the nine countries. Countries with baseline fossil fuel power generation systems either have domestic resources of the fossil fuel (coal and gas) that are used for power generation or imported coal and gas from other countries. The GHG emissions footprint of the countries’ sectors and power and the potential to reduce it will depend on the negative impacts of climate change, and the resources delivered will be dependent on whether primary fossil fuels that are utilized in the baseline and that will be gradually displaced into the future are domestic resources or imported. Where these resources are imported, the GHG emissions footprint of the resources delivered will be higher magnitude compared to if they are domestic resources. This is because the carbon intensity of power generation from imported coal and gas for power generation, the balance of trade of the country will therefore be improved and the impact on the economy of the nation will be reduced.

Where these resources are imported and where the resources are country-owned, economic co-benefits will be delivered not only to the country but also at an individual level from more reliable electricity supplies from the RE generation alternatives over a period of time.

COVID-19 has also severely impacted on the economies of many, if not all, of the nine countries. The governments have had to use their limited resources and already inadequate public funds available during the pre-COVID-19 period have been utilised by the government to contain the spread of COVID-19 in each country. This has negatively affected the economic growths of these nations, which have had to delay many economic programmes, including the unconditional funding of RE generation systems included in the countries’ national energy plans and the implementation of projects on the economies of the nations, thereby delivering some level of economic co-benefits to the nations and their citizens.

The up-front strategic analysis, which will be used to define the action plan that will maximize the socioeconomic benefits of the implementation of the RE generation projects of the programme in each country, will include a focus on the socio-economic impacts of the involvement of the local players on the solar/wind value chain in a competitive manner to the extent possible. The programme will also support the development of RE industries and will also support local community development in the areas of implementation of the projects through the provision of training and capacity building for women and men, with particular attention being paid to women’s empowerment, as otherwise men may benefit disproportionately from new employment opportunities across the RE value chain.

The programme will also provide for a phased personal implementation of SMIs. Resilience will spare limited public sector finance for the countries, which could be directed to other sectors such as health, education and infrastructure, etc., where these are co-benefits resulting from the successful implementation of the programme.


### Environmental co-benefits


---
**Page 169**
---

10. Significant environmental co-benefits are achieved through reduced air and water pollution and reduced noise emissions. By replacing and displacing fossil fuel transport due to the switch to RE generation, SRII Resilience will reduce air pollution from particulates and nitrogen oxides (NOx) by 75 percent in each of the nine countries targeted in this programme. These are among the world's most polluted cities. The World Health Organization estimates that 1.2 million people die prematurely from excessive exposure to fine particulate matter, which accounts for 7.6 percent of all deaths.* *The number of people immediately affected by air pollution as they tend to live and work close to its sources*8 Children and the elderly are particularly vulnerable to the adverse health effects of air pollution. The countries targeted in this programme have RE generation facilities than has a significant impact on the air quality and health of the people, thus delivering social and health co-benefits.

1.3.4 Social and health co-benefits

20. At national level in all nine countries, the significant penetration of RE power infrastructures will deliver social and health co-benefits. By displacing fossil fuel power generation facilities, air pollution from particulates and nitrogen oxides (NOx) will be reduced. This will deliver tangible health co-benefits for people living near the displaced fossil fuel power plants. The better and more reliable availability of power from the RE generation options will also have a positive impact on the quality of life in residential households, as connected consumers, especially residential households, will have a better quality of life and increased access to energy services. This will deliver tangible social co-benefits. A successfully implemented programme will deliver tangible social and health co-benefits in each of the nine target countries.

1.3.5 Generalized co-benefits

26. The World Bank is the AE for this programme, and the World Bank Group has a well-established gender strategy, which has four pillars: namely; improving human endowment; removing constraints for more and better jobs; finding solutions that help increase women's ownership of productive resources; and promoting gender equality in education among men and boys. More importantly, the programme plans strongly stated that the work that will be carried out under this project will be aligned with the World Bank Group Gender Strategy. The World Bank Group Gender Strategy is a key component of the World Bank Gender Action Plan was proposed to integrate gender mainstreaming into the approach used in programmes and projects. The World Bank Group Gender Strategy, which was included as an Annex in the funding proposal, is aligned with the World Bank Group Gender Strategy. It is therefore apt to conclude that the implementation of SRII Resilience in the nine countries will deliver co-benefits.

35. The independent TAP concludes that SRII Resilience will significantly help strengthen the delivery of each country's SDG 7 and SDG 13 goals, and deliver real and tangible economic, environmental, social, and health co-benefits in all nine countries targeted by this programme. Therefore, the sustainable development potential of the programme is ranked as High.

The absence of public funding will also constrain post-COVID-19 recovery. Public funding needs to catalyse a low-carbon future will therefore be unavailable in the absence of the programme.

### 1.4.3 Institutional needs

65. Institutional needs will be addressed in this section using grant funding to implement some of these institutional activities. Specifically, the baseline of relevant public institutions in the power sector of these countries can be described as follows: very low capacity to carry out generation planning and investment; limited experience in the development of RE projects for determining how VRE can be integrated into the grids of their country; low capacity to organize training on VRE and grid integration; limited experience with international advisory experience; and low capacity to plan the development of RE infrastructure. These weaknesses will be resolved in participating countries by SRII Resilience, which will provide a transition support for these institutions through grant funding to implement some of their activities as stated in the funding proposal and in many of the annexes reviewed by the independent technical advisory panel (ITAP). This will also build on the capacity of the local teams through a “learning-by-doing” approach. This will deliver some level of local institutional capacity building, which can then be scaled up, which may then reduce the institutional weaknesses in the baseline.

67. The various institutional needs, including those that would benefit the country as a whole and those that would directly or indirectly benefit key stakeholders (the grid), will be positively affected by the success of the project, as discussed above. In addition, successful implementation of the project will also contribute to building the capacity of the countries in the enabling environment for planning, implementing and managing RE programmes will be built into the project design. This will ensure that the institutional capacity of the people and institutions will be adequately met by the project. As a result, the independent TAP concludes that the proposed project has a high potential to meet the needs of the country and its people.

### Alignment of the project with relevant national strategies

The SSMI Resilience programme aims to deliver a much higher level of RE capacity than that shown in the baseline. This plan aligns with each country's NDC, relevant national plans.


---
**Page 173**
---

### Ethiopia

The energy sector is the fourth most important driver of overall emissions in Ethiopia (after livestock, land-use, land-use change and forestry (LULUCF), and industry) as per its NDC. The government has set a target to increase electricity generation from renewable sources by 2030. As the country industrializes, the energy sector will become increasingly important as Ethiopia faces higher urbanization rates and population growth. In addition, the share of the energy sector's GHG emissions comes from the use of biomass (unsustainable biomass) since it is used for cooking and heating in rural areas. The government aims to reduce this by 2030 through a combination of reducing the use of biomass (by 18% and 2 percent fossil fuels). Shifting from unsustainable biomass energy demand to renewable sources and electric stoves is one of the key interventions targeted by Ethiopia's NDC. This will also contribute towards achieving its target for reducing total emissions by 2030 even further. The programme goals are therefore aligned to the targets set out in the NDC.

### Guinea-Bissau

In recent years, the Government of Guinea-Bissau has undertaken a number of action plans with a view to increasing access to energy and expanding electricity generation capacity. In this context, it submitted its first NDC, which was submitted to the UNFCCC on 12 October 2021. Its NDC: Guinea-Bissau announced that it will aim for zero emissions from the energy sector by 2050 and reduce GHG emissions from the energy sector by 46 per cent by 2030 compared to its reference scenario. The energy sector and the commitment to scale up RE generation in its generation mix are aligned with the NDC.

### Indonesia

In its most recent NDC (updated in 2021), the Government of Indonesia committed to an unconditional reduction of GHG emissions by 25 per cent relative to the BAU scenario for 2030. The country also announced that it will seek international support is provided. In 2021, the government also committed to a net zero carbon emission target in its energy sector by 2060. This is one of the key objectives of the programme: to reduce the country's GHG emissions footprint and do this in the energy sector.

### Kyrgyzstan

More than 95 per cent of all emissions in Kyrgyzstan are found in the energy sector. According to the updated NDC of Kyrgyzstan, the mitigation capacity in the energy sector is expected to be achieved by increasing the share of RE generation from 10% to 23% by the modernization of energy supply system. The country intends to focus on a decrease in coal combustion, reduction of transmission and distribution losses and an increase in RE generation. It also aims at reducing the use of biomass for cooking and heating, among other things. These goals are aligned with the objectives of SMI Resilience.

### Mongolia

The Government of Mongolia has committed to achieving a 30 per cent reduction in total national GHG emissions by 2030 compared to the projected emissions under a BAU scenario for 2018-30. This has been getting more attention as a key solution to deteriorating air quality and climate change. The country is also committed to increasing its installed capacity of RE plants in Mongolia to 256 MW, including 155 MW of wind, 60 MW of solar power and 41 MW of hydroelectricity by the end of 2030. The government considers that the expansion of coal-based power generation is indispensable for meeting the country's energy demand. However, it also recognizes the need to progress in a gradual transition toward a more sustainable energy supply. These sectoral goals for Mongolia are aligned with what is expected to be accomplished by SMI Resilience.

### Seychelles

According to the updated NDC of Seychelles, the country committed to reducing its GHG emissions by 26 per cent by 2030. Thus, boosting electricity generation


---
**Page 174**
---

In all of the main mitigation actions foreseen in Seychelles' NDC. Additionally, the National Energy Policy pursues, in the long term, a target of 100 per cent of energy supply to be generated from RE sources, with a target of 15 per cent by the reached by 2030. The national energy policy and the climate change mitigation actions and resilience projects will align perfectly with the ambition of the National Energy Policy and will also increase the resilience of the power system by diversifying the mix.

The demand side management (DSM) component of the NDC foresees savings of 30 per cent by 2030 in comparison to the BAU case, totaling 5.24 Mtoe/yr. The updated NDC is in line with the DSM component and includes additional measures in the sectors: agriculture, forestry, transport and waste sector. The development of RE electricity (solar and wind), including decentralised solutions, are key parts of these country plans. These actions will be implemented through a combination of public and private sector.

Tajikistan: In its updated NDC, Tajikistan has committed to reduce carbon emissions to 60 per cent below the BAU case by 2030 in comparison with the BAU scenario, in comparison to the BAU scenario. The energy sector of the economy is one of the sectors where this reduction is expected to be achieved. One of the key mitigation actions with regard to the energy sector is the promotion and development of renewable energy, with particular focus given to solar technology. This will be achievable when SRMI Realisation Model (SRMI RM) is implemented.

It is the opinion of the independent TAP that SRMI Resilience, as proposed, is in alignment with the climate change mitigation objectives of these nine countries, given that it is a key component of the NDCs and the national energy plans of some of these countries is leading contributor to the national GHG emissions inventories.

### Executing capacity of the executing entity

As stated in the funding proposal, the EE has been carefully chosen to combine the public entity responsible for overall energy matters in the country (e.g. the ministry of finance) and the public entity responsible for overall energy matters in the country (e.g. the ministry of energy). The EE is also expected to be a key partner in the implementation function of the EE, together with the supervising ministry of energy. It was stated in the funding proposal that for the purposes of the loans and grants, the EEs would be the borrowing entity. In the case of Ethiopia, the Ethiopian Electric Power Corporation (EEPCo), acting through the Ministry of Water, Irrigation and Energy and the state utilities Ethiopia Electric Utility Company (EEUC) and Ethiopia Electric Generating Company (EEGC) are contemplated under one of the components; the EEUs would be the private sector RE project component.

The ministries of finance (in Tunisia, the Ministry of Development, Investment and International Cooperation) are responsible for managing funds from international institutions on behalf of their respective countries. The EEPCo is a public entity in Ethiopia. The ministries in the nine countries have extensive experience and can serve in the capacity of EE. The EEPCo has been selected as the executing entity to implement RE activities in the national energy sector, including the power sector. They are thus fit for the role in SRMI RM. However, it is important to note that while the EEPCo has significant RE experience, the national power utility has not been included in the EE arrangement presented in the funding proposal, but the ministries of energy are likely to seek their participation. The utility companies in some countries (e.g. Egypt) have limited RE experience and therefore have limited RE experience. This is not likely to hamper the success of the programme as the transition from public sector to private sector will provide them with skills not only to participate in the projects, but to continue to apply their skills in replication projects after the programme ends.

## Co-financing, leveraging and mobilizing long-term investment

### The USD 140 million requested from GCF will be complemented by about USD 959 million from the World Bank. The GCF funding as well as the complementary World Bank funding are expected to leverage private sector investments in the nine countries. These funds are expected to leverage private sector investments for the countries to build RE power plants, selected energy efficiency projects and grid infrastructure. The World Bank has committed USD 1.8 billion in private investments into the nine countries. This will yield an additional leveraging between public investments (World Bank and GCF financing) and private investments of about 3 to 4 times. The total amount of co-financing for this programme is USD 2,059 million as stated in the funding proposal, the leveraged private investment will only occur if the public investment is made by the World Bank and GCF. The USD 1.8 billion committed by the complementary leveraged USD 1.8 billion is not considered part of the co-financing for this programme.

### Cost efficiency

The project is expected to deliver about 55.04 million tCO2eq of GHG emission reductions over its lifetime period. The efficiency metrics that will be delivered by this project intervention were estimated as follows:

- Total project financing = USD 1,119 million
- Requested GCF amount = USD 160 million
- Expected lifetime GHG emissions reductions = 55.04 million tCO2eq

1. **INTRODUCTION**

The Sustainable Renewables Risk Mitigation Initiative (SRMI) Facility aims to support developing countries in advancing sustainable solar and wind programs and attracting private investments. The proposed Facility's objective is to enable nine countries to unlock GWh of solar and wind generation in the medium-term with financial support from the World Bank Group. These countries are Ethiopia, Kenya, Mozambique, Rwanda, Kyrgyzstan, Mongolia, Seychelles, Somalia, Tajikistan, and Tunisia. The SRMI Facility will deploy its support through three Components, namely (i) Technical Assistance, (ii) Public Investments and (iii) Risk Mitigation Instruments.

The present Gender Assessment and Action Plan provides an overview of issues affecting men and women related to the proposed SRMI Facility. The findings from this assessment will be adopted by the SRMI Facility to effectively address these challenges and ensure the investment projects contribute to promoting female development, for instance through employment in the energy sector, while enhancing safety and security. In addition, the gender action plan (GAP) are aligned with the GCF third policy objective "to contribute to reducing the gender gap of access to resources and opportunities, including financial services, and to address potential vulnerabilities and exclusions from GCF climate investments that mainstream gender equality issues."

The Facility's activities will also directly support the World Bank Group 2016-2023 Gender Strategy and its four pillars of action: Projects will include, to the extent possible and subject to funding availability, interventions to facilitate women’s access to energy services (Pillar I); promote gender equality in energy safety (Pillar I); promote proactive policies to increase female employment along the energy value chain (Pillar II); create income-generating opportunities through access to grants or access to micro-finance for women living in the areas of implementation (Pillar III); ensure that all SRMI Facility projects are gender-sensitive and informed by stakeholder consultations; and implement rigorous mechanisms to prevent and respond to gender-based violence (GBV) in project areas, thus enhancing women’s voice and agency (Pillar IV).

1 The WBG Gender Strategy has four objectives: (i) Improving Human Endowments, (ii) Removing Constraints for More and Better Jobs, (iii) Reducing Barriers to Women's Ownership and Control of Assets and (iv) Enhancing Women's Voice and Agency and Engaging Men and Boys.

# COUNTRY BACKGROUND

Despite varying economic and social development levels, the nine countries targeted by the SRM Facility face similar gender gaps and energy challenges. Some countries have improved significantly on women equality agenda such as Armenia. Tunisia is ranked 82 out of 153 countries in the World Economic Forum (WEF) 2020 Global Gender Gap Report. According to UNESCO, Ethiopia ranks 135 out of 146 countries and is among the top five most-improved countries in the overall index together with Spain, Mali, Albania, and Mexico.

Some countries have even nearly closed the gender gap on education such as Indonesia which has nearly closed the gender gap in primary education. In contrast to other countries, in Seychelles, the employment gap is particularly significant in the energy sector, where women are mostly confined to administration, sales, finance and catering activities. Seychelles is also example: Boys and girls enrollment and completion rates for primary school were similar at 98% but girls dominate secondary education economically, including in sciences, and are more represented in higher education. However, young Seychellois women are more likely to be unemployed. The country's labor market is also characterized by high occupational sex-segregation and a weak representation of women in leadership positions. In contrast, Tunisia has made significant strides, reversing the gender gap in favor of girls. In 2010, women represented 63 percent of graduates from higher education institutions compared to only 45% in 2000. The number of female students increased by 78% regularly from public universities for the academic year 2018-19. However, despite increased access to education at higher levels, women's labor force participation and employment in Tunisia remain extremely low while employed women are concentrated in lower paying jobs.

In Armenia, women have achieved near parity in basic education attainment, but women predominate in higher studies: 46.7 percent of persons were enrolled in tertiary education institutions in 2018-19 compared to only 35% in 2008-09. In Kyrgyzstan, the labor market is characterized by a substantial gender gap in labor force participation, income, and quality of employment. In 2019, women's labor force participation rate stood at 51.7 percent compared to 79.3 percent for men.

Among the nine countries covered under this SRM facility and despite the variety of situations, gender gaps remain: in all cases women are facing similar challenges in terms of employment, in most cases lack of crucial infrastructure such as roads, electricity or water supply, and limited access to financial services and energy services as energy consumers. These challenges have been aggravated by the Covid-19 crisis which has worsened women's health outcomes by further reducing their access to health services, due to restrictions in movement and lack of financial resources. Women are also more likely than men to be responsible for childcare and domestic work, including women who are most responsible for household and family responsibilities. In addition to increasing their burden, this situation is also leading to a rise in violence against women and girls, which can lead to the abandonment of children and limiting their access to support services such as safe houses. All countries suffer from social norms that confine women to unpaid household and care work, often resulting in lower education, occupational sex-segregation and limited participation in public life.

The gender gap assessments conducted have in particular showed that:
- Legal framework on women's rights is often weak, poorly implemented and often contracted by traditional practices.
- Girls are more likely to drop-out of school due to household responsibilities, early marriage and childbearing as well as lack of infrastructure and gender-based violence.
- Women are under-represented in labor force participation and more often found in unpaid and vulnerable employment. They also earn less than men for the same work, have lower quality jobs and are often exposed to low wages and lack of legal protection in the informal sector.


---
**Page 185**
---

- Women continue to have limited access to ownership and finance. Female entrepreneurs also face barriers that limit their access to financial institutions including due to lack of collateral as a consequence of the aforementioned factors.
- Energy access is also hindering women's empowerment such as in Tajikistan where female entrepreneurs are particularly affected by the unreliability of electricity supply due to the fact that businesses where they produce and sell goods are often located far from energy infrastructure.
- Gender-based violence is pervasive and manifest itself in different forms, from domestic and intimate partner violence to female genital mutilation.
- Women's participation in politics remain extremely low and men continue to predominate within decision-making structures.
- Lack of comprehensive gender disaggregated data for the energy sector remain a significant challenge for making baseline assessments and for monitoring progress on gender equality across SRMIs countries.

In low income countries, women are disproportionately affected by lack of access to modern energy services due to their limited access to electricity and clean cooking facilities as they are mostly responsible for household chores. In Sub-Saharan Africa electrification rate stands at less than 48 percent, leaving nearly 600 million people without access to electricity. Lack of access to modern energy services has a direct impact on women's health outcomes. For instance, every year, 4.3 million people—mainly women and children—die as a result of indoor air pollution from the use of solid fuels for cooking and heating in low income countries. In South Africa the electrification of rural communities raised female employment by 9.5 percent.

Women have limited employment opportunities in the formal sector, including the energy sector, due to limited education, qualifications, and skills. They are therefore more likely to occupy lower-paid, lower-quality jobs in family-based enterprises or informal sectors. Women's participation is also low in science, technology, Engineering and Mathematics (STEM) fields and are disproportionately active in the construction and information and communication technologies sectors. In South Africa women represent 25 percent of workers in the production of gas, electricity and water and 96.5 percent in the building industry. This educational and occupational gap is reflected in energy infrastructure projects where female employment is lower due to a lack of technical skills and qualifications. Women's participation in the energy sector is also low as they represent about 12 percent of women, while it is estimated that the number of available jobs in the sector will increase from 10.3 million in 2015 to 14.8 million by 2030. The majority of these new jobs are expected to be created in projects is further constrained by cultural norms that limit women's choice and access to non-traditional fields. Employment opportunities created by women in large-scale power projects are therefore usually in traditionally female sectors such as catering, cleaning and security.

Women's limited political agency and representation in political institutions translates into lower female access and participation in energy-related decision-making. None of the targeted countries has more than 30 percent
