of female parliamentarians. In Mongolia, Kyrgyzstan, Indonesia and Guinea-Bissau, the rate of female in Parliament does not exceed 20 percent. Women’s representation and participation in the energy sector, including the RE sector, is also low because women are not seen as of a key stakeholder.

Although more data is needed to assess the prevalence of gender-based violence, studies indicate that women and girls routinely experience high levels of sexual harassment and intimate partner violence in target countries. This phenomenon is reinforced by social norms normalizing violence against women. For instance, in Guinea- Bissau, 50 percent of women report having experienced physical or sexual violence from their husband or wife in certain contexts while more than half of Mongolian women believe that women should be obedient to their husbands and that men should have more decision-making authority than their wives. The influx of migrant workers during the implementation of large-scale RE projects may increase GBV risks and should receive specific attention throughout the project cycle.

Most countries are signatories of international and regional gender equality agreements (e.g., the Convention on the Elimination All Forms of Discrimination against Women or CEDAW, the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action) and have ratified these instruments. Many countries have also adopted national strategies and mechanisms to address inequality between men and women. During project implementation, national and international legal frameworks may therefore be used to strengthen stakeholders’ commitment to promote women’s economic empowerment and agency, as is often aligned with government priorities.

Significant data gaps persist for gender disaggregated information across the energy sector in SMI countries, for example, in terms of access to electricity and clean cooking fuels. In 2018, only 45 percent of all 76 utilities across 45 Sub-Saharan countries published annual statistics that include gender data on employees. ESMAP is however prioritizing to contribute to closing the gender data gap through own research and in engagement with stakeholders and across the broad field. The gender system for energy access provides insights from new data developed by the forthcoming 2022 Regulatory Indicators for Sustainable Energy (RISE), which will be published by ESMAP.

### 2.1 Ethiopia’s Progress

Ethiopia’s economy experienced strong, broad-based growth in the past decade as one of the world’s fastest-growing economies. This period of robust growth of about 10 percent was driven by large-scale public investment in infrastructure and energy, which was made possible by favorable commodity prices and international debt relief (World Bank Group, 2018). Ethiopia has achieved a remarkable reduction in poverty from 45 to 23 percent between 2007 and 2016, one of the most impressive poverty reduction results recorded internationally. Primarily enrollment rate quadrupled, child mortality rate halved, and the number of people with access to clean water more than doubled.

However, Ethiopia remains within the 25 poorest countries in the world, with a per capita income of US$772 (2018). The country is still heavily dependent on rainfed small-scale agriculture. In addition, Ethiopia hosts more than 920,000 refugees, primarily from neighboring countries. The number of people living below the poverty line has risen from 35 to 46 percent (2018) and has risen from 1.6 million to 2.8 million since the beginning of 2018. Access to education has increased, but only 57 percent of children starting first grade will complete ninth grade. The Global Gender Gap report of 2020 ranked Ethiopia 82 out of 134 countries, with an improvement of 15 positions compared to 2018. Nevertheless, significant


---
**Page 187**
---

and deeply engrained disparities remain, and addressing gender inequalities in access to education, decision making, rights, unpaid labor, land, and credit continues to remain a crucial challenge for Ethiopia's economic growth.

Despite recent progress, large gaps persist between Ethiopia with regard to women’s rights mainly in the area of domestic violence. This is an issue of key women’s rights international and regional instruments including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW, 1981) and the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (signed in 2018). The Federal Constitution of Ethiopia (1995) prohibits discrimination on the basis of sex [Article 25] as well as “laws, customs, practices and stereotyped ideas that oppress women and adversely affect their physical or mental health” and provides for free and equal access to education, employment, and health services. Moreover, women’s right to acquire, administer, control, use, transfer and administer property is guaranteed by the Constitution [Article 25]. However, these constitutional provisions have not translated into equal access to inheritance and property rights, notably through facilitating land registration at lower cost.

In 2000, the Family Code was revised to strengthen women’s rights and remove discriminatory provisions. The age of marriage for both boys and girls was raised to 18 and women's legal rights to assets was increased. Furthermore, the Penal Code was amended to criminalize marital rape while still not providing a holistic definition of gender-based violence. Ethiopia has yet to adopt a comprehensive law on domestic violence.

At the national level, Ethiopia implemented several national policies and actions plan to promote women’s empowerment starting in 1993 with the first National Policy on Women. This policy was followed by a National Gender Policy (2004), the National Strategy for Empowerment of Women through Education, Employment and Change Package, the National Strategy and Action Plan on Harmful Traditional Practices (2013), and the Sexual and Reproductive Health Strategy (2016-2015). In 2016, the GoE published its second Growth and Transformation Plan (GTP II) with a focus on improving women’s economic empowerment by increasing their access to finance, ensuring their active participation in the country's political, social, and economic processes.

To narrow gender gaps in the energy sector, the GoE included tangible gender priorities in the National Electrification Strategy (2015-2030), the Energy Sector Reform Program (ESRP) and policy reforms on skills development. The World Bank Group provided US$737 million to support implementation of the government’s broader sectoral reform program with a unique focus on promoting gender equality as part of a broader, portfolio-wide effort to improve access to energy in Ethiopia. This included engagement with key national and international stakeholders, including senior leaders at Ethiopia Electric Utility Company (EEU) and the Ministry of Finance and Economic Development (MoFED). The World Bank Group also supported the development of the Gender Affairs Policy and Procedures, and related activities. In 2014 they established the Women, Children and Youth Affairs Directorate (WCYAD) to promote gender equality, institutionalize gender mainstreaming, and advocate for the rights of women employees [see the results in the case study below].

Box. I: Mobilizing Resources to Enhance Gender Equality in the Ethiopian Energy sector

Assessment of Gender Equality and Inclusion

The World Bank Group’s Energy Sector Management Assistance Program (ESMAP) provided technical support to explore gender equality issues within the energy sector. The goals centered on employment and leadership in technical fields; access to financing for adopting clean technologies; and agricultural productivity-related activities. Extensive in-country engagement by the World Bank Group with EEU led to the identification of key barriers to women’s participation and policy gaps, as well as an examination of the state of gender equality and inclusion in the EEU workplace.


---
**Page 188**
---

Engaging partners to promote gender equality

Along with the data collected from the project team began an in-depth engagement with client organizations to support the government’s commitment to a deeper understanding of what it will take to reach gender equality at the utility. These engagements included a Gender and Citizen Engagement Workshop, a STEM Dean’s Roundtable to strengthen links between energy institutions and universities and EEU Women and Youth Forums and Staff Councils.

Building capacity to implement women's affairs policy and procedures

Once an initial institutional mapping and stakeholder engagement was completed, structures were put into place at the WEEC to ensure that core components within which the World Bank Group program would be supported. With this in mind, new structures and support in place will enable the EEU to move from EEU to institutionalize gender equality, including the increased recruitment, retention, and promotion of female employees.

• A partnership between EEU, the Ministry of Science and Higher Education (MoSHE), and 12 Ethiopian universities has been established in order to increase women’s employment in the energy sector

• EEU will work with MoSHE to ensure that at least one-third of all new appointments made over the next three years should be female, in order for the utility to meet its 30 percent overall target by 2025

• Establishment of a women's mentorship program

• Strengthened institutional response to sexual harassment in the workplace, increased protection for women’s rights and access to justice, and improved quality of childcare services

• Measurement of progress made in EEU gender and citizen engagement program


### Guinea-Bissau

Guinea-Bissau is a small country in West Africa with a surface area of 36,000 km² and a population of about 1.8 million. It is one of the most fragile countries in Sub-Saharan Africa, plagued by political turmoil and coup d'états since independence in 1974. Between 2000 and the present, the country has had sixteen prime ministers appointed to office. The lack of a stable government, combined with the weak economic base and the political and military structure of the country, has prevented the formation of stable and accountable institutions and has a crippling effect on the economy.

The incidence of poverty is high, with over 71 percent of the population living below the poverty line (2011 PPP at US$5.5 per day), making it one of the poorest countries in the world². Average real GDP growth, although modest, improved from 0.3 percent per year in 2013 to 6 percent per year in 2017, mainly driven by a recovery in agricultural production and remittances. However, despite this improvement, the Gini coefficient is high (sharply increasing from 0.59 in 2002 to 0.5 in 2013), pointing to high levels of inequality with economic growth mostly benefiting the top income group. The country ranks 189th out of 189 countries on the 2013 UNDP Human Development Index, and is ranked as one of the lowest performers globally for all three low human development categories. Only 29 percent³ of Guinea-Bissau’s population – around 60 percent in the capital – has access to electricity.


1UNDP (2017) The Next Frontier: Human Development and the Autonomous Briefing note for countries on the 2020 Human Development Report, Guinea Bissau. <https://hdr.undp.org/sites/default/files/hdr_countries/egu.pdf>

2 Declined to 3.8 percent from a sharp drop in cashew production and somewhat lower international cashew prices.

3 Global Tracking Framework, 2018: https://trackingdata.eomap.org/country/guinea-bissau


---
**Page 189**
---

Guinea-Bissau is one of the countries that are most vulnerable to climate change. Recent climate scenarios point toward changes in the climate of Guinea-Bissau. Irregular droughts and frequent flooding in coastal and island regions threaten the economy and the livelihoods, especially for poor and vulnerable households with limited alternative livelihoods. Consequences of climate change constitute an important source of additional pressure for the need of climate-resilient energy infrastructures.

Guinea-Bissau's legal framework on women’s rights is weak, poorly implemented and often contradicted by traditional practices. The Constitution of Guinea-Bissau (1996) guarantees equality before the law of men and women in all fields of political, social, economic and cultural life. Article 24 prohibits all forms of discrimination on the grounds of sex, race or religion. Gender-based violence is not specifically addressed in the text of the Constitution. Article 37 states that the "moral and physical integrity of citizens cannot be violated" and that "no one can be subjected to torture or any other cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment or punishment". Women's rights are protected by the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the additional protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights concerning the Rights of Women [Malawi Protocol] (2015). The country ratified CEDAW in 1987, adopted a National Action Plan for the protection of women's rights in the workplace, and adopted a Labor Law in 1986 prohibiting gender-based discrimination. However, women are not allowed to perform heavy work or to work during the night.

The Constitution of Guinea-Bissau (1996) guarantees equality before the law of men and women in all fields of political, social, economic and cultural life. Article 24 prohibits all forms of discrimination on the grounds of sex, race or religion. Gender-based violence is not specifically addressed in the text of the Constitution. Article 37 states that the "moral and physical integrity of citizens cannot be violated" and that "no one can be subjected to torture or any other cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment or punishment". Women's rights are protected by the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the additional protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights concerning the Rights of Women [Malawi Protocol] (2015). The country ratified CEDAW in 1987, adopted a National Action Plan for the protection of women's rights in the workplace, and adopted a Labor Law in 1986 prohibiting gender-based discrimination. However, women are not allowed to perform heavy work or to work during the night.

Although the Constitution explicitly guarantees equality between men and women, discriminatory provisions remain in the Civil Code. Men are described as the unique head of the family with the right to represent their wives and make decisions on behalf of the household (Article 174). Wives are not allowed to be divorced without her husband's consent unless appointed administrator of all assets (Article 1674), and gender-based violence is not specifically addressed in the text of the Constitution. Article 37 states that the "moral and physical integrity of citizens cannot be violated" and that "no one can be subjected to torture or any other cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment or punishment". Women's rights are protected by the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the additional protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights concerning the Rights of Women [Malawi Protocol] (2015). The country ratified CEDAW in 1987, adopted a National Action Plan for the protection of women's rights in the workplace, and adopted a Labor Law in 1986 prohibiting gender-based discrimination. However, women are not allowed to perform heavy work or to work during the night.

Guinea-Bissau banned Human Trafficking in 2010. Female-Gendered Maternal Illness (FGMI) in 2011 and adopted a Domestic Violence Bill to prevent physical, psychological, economic and sexual violence in 2013. Rape is criminalized under the Penal Code of Guinea-Bissau. The country ratified CEDAW in 1987 and Guinea-Bissau adopted the Act of Reproductive Health in 2011, raising the minimum age of marriage to 18 for both girls and boys. Early and forced marriages are prohibited by law but not enforced. Many women are married before or without consent from a parent, guardian or court authority. Moreover, many marriage are not registered legally and the law does not seem to be implemented across the country, resulting in a high number of early and forced marriages [UNDP, 2021].

At the national level, the Government of Guinea-Bissau has adopted several framework documents in the past decade aimed at promoting women's rights. These include the National Strategy for the Promotion of Women, Peace and Security Agenda (NAPS), the Second Poverty Reduction Strategy (DENAP II,2030) as well as the National Policy for Gender Equity and Equality PNIES (Política Nacional para a Promoção da Igualdade e Equidade de Género). The Ministry of Justice is responsible for the promotion of women's rights. Includes the Institute for Women and Children, JIMC (Instituto da Mulher e Criança).

# Indonesia Profile

Indonesia is the largest archipelago in the world and the largest economy in Southeast Asia. With more than 260 million inhabitants, the country is ethnically and culturally diverse and home to 300 different language groups. Indonesia reached upper-middle-income status in 2019, following years of steady economic growth. Its economy is based on natural resources such as oil, gas, coal, gold, copper, nickel, tin, rubber, paper, timber and rubber. Indonesia experienced rapid poverty reduction in the past decades: the poverty rate declined from 34% to 7% between 1998 and 2015; however, inequality remains high and 60 percent of the population is still at risk of falling into poverty.

Poverty is concentrated in rural areas, where about 45 percent of the country's population lives and is compounded by uneven distribution of services and a lack of education. The most impoverished provinces are Papua, Papua Barat, West Nusa Tenggara (NTT) and East Nusa Tenggara (EAST NTT). Eastern Islands - targeted by the project - host around half of the 25 million people living below the poverty line. While 90% of the population has access to electricity, the majority of the 6 million people who lack electricity lives in the Eastern islands.

Due to its geographical location and the over-exploitation of resources, Indonesia is extremely vulnerable to climate change. The country faces a high risk from natural hazards such as earthquakes and tsunamis and its islands are threatened by rising sea level. The impact of climate change may disproportionately affect women who are more likely to rely on agriculture for food security (FAO, 2019).

Indonesian women are not fully protected by the Indonesian legal system due to legislative gaps, conflicting regional laws, and a lack of enforcement mechanisms. Indonesia has a complex legal framework combining a combination of civil, customary, and religious laws that may contradict each other, with a direct impact on women's rights. For example, while Muslim women in Aceh are protected under Islamic law, non-Muslim women must refer to the Islamic Law. Furthermore, the country’s legal system lacks provisions to protect women from discrimination in the workplace such as sexual harassment.

The Government of Indonesia has ratified several international conventions on gender equality, including the United Nations Convention on the Political Rights of Women (ratified by law 64/1958), the United Nations Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) (ratified by law number 73/2004), and the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights, which was signed by the Government in 2000.

Key achievements in the field of women's rights include:

- The 1945 Constitution stating that "every citizen enjoys equal status before the law and government and is obliged to uphold this status without exception" (Paragraph A, Article 27).
- The Indonesian Law No. 3/1960 on Equal Pay for Equal Work and Equal Remuneration payment for equal work;
- The Agrarian Law No. 5/1960 recognizing individual land ownership and that women and men have equal rights to land ownership;
- The Presidential Regulation No. 5/2000 on Gender Mainstreaming in National Development instructing all government bodies to mainstream gender in their policies;
- The Labour Law No. 13/2003 on Maternity Leave (4 weeks per month), maternal leave (3 months), leave for miscarriage (1.5 months), and provision of time for breastfeeding;
- The Law 23/2004 on the Elimination of Domestic Violence;
- The 17/2006 Law on Combating Human Trafficking, child labour trafficking and sex trafficking of adults; 
- The Law 3/2009 on the Protection of Women and Anti-Gender-Based Violence;
- The Law No. 45/2008 on the Right to Vote and Run for Office for Women Candidates to be elected to the House of Representatives, People's Representative Council, and Regional House of Representatives.

Article 10 of Law No. 2593 ensures access to parental leave for the electricity utility workforce*.

### 2.4 Kyrgyzstan Profile

The Kyrgyz Republic remains one of the poorest countries in the ECA region and is one of only six countries there (Tajikistan being the other). Its population is 6.4 million, living in largely mountainous terrain. The country's GDP per capita was $1058 in 2019, with a large share of its economy dominated by agriculture and export of mineral oil as well as gold. It makes it vulnerable to external shocks such as the global financial crisis and the recent COVID-19 pandemic owing to the COVID-19 outbreak. Shock to the health, economic, and social aspects such as lower labor earnings, reduced remittances, job losses, and higher food prices have driven poverty up; poverty rate predicted to increase by 5.9 percentage points in 2020.

Kyrgyzstan has adopted several landmark laws in recent years to strengthen women's rights and promote gender equality. The Kyrgyz Republic is a party to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), ratified in 1997) and its optional protocol. The Constitution of the Kyrgyz Republic amended in 2010 guarantees equality between men and women, prohibits discrimination against women, and provides for equal rights.

Kyrgyzstan improved its regulatory and legal framework on women's rights in recent years. The Election Code was revised in 2007 and 2011 to include a binding 30 percent quota of women in civil service and party candidates lists. Moreover, the Kyrgyz Republic has adopted laws that allow women to run for election to country’s local councils. In the area of gender-based violence, Kyrgyzstan adopted a Law on the Prevention and Protection against Domestic Violence in 2015. The law prohibits domestic violence, defines domestic violence and allows any person to report domestic violence incidents. While the country's Family Code prohibits marriage under the age of 18 (Article 14) and the abduction of girls (Article 154 and 155), research indicates that many women are still married before they reach this age.

In 2008, the government of Kyrgyzstan adopted the Law “On State Guarantees of Equal Rights and Equal Opportunities for Women and Men.” The law provides for equal rights in all spheres of life. This Law also sets out mechanisms for its effective implementation by public agencies. At the policy level, the Kyrgyz Republic enacted its first National Gender Strategy (NGS) in 2012, followed by the adoption of several national gender policies. The Ministry of Labor and Social Development is responsible for the implementation of the National Gender Strategy, as well as the National Council for Gender Development. In the energy sector the utility workforce has been improved to ensure a minimum of one woman among its top three ranked candidates (Article 127 of the Labor Code). Kyrgyzstan has also developed policies to improve gender balance of the utility workforce*.

# Mongolia Profile

Mongolia is a landlocked country in Central Asia bordered by China and Russia. It is among the least densely populated countries in the world, with a population of 3.2 million for a land area of 1.5 million sq km. Approximately 60 percent of the population are nomads. Mongolia endures a harsh climate, agriculture, livestock and mineral resources including coal, copper, and gold.

Mongolia has made progress in establishing a market economy and democracy following its independence from the Soviet Union, allowing it to reach low-middle income country status. However, Mongolia's economic reliance on the extraction and export of natural resources make it dependent on fluctuating commodity prices. In 2018, close to one million people lived below the poverty line in rural areas while another two million were considered in urban areas. In Ulaanbaatar where half of the population lives, approximately 16 percent of households live below the poverty line.

Mongolia's climate is extreme and characterized by a wide range of temperature. The capital city Ulaanbaatar is one of the coldest capitals in the world with long winters and temperatures regularly below 30 Celsius degrees. Energy services are therefore essential, while electricity needs have increased due to population growth and migration from rural areas into urban centers. However, access to energy services for households remains often unreliable due to insufficient supplies and poor condition of the distribution networks.

In the past decade, Mongolia has strengthened its legal and policy frameworks to improve women's rights protection and promote gender equality. The Constitution guarantees equal access to land and assets (Article 15) "in the political, economic, social, and cultural life and family relations" and prohibits sex-based discrimination in its Article 14. In addition, the Constitution guarantees equal access to land and assets and the Civil Code (Article 320) protects women's rights to property ownership and inheritance. The law also provides for a quota of at least 35% for both boys and girls while allowing for derogations in some cases. In the workplace, women are protected from gender-based discrimination by the Labor Law of 1999 which recognizes specific needs of pregnant and nursing women.

Mongolia adopted several landmark laws in the past decade to improve women's rights protection and promote gender equality. In 2011, the Government enacted the Health Law recognizing the right to maternal and child care and providing for the establishment of a national health insurance system. The law also established public agencies responsible for the promotion of gender equality. In addition, the Law on Parliament Election adopted in 2013 provides for women's representation in central and local political bodies, and 20 percent in candidate list for national and local elections.

In the area of gender-based violence, Mongolia's Criminal Code provides a definition of trafficking aligned with international conventions and prohibits rape in its Article 126. In addition, Mongolia's Parliament adopted a Law on Domestic Violence Trials (Article 3) which entered into force in January 2017. The law was further strengthened in 2017, Mongolia adopted its first comprehensive Law to Combat Domestic Violence (LCDV) after more than a decade of advocacy by civil society organizations. The newly enacted law includes a comprehensive definition of domestic violence and recognizes it as a criminal offense.

Mongolia's institutional machinery for the promotion of gender equality at the national level includes the National Commission on Gender Equality, which is responsible for coordinating the implementation of the Law on Gender Equality. Key policies for women's empowerment include the National Programme on Gender Equality (NIGE) regularly revised since 1996, the National Programme on Fighting Against Domestic Violence (2007) as well as the third Reproductive Health Program (2013). In the energy sector, the female labor workforce

The Republic of Seychelles lies in the Somali Sea segment of the Indian Ocean, about 1,500 km east of Kenya.

Its population of nearly 100,000, about three quarters of whom live on the island of Mahé, places it as the smallest population of any sovereign African country. Since gaining its independence in 1976, its nominal GDP grew by 700%, making Seychelles one of the world’s fastest-growing economies. Seychelles has the highest per-capita GDP in Africa and the second highest HDI in Africa behind Mauritius.

The economic and social shock from COVID-19 on the Seychellois economy has been severe. Economic growth declined significantly in 2020 to -13.5% from 4.1% in 2019 due to the disruptions occasioned in activities economic in Seychelles. The tourism sector, which accounts for approximately 30 percent of GDP and is facilitated by more than 60% in the fisheries sector, around 50 percent of the domestic artisanal catches of demersal species are sold to the resorts and restaurants for tourists. With the closure of all resorts in Seychelles after the start of the pandemic and restrictions surrounding its recovery, the fisheries sector was vulnerable to the effects of Covid-19 restrictions.

As a small country composed of 115 tropical islands, Seychelles is highly vulnerable to the effects of climate change. Its primary economic activities are tourism and fishing. The island nation’s economy is also heavily reliant on tourism, including rainfall (crop and fish losses; flooding); and sea-level rise (coastal erosion and salinization), and consequent losses to tourism and food and water security. In particular, the main islands of Mahé, Praslin, La Digue, and Silhouette are particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts such as those with high exposure to hazards including storms, flooding, landslides, and destruction of coastal property and livelihoods make them particularly vulnerable to climate change.

Currently, Seychelles is heavily dependent on fossil fuels for its energy generation, with 97.5% of demand being met from fossil fuels and only 2.5% coming from renewable sources. To address this issue, the Government of Seychelles (GoS) has submitted Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) targets at part of the Paris Agreement to reduce its greenhouse gas emissions by 30% below business as usual levels in 2030 and by 75% in 2050. In the long term, the National Energy Policy (proposed in 2016) pursues a target of 100% of energy supply to be provided by renewable energy sources by 2050, with a target of 13% to be reached by 2028.

While the GoS has made significant progress on its NDCs and despite recent progress, significant legal gaps remain. In 1997, Seychelles ratified the Convention for the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) without any reservations. However, civil society organizations highlight that there are still many cases where women’s rights are not respected in practice by judges and legal practitioners and that the CEDAW has never been cited in legal cases.The island nation also adopted the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (1999) and submitted to the Southern African Development Community (SADC) Protocol on Gender and Development in 2008.

The Constitution of Seychelles does not explicitly acknowledge the principle of equality between men and women and does not prohibit gender-based discrimination. Article 30 of the Constitution protects the right of


---

working mothers and their "village status and natural maternal function". The 1995 Employment Act guarantees 10 days of paid paternity leave to working fathers. 112 days for mothers. Seychelles has made recent progress in strengthening legal protection for women against violence. The country adopted a National Domestic Violence Bill in May 2020, which provides legal definition for different forms of violence while setting out institutional mechanisms for its prevention and response. The Prohibition of Trafficking in Persons Act enacted in 2014 criminalizes all forms of trafficking of adults and children.

At the institutional level, gender-related initiatives are managed by the Gender Secretariat within the Ministry of Family Affairs. Seychelles has adopted its First National Gender Policy in 2016. However, in its 2019 report, the UN Commission on the Status of Women noted that the country had not yet implemented it and that the Gender Secretariat only operated with one full-time officer and that allocation of resources was insufficient.

### 2.7 Somalia Profile

Somalia bears the development burden of two and a half decades of conflict, fragility and state fragmentation following the collapse of the Siad Barre government in January 1991 and ethnic and border disputes in the Horn of Africa. The country has experienced several civil wars since then, resulting in the displacement of millions. Somalia's current political structure broadly consists of three self-administered and self-governed regions: Somaliland, Puntland, and Southern Somalia. Somaliland is an autonomous region that declared independence from Somalia on 26 June 1991.

Somalia is on a trajectory towards poverty reduction and inclusive growth including political stabilization and reconstruction. In 2012, a provisional constitution was adopted, establishing a new Federal Government (FGS) and state structures for the country's three regions. The FGS has been working with the international community to agree the Somaliland Compact with the FGS based on the New Deal, a guiding set of principles for peacebuilding and state-building in fragile contexts.

Somalia has a population of about 15 million, of which roughly 60 percent are nomadic or semi-nomadic pastoralists, and 60 percent live in rural areas. Nearly 70 percent of the population lives below the poverty line, and another 10 percent live close to the poverty line. About 6.2 million Somalis face acute food insecurity and 2 million people are internally displaced persons (IDPs) as a result of conflict and drought.

Before the COVID-19 pandemic, Somalia's economy was on an upward trajectory, recovering from the 2016/17 drought and the economic crisis at an estimated rate of 2.9 percent in 2015. On par with population growth, and was projected to reach a GDP per capita of $384 by 2020. The COVID-19 pandemic has had a devastating impact on Somalia's economy together with devastating flooding and a new inflation of desert locust the economy is expected to contract by 2.5 percent in 2020. Somalia’s population and remains highly vulnerable to natural disasters and climate changes - especially in the north, where most of its IDPs are located.

Somalia recently adopted its Ninth National Development Plan (NDP) for the period 2020-2024, which outlines the country's priorities for programs to reduce poverty and boost inclusive growth in the medium term. In March 2019, Somalia signed a $35 million International Development Association (IDA) loan agreement - a major milestone that allows resource flows from International Financial Institutions (IFIs). This milestone respects access to regular concessional financing from IDA and other IFIs, together with investment of private capital from the International Finance Corporation.

The conflict destroyed the public physical and institutional electricity sector infrastructure of the former single public utility ENEL. Today, private players are the main electricity service providers (ESPs) in Somalia with isolated diesel-powered mini grids. Limited infrastructure footprint and high cost of supply prevent access to electricity.


---
**Page 195**
---

The electricity access rate is estimated at 33 percent1, with 10 million Somalis living in the dark. A disparity remains between access rates in urban areas (approximately 70 percent), rural areas (19 percent).

### Somali Government sector institutions are in the formation stage with no effective institutional and legal frameworks for electricity sector development. The Federal Government of Somalia (FGS), the Ministry of Energy and Water Resources (MoEWR) has the mandate to oversee operations in the electricity sector, whereas in Somaliland, the Ministry of Energy and Minerals (MoEM) has the mandate over the energy sector. However, both institutions are still in their formative stages with no institutional capacity or fully functional. Key sector decisions are made by the MoEWR in the FGS and MoEM in Somaliland respectively. Due to the absence of regulations and standards codes of practice, there is no mechanism to vet and enforce electricity service providers’ safety and quality.

Somalia submitted its first NDC in November 2015 and is in the process of preparing updated NDCs. remains committed to achieve the target despite its challenges and national circumstances. Through the updated NDC, Somalia has set a target for reducing its GHG emissions by 69%CO2e relative to the BAU scenario by 2030. To achieve this target, Somalia will have to reduce its GHG emissions by about 69%CO2e relative to the BAU scenario by 2030; (ii) increase energy efficiency in buildings and appliances through increased implementation action in the energy sector include (i) increased use of clean and energy efficient cookstoves (at least a million new cookstoves); (ii) replacement of kerosene lamps by renewable lamps (30% renewable lamps by 2030); (iii) increase the share of electricity from renewable sources to at least 15% in 2030; and (iv) development of renewable energy resources (at least 60 MW new RE by 2030 and 30% RE based electricity generation by 2030); and (v) reduction of GHG emissions from land use, land-use change and forestry works. The estimated required investment to meet the above NDCs for the energy sector is US$ 15 billion.

### Somalia's legal framework on women’s rights is weak and poorly enforced across the country. At the international level, the Federal Government of Somalia is among a few countries globally that have not yet ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW). Similarly, Somalia has not yet signed or ratified the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (The Maputo Protocol).

Somalia is governed by a plural system combining secular law, customary law known as erer and Islamic laws. These three systems compete with themselves, to the detriment of women’s rights. For instance, although Somalia's Family Law guarantees equal access to inheritance, inheritance rights are governed by Sharia Law which disadvantage Somali women. Furthermore, customary practices often prevent women from getting any inheritance at all. Despite the fact that the Federal Government of Somalia and some local governments have provided some progress with regards to women’s rights protection, implementation is lacking. Despite an amendment in 2014, the law on paid maternity leave for both private sector and government female employees has not been enforced by employers. In addition, although the law was amended in 2016, most companies do not enforce the law and the majority of employed women get less than 4 months of leave. At the policy level, a National Gender Policy was drafted in 2008 and came into effect in 2014. That same year, the GoS adopted a National Action Plan on Ending Sexual Violence in Conflict.

Somalia’s labor legislation provides four months of paid maternity leave for both private sector and government female employees. However, despite this amendment, most companies do not enforce the law and women in Somalia in 2016, most companies do not enforce the law and the majority of employed women get less than 4 months of leave. At the policy level, a National Gender Policy was drafted in 2008 and came into effect in 2014. That same year, the GoS adopted a National Action Plan on Ending Sexual Violence in Conflict.

2.8 Tajikistan Profile

Tajikistan, a former member of the USSR, is a landlocked and mountainous country in Central Asia with a population of about 9.3 million, almost three-quarters of whom live in rural areas. Mountains cover more than 90 percent of the country's surface area, with only a small portion suitable for cultivation. The country is endowed with rich hydropower potential and mineral resources such as gold, coal, and uranium. Tajiks form the main ethnic group within the population, which also includes Russians, Uzbeks, Kyrgyz, and others. As in the case of the Kyrgyz and the Russians, Islam is the predominant religion throughout the country with more than 96 percent of the population being Muslim.

Following the country's cessation from the Soviet Union in 1991, a devastating 5 years civil war took place resulting in the death of more than 60,000 people and the displacement of 600,000 people. An estimated 26,000 women were raped during this period. The UN estimates that 38 percent of Tajikistan's population is under age 15, indicating that women were specifically targeted during the war, experiencing forced marriages, sex trafficking as well rape and other forms of sexual violence. Furthermore, at the same period and following the end of the Soviet era, traditional gender roles have been reinforced.

Despite declining poverty rates, Tajikistan underperforms with regards to quality and accessibility of public goods and services. Tajikistan enjoyed rapid decline in poverty rates over the past two decades, allowing the country to achieve MDG 1 (halving extreme poverty) by 2015. The poverty rate fell from 48 percent in 1997 to 3.6 percent in 2018, reflecting acceleration of economic growth and recovery in inflows of remittance. The rural poverty rate declined markedly from 36.1 percent in 2014 to 30.2 in 2018, reflecting rising household consumption levels and the decline in the share of households living below the poverty line in rural areas.

Transport and communication are limited by the country's mountainous topography affecting the satisfactory provision of electricity, water, heating and roads network.

Tajikistan has made significant progress on rapid increasing, further affecting the availability of productive employment, public goods and services as well as food supply. According to the 2017 Demographic and Health Survey (DHS), 38 percent of the population is under age 15. More than 30 percent of the population is under 30 years old. Tajikistan has made significant progress in reducing maternal mortality, with a decline from 469 per 100,000 live births to 274 per 100,000 live births between 2005 and 2018. However, the country still faces challenges affecting children of female-headed households who are poorer on average and may have limited access to clean water, sanitation facilities, and health services.

Although women's rights are acknowledged and protected by the country's national laws and policies, gender experts often point out the lack of clear implementation processes as well as the lack of knowledge of gender-related domestic and international legal norms among law practitioners. Tajikistan has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1992. The country also adopted a national action plan to comply with the UN Security Council resolutions on women, peace, and security.

Article 17 of the Constitution of the Republic of Tajikistan prohibits all forms of discrimination, including gender-based discrimination. Anti-discrimination principles are embedded in the country's national laws and regulations including the Law on Equal Rights and Opportunities for Men and Women adopted by Tajikistan in 2005. The Law on Equal Rights and Opportunities for Men and Women adopted by Tajikistan in 2005 promotes gender equality without setting up any enforcement mechanism. This law was often criticized by experts for its declaratory nature. Other laws promoting women's rights include the 2006 Law on the protection

# Tunisia: A Structural Reform Program Hobbled by Democratic Transition

## Tunisia’s structural reform program was hampered by the democratic transition since the 2011 revolution.

Tunisia is a lower-middle-income country with a population of 11.6 million and a gross domestic product (GDP) of US$39.9 billion, or GDP per capita of US$3,137 in 2020). Often hailed as the only success story of the Arab Spring, the country has been facing economic challenges since the 2011 revolution. The transition to democracy was accompanied by a series of reforms aimed at addressing the structural weaknesses that had hindered Tunisia’s development over decades.

### Tunisia’s Structural Reform Program

The Tunisian government launched its first-ever structural reform program in 2013 and introduced a new constitution in 2014. The Government of Tunisia (GoT) has also embarked on an ambitious reform agenda, aimed at boosting civil society and democratic freedoms, improving the business environment, and promoting economic growth.

### Key Reforms

The GoT’s reforms were part of the Five-Year Development Plan 2016–2020 (FYP), followed by the Economic and Social Roadmap 2018-2020, aimed at accelerating structural reforms. These reforms included the establishment of a national dialogue on social safety nets; and the enhancement of private investment, competitiveness, and productivity.

### Challenges

Despite the effort provided by various governments, the country failed to move forward in the implementation of reforms due to political instability and lack of consensus among stakeholders. A new reform agenda has been initiated by the GoT to negotiate the government’s economic reform plan to address critical economic and social challenges.

### Macroeconomic Performance

The GoT’s economic policies have further undermined Tunisia’s weak macroeconomic performance and budgetary constraints, leading to limited fiscal capacity to respond to future shocks. The economy is heading towards its deepest recession since the 1960s after a long period of low economic growth and employment levels, low productivity, and declining competitiveness.

### Export-Oriented Sectors

In 2021, export-oriented sectors such as light manufacturing in electronic products, textiles, etc., tourism, and other services have been negatively affected by the pandemic. The sector has also been impacted by border closures and confinement measures. Despite a US$1 billion package to support vulnerable households and firms to cope with the COVID-19 crisis, the macroeconomic situation, which was already fragile, is under further strain due to the economic impact of the pandemic.

### External Debt

Almost 4 billion euros of external financing are required to finance the 2021 budget; given the limited fiscal space, the use of savings and foreign aid are among a few ways to ensure the budget is funded. Unless a safe and efficient path to debt service is deployed on a large scale, the country’s ability to finance itself will be compromised.

### Tourism Sector

The tourism sector, which was not able to reopen, constrains economic growth.

### Structural Reforms Needed

Structural reforms are most needed to face the critical economic and social challenges. The country’s macro-fiscal deficit is projected to reach 10% of GDP in 2021, with a large fiscal gap. While most of public resources have so far been absorbed by the impending needs of the post-revolution context, going forward,

Tunisia must focus on broad-based and sustainable growth. This entails concrete actions to consolidate the country’s macroeconomic and fiscal situation and attract private investment, trade, and entrepreneurship. Equally important is to ensure that women have equal opportunities in all these areas.

While Tunisia has adopted a comprehensive legal framework to promote women's rights in recent years, significant gaps remain especially with regards to access to inheritance. Tunisia was among the first countries in the region to ratify the Convention on the Elimination of All forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 2016 and signed the African Charter on Human and People’s Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (Maputo Protocol).

**Article 21 of Tunisia's Constitution acknowledges equality between men and women. This Constitution was adopted in January 2014, after months of intense debate initiated by conservative forces that sought to include a mention of Sharia law. Article 35 of the Constitution guarantees equal opportunities for men and women to access "all levels of responsibility in all fields" and Article 34 seeks to promote women's representation in elected bodies.

In the area of gender-based violence, the 2016 Law on Preventing and Combating Trafficking in Persons provides protection against trafficking. The same year, Tunisia adopted a comprehensive law on Eliminating Violence against Women including the creation of several mechanisms to protect women and girls from gender-based violence. However, no specific mention of marital rape is made in the law. Article 226 of the Penal Code prohibits sexual intercourse with a minor under 15 years old. The law also includes a provision that removes a "marriage rapist" provision in the Penal Code that exonerated a rapist if he married his victim.

Personal Status rights are quite progressive compared to other countries in the region, although women continue to be discriminated against under inheritance laws. The legal age of marriage for each spouse is 18 years and above. Women have equal rights with men regarding divorce, which can be initiated by either party and protected by the State. Abortion is permitted under Article 214 of the Penal Code, during the first three months of pregnancy. However, women are disadvantaged in terms of inheritance as they only inherit half what sons receive. Furthermore, although men and women have the same rights to pass on their nationality to their children, this is not the case with respect to passing citizenship to a foreign spouse.

Women's right to work is guaranteed by the Labor Code although women are prohibited from undertaking night work as well as mining and scrap metal work. Furthermore, Article 64 of the Labor Code states that women are only entitled to 30 days of maternity leave which is below the 14 weeks recommended by ILO’s standards on maternity protection.

3. KEY GENDER GAPS

The following section identifies key issues that may impact projects under the SRM Facility. Preliminary gender assessments for each of the nine countries under the SRM facility highlighted the following gaps and their potential mitigation actions which will be explored during project design.

### 3.1 Ethiopia Gender Gap

Despite substantial progress over the past two decades on several aspects of gender equality, significant gender gaps remain with regards to women's access to education, health, and economic opportunities. Ethiopia is ranked 82 out of 154 countries by the World Economic Forum (WEF) 2020 Global Gender Gap Ranking. According to the report, Ethiopia closed 70.5 percent of its gender gap and is among the top five most improved countries in the region.

The United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) and the World Bank Group Development Research Group introduced the Gender Inequality Index (GII), to reflect gender-based inequalities in three dimensions: reproductive health, empowerment, and economic activity. Ethiopia is ranked 125 out of 162 countries for its GII in the 2019 index, between Rwanda (ranked 73) and Uganda (ranked 131).

#### 1. Gender gaps in educational outcomes

While there has been a significant leap in primary school enrollment and increased school attendance at primary and secondary levels, inequalities remain in educational attainment. Girls are more likely to drop out of school due to household responsibilities, early marriage, and childbearing as well as lack of infrastructure and gender-based violence. Ethiopia significantly increased educational outcomes for boys and girls, due to the elimination of fees for primary education under the Education Sector Development Program (ESDP). According to the Ethiopian Demographic and Health Survey (SDHS, 2016), the proportion of women who did not receive an education decreased from 77 percent in 2000-09 to 49 percent in 2016. Men are on average more likely to have received an education. The number of men with no formal education is 35 percent, compared to 48 percent for women.

Women and men's access to education vary greatly according to region. In 2016, the proportion of women with no education stood at 45 percent in rural areas, compared to 24 percent in urban areas. However, literacy rates remain high, especially for women who are dealing with issues related to education: only 43 percent of Ethiopian women were literate, compared to 69 percent of men.

Despite improvement in educational outcomes for both men and women, gender gaps remain wide, especially in higher education. The proportion of women aged 20-29 years who had completed secondary or higher education in 2016 stood at 15 percent, compared to 23 percent for men.

## Early marriage, childbearing as well as household responsibilities are among the key factors underlying women's lower educational outcomes (according to the SDG). Girls in their teens and early twenties who are likely to be responsible for childcare and water. (Ethiopia) girls of age 18-19 spend on average more than 4 hours a day on domestic chores, compared to 1.5 hours for boys of the same age. In Ethiopia, 16 percent of girls drop out of school due to care after their siblings (World Bank, 2020). Furthermore, the median age at first marriage is only 17 years and in Ethiopia women complete to nearly 24 years among men. The vast majority of women who attend school do so when they get married (5% percent drop out immediately after their marriage. In addition, women can be discouraged from pursuing their education by the lack of proper sanitation at schools, as well as GBV risks on the way to school and in school facilities.

## 2. Gender gaps in health outcomes

Despite improvements in recent years, Ethiopian women continue to suffer from poor reproductive health outcomes, maternal mortality, and indoor pollution. Between 2000 and 2016, the fertility rate has decreased consistently from 5.5 to 4.6 births per woman. Fertility rates vary greatly across the country: rural women have nearly 3 more children than urban women (5.2 children versus 2.3 children). Women have on average one more child than men. Only 22 percent of currently married women have an unmet need for family planning (SHSID, 2016). The proportion of women who used modern contraception has increased in the last two decades while remaining low: only 35 percent of women used modern contraceptives in 2016, compared to 6 percent in 2000.

The Maternal Mortality Ratio continues to be high despite recent progress (Figure 2). Every year, 401 women out of every 100,000 die giving birth, one of the highest rates in Africa. Only 32 percent of women had at least four


---
**Page 201**
---

antenatal care visits during their pregnancies and only 27 percent of births are attended by skilled health personnel. Most women encounter problems receiving birth services, with 70 percent of women age 15–49 reporting unmet need for antenatal care (EDHS 2016). Research indicates that early childbearing is associated with higher health risks for the mother and her child. In Ethiopia, 10 percent of women aged 15–19 are already mothers, and 2 percent are pregnant with their first child.

Source: Ethiopia Demographic and Health Survey, 2016

Ethiopian women are disproportionately exposed to indoor pollution due to the widespread use of solid fuel for cooking. Data from the 2016 Demographic and Health Survey indicate that 93 percent of households use traditional forms of cooking. Cooking is done in a separate building or outdoors in only 47 percent of households. Indoor pollution has been associated with increased health risks such as respiratory diseases.

### 3. Formal employment

Women are underrepresented in labor force participation and more often found in unpaid and vulnerable employment. Women's labor force participation rate is 11 percentage points lower than men: 87.8 percent of men are in active employment compared to 77 percent of women. Gender labor gaps are the most apparent among young people, with female labor force participation rates for ages 24–34 at 50 percent and male rates at 69 percent (UNICEF, 2020). According to EDHS data for 2017, women were underrepresented across the workforce, especially in decision-making positions. The data for 2018 found that women accounted for 5–37 percent of STEM-related participation in agriculture, information and communication technology (ICT), and electrical safety. In 2018 the Ethiopian Electric Utility (EEU) set a target to reach 30% women in their workforces by 2023. The current employment stands at 25% women, which is an increase over 80 female employees.

Women are less likely to be active economically due to disproportionate domestic and childcare responsibilities. Data from the EDHS indicate that the vast majority of husbands (63 percent) rarely participate in household


---
**Page 202**
---

The majority of economically active women are engaged in the agricultural sector, often in subsistence farming. Women working in agriculture and small-scale manufacturing earn less than men. Half of women engaged in the agriculture sector receive no payment, and 58 percent in small-scale manufacturing. In 2016, only 9 percent of employed women were waged and salaried workers. When they are paid, employed women experience a significant gender wage gap, partly due to lower education. According to the WEP Global Gender Gap report, Ethiopia has yet to close over 50% of its wage-related gap. Women in employment are less likely to obtain skilled and management positions and are underrepresented in technical employment.

### Women's access to ownership and finance
Although Ethiopia’s legislation guarantees equal access to assets, women are less likely to own productive asset and financial services. In 2020, only 35 percent as well as their male counterpart. In Ethiopia, 16.5 percent of firms have a female majority ownership and only 4.5 percent have a female top management (WEF, 2020). In the agriculture sector, female farmers manage on average smaller lands, have less diversified crops and are less


---
**Page 203**
---

productive than male farm managers. Female farmers produce on average 36 percent less per hectare than man and manage an average of 0.6 hectares compared to 1 hectare for men (World Bank, 2020). In addition, self-employed women have less access to credit in their households than men do because they are seen as a lower risk by lenders as well as GBV risks preventing them from working after certain hours. As a result, self-employed women spend on average 17 hours per week on their economic activity, compared to 23 hours for self-employed men. In addition to spending less time on their economic activities, female managers are less likely to own a business licence, have access to credit and insurance, or be literate.

Key drivers preventing women from scaling their businesses include lower access to technology, limited education, gender norms and lack of access to support networks. Data from the 2016 EDHS indicate significant gender and regional disparities in the use of bank account and ownership of mobile phones. While 44 percent of urban women have a bank account, this is only the case for 73 percent of rural women. Likewise, 71 percent of urban women own a mobile phone compared with 15 percent of rural women. Since 2014, men have increased bank account ownership by 18 percentage points, nearly twice the increase among women (Findeis, 2017).

In terms of energy and economic opportunities, 47% of female-headed households are in the bottom two spending quintiles, significantly higher than the comparable figure of 38% of male-headed households. The lack of financial resources of female-headed households impact their ability to invest in grid-connectivity. Among unconnected households, 61.5% of female-headed households are willing to pay full price upfront for a connection to the grid, compared with 60.1% of male-headed households, according to a 2018 World Bank survey.

**Page 204**

5. Women’s political participation

Although women’s representation in political institutions has increased significantly in recent years, men still predominate at regional and community levels. The 2018 election saw a substantial increase in female representation, making Ethiopia one of the top countries worldwide for female political participation. Female representation in Parliament increased dramatically over the past thirty years, from 3 percent in 1999 and 7 percent in 2005 to 46 percent in 2018. In addition, Sahle-Work Zewde became Ethiopia’s first female President. At the government level, more than 47 percent of ministers are women and Meaza Ashenafi is the first female defense minister in the country's history. Moreover, the number of female candidates running for federal and regional elections has significantly increased, reaching 1270 female candidates in 2015 compared to 937 in 2000. However, women in politics are often exposed to gender-based violence in politics, preventing them from freely exercising their political rights (NRC,2020).

6. Gender-Based Violence (GBV)

Gender-based violence is pervasive and manifest itself in different forms, from domestic and intimate partner violence to female genital mutilation. According to the 2016 EDHS, 23 percent of women age 15–49 have experienced physical violence and 30 percent have experienced sexual violence. Domestic violence affect 34 percent of women who are married or in union. Fear of violence and high levels of tolerance for domestic violence reinforce perpetrator impunity. Only one-quarter of women who have experienced GBV seek help, while 63 percent of women continue to believe that a husband can beat his wife in certain contexts.

**Page 205**

Although slowly declining, female genital mutilation continue to be widespread with wide regional disparities. Data from the 2015 SDG indicate that 65 percent of women age 15-49 have undergone female genital mutilation, with a prevalence rate of 78 percent in the Sennar region, as at 33 percent in Tigray. However, trends indicate growing despair for the practice: in 2016 only 18 percent of women believed female circumcision should be continued, compared to 31 percent in 2005.

With support from the World Bank Group and Energy Sector Management Assistance Program (ESMAP), the government has launched a major reform of its energy sector to reach universal electrification by 2025, in which it aims to create more equitable institutions and equal benefits for women. Working together with teams from across the country, ESMAP supported the World Bank Group in developing the Energy Sector Management Assistance Program (ESMAP) — which developed new ways of looking at gender across the entire US $2 billion World Bank energy portfolio in the country. Findings from consultations with multiple stakeholders were combined with country-specific data and lessons learned to inform a gender equality action plan for the sector. This knowledge helped to shape specific actions on gender equality under the $375 million Ethiopia Electrification Program (EEEP), one of the largest and most ambitious electrification programs IDA has undertaken in Africa. Dedication to gender equality is also reflected in the EEEP’s budget allocation (USD 54.5 million) for gender equality. Funding has catalyzed a focus on creating more women-owned businesses, starting with helping the Ethiopia Electric Utility (EEU) achieve a better balance between men and women among leadership and its workforce, including a goal of women holding 30% of all jobs over the next four years. The initiative was recognized for its contribution by being awarded the President’s Award for Excellence in June 2018.

### 3.2 Guinea-Bissau Gender Gap

Guinea-Bissau has one of the worst human development outcomes in the world. Poverty is widespread, particularly affecting women who are an average less educated, lack access to land and assets and are under-represented in decent employment and political institutions. Patriarchal values are entrenched in the customs of the country, which include female genital mutilation (FGM), early marriage, Mancujo and Projet UNEP[2011]. Due to a lack of internationally comparable data, Guinea-Bissau does not appear on the World Economic Forum’s 2020 Global Gender Gap Report or UNDP’s 2020 Gender Inequality Index.

#### 1. Gender gaps in educational outcomes

Girl's enrollment and completion rates start to lag behind boys in lower secondary school and increase with the educational level. Across all levels of education over the past decades, Guinea-Bissau's educational outcomes are among the lowest in the region and remain low, particularly affecting girls. According to Guinea Bissau’s MICS & report (2020), more than 40 percent of women age 15-49 did not receive any education, compared to 18 percent of men. While primary school completion rates are similar for boys and girls at 37% and 36%, respectively, the gap continues to be low: only 29.2 percent of boys and 25.1 percent of girls complete primary school. Gender gaps deepen in lower secondary school : only 17.8 percent of boys and 16.4 percent of girls complete lower secondary school, while 13.7 percent of boys complete secondary school compared to only 8.1 percent for girls. Access to education is at the same time a gendered issue: 50% of women in urban areas are literate as opposed to just 32.6 percent, only 16.3 of rural female are literate compared to 56 percent of urban women. In comparison, 78.9 percent of men living in urban areas are literate, compared to 32.4 percent of men living in rural areas.


---
**Page 206**
---

Commonly cited reasons for girls lower educational outcomes include early marriage and pregnancy, distance from school, lack of access to sanitary facilities, lack of money and household responsibilities (UNICEF, 2019). Early marriage is a common practice in Guinea-Bissau occurring in most of the country's ethnic groups. While nearly 16 percent of girls age 15-19 are currently married or in union, this is only the case for 0.3 percent of boys the same age. More than 8 percent of girls were married before their 15th birthday (MICS, 5, 2020). Early marriage often results from poverty and lack of education. In addition to early marriage, 47 percent of women aged 20-24 years old had their first live birth before the age of 18 compared to only 3.4 percent of men. In addition, lack of adequate sanitary facilities often constitute a barrier to girls' education, preventing many from attending schools during menstruation or contraception. More than 8 percent of girls miss social activities, school work due to menstrual periods. Furthermore, the vast majority of the teaching workforce is male, with only a few percent of female teachers in secondary schools.

### 2. Gender gaps in health outcomes

Bissau-Guinean women are particularly affected by low quality of healthcare and one of the world's worst reproductive health indicators. In Guinea-Bissau's maternal mortality ratios are among the highest worldwide, resulting 667 deaths per 100,000 live births. Results from the 2020 MICS 5 survey indicate that antenatal care (ANC) coverage is widespread, suggesting that the quality of care is not sufficient to prevent many maternal deaths. Overall, only about half of women in Bissau-Guinean give birth at a health facility, while nearly 97 percent of women had a birth attended by a skilled personnel. In Guinea-Bissau, nearly half of births (48.8 percent) occur at home. The current COVID-19 Crisis may have worsened women's health outcomes, further reducing their access to health services due to restrictions in movement and lack of financial resources.

Fertility rates remain high due to lack of knowledge about reproductive health, limited access to services, and low contraceptive use. In 2018, the country's fertility rate reached 4.5 births per woman. Only 21.3 percent of women aged 15-49 years have a partner with a satisfied need for family planning. Furthermore, women are disproportionately affected by the prevalent use of traditional sources of energy. Biomass cooking has been associated with health risks due to indoor pollution.

### 3. Female employment and access to decent work

Despite contributing significantly to the country's economy, women are disproportionately represented in vulnerable employment and perform the large part of unremunerated and household work. Women's access to economic opportunities is limited due to lack of time, lower education and gender norms confining women to their homes. Women and girls are also largely responsible for domestic chores such as cooking, care and spend several unpaid hours per day collecting water and firewood. When they enter the labor market, women are more often in informal employment and less likely to be paid. Guinea-Bissau's economy is mostly agrarian and characterized by high dependency on the cashew industry where women are relegated at the bottom of the value chain. Women working in the cashew sector are indeed mostly responsible for farming and harvesting the cashew nuts on lands that belong to their male relatives, while men decide if and when to sell (MICS, 2015).


---
**Page 207**
---

# Women’s access to ownership and finance

Bissau-Guinean women have limited access to land and other productive assets than men, making single, widowed, and divorced women particularly vulnerable to poverty. While Guinea-Bissau's Land Law protects women's right to inherit, land allocation is mostly governed by customary norms which are often discriminatory towards women. According to the tradition of the Balanta, Fula, Mandinga, Manjaco and Pepet ethnic groups, women have no rights over land. In the case of the "Balanta" and "Fula", women are traditionally responsible for land management and decide how much access - if any - is granted to female members of their household. As a result, women own less than 2.5 percent of land, which is lower than the regional average (UNDP, 2013). Women also have limited access to credit, material and equipment, preventing them from accessing income-generating opportunities. According to the World Bank's 2006 Enterprise Survey, only 19.9 percent of businesses stated that they have female representation in their ownership compared to 33 percent on average in Sub-Saharan Africa.

## Women’s political participation

Despite the recent amendment on Party, women's participation in politics remain extremely low. In May 2018, the National Assembly of Guinea-Bissau adopted a law requiring that any list of candidates for national and local elections, as well as for elective offices in public institutions should include fifty percent of women. However, only 14 female candidates won the 2019 legislative election, representing 3.7 percent of Parliament seats. This result can be explained by the lack of political parties that have a specific policy on gender equality and women's rights law and the election, as well as reluctance from political parties to allow female candidates to head lists (UNDP, 2021). The 2019 legislative elections were marked by a record number of female voters and female candidates, mostly in secondary position.

Patriarchal norms associating decision-making with masculinity and the disproportionate burden of unpaid and domestic work prevent many women from participating in political life. Women are indeed traditionally excluded from community-based organizations (CBOs) such as the "Bakari" or "Talabane" associations, traditional societies ("Rigados") or religious leaders (UNDP, 2021). In addition, results from the MICS 6 2020 survey indicate that women have lower access to means of communication, resulting in limited access to information and opportunities. While 87 percent of urban men own a mobile phone compared to only 49.3 percent of rural men, this difference can also be observed: 80.7 percent of urban women own a cellphone compared to only 46.9 percent of rural women. Furthermore, only 29 percent of urban women and 2.5 percent of rural women use internet. In comparison, 68.2 percent of urban men and 18.6 percent of rural men have access to internet.


---
**Page 208**
---

### Gender-Based Violence (GBV)

Gender-based violence is a widespread phenomenon in Guinea-Bissau and takes many forms from female genital mutilation to rape. Although national statistics are lacking to adequately assess the prevalence of gender-based violence in the country, results from the 2020 MICS 6 Survey indicate high levels of tolerance toward domestic violence. More than 36 percent of women aged 15–49 years believe that a husband is justified in beating his wife for not cooking well enough or for not keeping the house clean.

Female Genital Mutilation (FGM) is practiced all over the country with wide geographical and ethnic disparities. More than 40,000 girls and women in Guinea-Bissau have experienced FGM (UNICEF, 2020). Girls from Muslim communities in rural areas with less education are at great risk of FGM. On the other hand, female circumcision is practically non-existent in some ethnic groups such as the Balanta, Fulape, Manjaco and Papel. In the region of Gabu, FGM is practiced on 90 percent of girls aged between 15–49 years old. In the region of Bafatá, 82 percent of girls are excised in the Biombo region (MICS-6, 2020). FGM is not only seen as a religious obligation by many, but also often associated with purity and perceived as a way to prepare girls for marriage and increase their fertility (Balde, 2018). Overall, 76 percent of the population seem to believe the practice should end, even in areas where FGM is widespread. Overall, 76 percent of the population oppose female circumcision, compared to 59 percent of the population in 2006 (UNICEF, 2020).

### Indonesia Gender Gap

Despite significant reduction of gender inequality in the past decade and improving health and educational outcomes, significant gaps remain with respect to women’s economic and political participation. Furthermore, the state at risk of regression during the COVID-19 pandemic disproportionately impacts women who are mostly responsible for household and family responsibilities. Indicators’ achievements on gender equality vary greatly according to provinces. Gender gaps are indeed exacerbated by unequal access to infrastructure and public services. Rural women are particularly exposed to poverty and unemployment and have less access to health services and education than their urban counterpart.

1. **Gender gaps in educational outcomes**

Indonesia has nearly closed the gender gap in education and women are over-represented at all levels. Between 1995 and 2014, women's enrolment in secondary education doubled to reach 75 percent. In 2017, 18 percent of 25-34 year-old women completed a complete senior high school degree compared with just 6 percent for men. However, early marriage and poverty continue to limit many Indonesian girls' access to education. Girls who marry before the age of 18 are indeed six times less likely to complete senior secondary school. Educational outcomes vary according to province, with the highest female completion rates in East Java (75%) and West Sumatra (69%). Further, particularly in the Central Kalimantan, South Kalimantan and West Sulawesi provinces where more than a fifth of women aged 20-24 were married before the age of 18. Nationwide, women represent at least two-third of the country's university students.

Contributing factors include poverty leading households to prioritize the education of boys as future breadwinners, as well as religious and social norms. Women continue to be underrepresented in science, technology, engineering, and mathematics (STEM) subjects in technical education and universities. Female STEM attainment is also lower than male STEM attainment at all levels of education.

2. **Gender gaps in health outcomes**

Despite rapid gains in health outcomes over the past years, Indonesia continues to face high maternal mortality rates and low immunization coverage for children under five. The country's infant mortality rate fell quickly in the past decades but remains high for a middle-income country: between 2000 and 2017, the mortality rate went down from 301 to 177 deaths per 100,000 live births. Early pregnancy remains a major health issue in the country. According to the 2017 Demographic and Health Survey, girls in rural areas are two times more likely to have been childbearing than their urban peers. Early pregnancy is associated with lower education levels, lack of access to care, and poor quality of maternal mortality services. The risk of malnutrition, especially during pregnancy and lactation, as male members of the household are often given larger share-of-food. Despite being key actors of food production in Indonesia, women have limited control over resources and decision-making during periods of food insecurity (FAO, 2015). Rural women also have lower access to health services due to lack of transportation and poor health infrastructure and tend to have less access to drinking water and sanitation.

Among rural households that use firewood for cooking, women are more exposed to indoor air pollution as they are responsible for household chores and firewood collection. Rural women dedicate 25 percent of their time to firewood collection and represent the majority of the 165,000 premature deaths caused by indoor pollution (UNDP 2017).

### 3. Gender-Based Violence

Gender-based violence is widespread in Indonesia and takes various forms from intimate partner violence to female genital mutilation (FGM) and human trafficking. In 2019, the Indonesia's National Commission on Violence against Women (Komnas Perempuan) reported 406,178 GBV cases. This figure only represents a minority of actual cases due to underreporting and fear of retaliation or retribution as well as fear of reprisal. This phenomenon is reinforced by social norms that consider violence against women a private issue.

According to the 2017 Demographic and Health survey, 32 percent of women and 17 percent of married men believe that a husband is justified in beating his wife.

More than one in four women aged 15-64 who have ever been married or in a union has experienced physical, sexual and/or psychological intimate-partner violence. Key drivers of gender-based violence include women's lack of education and employment and lower access to information and resources.
